[scan 8]

THE HISTORY OF HEGEL’S YOUTH.

BY

WILHELM DILTHEY.

FROM THE TREATISES OF THE ROYAL PRUSSIAN ACADEMY OF SCIENCES / FOR THE YEAR 1905.

BERLIN 1905.

PUBLISHED BY THE ROYAL ACADEMY OF SCIENCES.

SOLD ON COMMISSION BY GEORG REIMER.

[scan 10]

Various labors, first the history of the human sciences and the life of Schleiermacher, have led me again and again to Hegel, and in occupying myself with him it has become clear to me that, even after the works of Rosenkranz and Haym, a historical understanding of him cannot be gained without a fresh investigation of Hegel’s manuscripts. Thus there arose for me an account of Hegel’s developmental history, from which I here present first the history of the period that extends from Hegel’s earliest notes to the beginning of the first expositions of his system that have come down to us and to his first publications in the philosophical journal. The fragments from this period have, like Kant’s youthful works, not only a significance for Hegel’s system: since they arose, still unconstrained by the compulsion of the dialectical method, from immersion in the greatest material of history, they possess a value of their own; moreover, they are an inestimable contribution to a phenomenology of metaphysics. It therefore seemed right to me to treat this time in a separate account, and in greater detail than would be appropriate for Hegel’s further development, for philosophers, historians and theologians. I have confined myself to separating from one another clearly distinguishable stages in Hegel’s intellectual history, and to bringing to knowledge, within each of them, the connection of his ideas. These stages themselves are marked off from one another according to the relative dating of the manuscripts; they cannot be fixed in periods that would be determined by years or by places of residence. Though I had to combine the outer history, which runs along the thread of his places of residence, with the inner development, in order to make visible the connection between the conditions of Hegel’s life and his ideas, it has at the same time been indicated how the two coincide only in part. So far as manuscripts could be recognized with certainty as belonging to the

[p. 4]

period treated, I have used all of them; and if the reader comes upon something of some consequence in Rosenkranz or Haym that I have not made use of, the reason for this is sometimes that it cannot belong to the stages of Hegel’s development I have presented, but sometimes also merely that I lack the means of placing reliably such pieces as no longer exist in manuscript. For my work was directed above all to this: to establish a secure foundation on which others may now build further. I have therefore also refrained for the present from attempting more exact relative datings within the stages. These manuscripts will have to occupy research for a long time yet, before their whole yield for Hegel has been won from them.

How the ideas of the period treated determined the advance to the definitive system I shall be able to show only in the account of Hegel’s further development: here I had to content myself with drawing a few lines that lead across to the later stages.

From the beginning of my occupation with these manuscripts several years ago until the conclusion now reached, the collaboration of my young friend Dr. Herman Nohl has accompanied it, and I owe it to his acuteness and his unflagging effort that it has gradually proved possible, by means of the system of signs Hegel employed, to join the leaves scattered through several volumes of the manuscripts into the larger wholes for which Hegel intended them. Were he to give us an edition of the manuscripts of this period, that would be the most valuable complement to my work.

[scan 12]

## Part One.

### First Development and Theological Studies.

#### The School Years.

Hegel was born on 27 August 1770 in Stuttgart, where his father was a ducal official. The household in which he grew up was simple, earnest, filled with an old-Protestant spirit. And however much the ideals of Weimar and Jena later altered his views of life, for his personal conduct of life the old forms of upright custom that had enclosed his childhood always remained decisive; however deeply his thought went into the problematic of the ethical world, he kept his own life untouched by doubt about the Protestant custom and rule of life of his father’s house. The Swabian character of his stock stood out more strongly in his nature and his appearance than in his two other great Württemberg contemporaries, Schiller and Schelling. There was nothing in him of the élan that comes from the proud consciousness of a significant individuality. His naïve, objective mind was averse to the aristocratic feeling of distinctiveness such as the North German natures—Jacobi, Humboldt, Schleiermacher—displayed. Cool and estranged, as a mere spectator, he stood before the romantic tragedies of fate that his companions lived through: Hölderlin in Frankfurt, Schelling in Jena, Creuzer in Heidelberg. Slowly, in quiet work, he developed. And all his life there clung to him the awkwardness that stems from the surrender of the person to the subject matter. The unconscious, inconspicuous, unpretentious quality of his nature won him

[p. 6]

friends everywhere, who loved him without finding the distance of genius from their own nature disagreeable. From his strong sense of reality sprang the gift of settling with contentment into the narrow circumstances that surrounded him, without pushing ambition. The soundest understanding governed his conduct of life, and friendly humor beautified for him the narrowness of life. So happily had Swabian ways, family and temperament endowed him.

He passed through the Gymnasium in Stuttgart as a model pupil, but from the beginning he also looked about him in life with philosophical curiosity. Everything in him and around him was regulated. A diary of his from this time has survived, in which he recorded quite pedantically his studies, his recreations, the events around him. In the schoolboy’s procedure in his private studies the developed technique of the scholar already showed itself; excerpts were labeled and ordered on separate sheets in such a way that they stood ready for every use; even then his habit was forming of carefully copying out writings he read, so as to master them entirely with objective fidelity. This practice, kept up into his late years, developed his great gift for mastering masses of material and for bringing the essence of historical phenomena to the most pregnant expression.

His school years coincided with the development of our poetry from Emilia Galotti to Iphigenie and Don Carlos. Yet there are traces only of his acquaintance with the Messias, with Fiesko, and above all with Nathan. The literature in which he was at home is the writings of the Enlightenment, from Garve and Sulzer down to Nicolai and the journals of the time. The confidant of his mind was Lessing. In the taste of Enlightenment psychology he noted down experiences of every kind, and his logical interest expressed itself in a collection of definitions. From the beginning objective thinking ruled in him. In this time of the swelling power of the life of feeling and of the poetry of feeling, his interest was turned only to things themselves, with the unselfconscious force of an entirely original orientation toward reality, without any reflection upon himself. And what he sought above all to take into himself was antiquity and history.

Greek literature had won an important place in the Gymnasium through Gesner and Heyne, and the understanding of it had just been

[p. 7]

opened up by Winckelmann, Lessing and Herder. Hegel turned above all to the tragedians; he translated Sophocles, and a particular fondness for the Antigone accompanied him through life from these school days on. But in Stuttgart, as in most other Gymnasien of the time, the understanding of the Greeks was still mixed with a strong admixture of the spirit of the Enlightenment, and a kindred trait in Hegel met it halfway. The sacrifice to Aesculapius that the dying Socrates orders in Plato, that profoundly ironic play with the image of deliverance from the long illness of life, is understood by the enlightened schoolboy as an accommodation of the sage to the religious notions of the rabble, and the mythology of the Greeks becomes explicable to him by the fact that in those first times they were still “people without enlightenment.” This precocious pragmatism spreads over all the writings of his last years at the Gymnasium.

Among the problems of the Enlightenment he was gripped especially by that of a philosophical understanding of history, and even then he made his own the method of the universal and philosophical historiography of the eighteenth century. In an essay on the religion of the Greeks and Romans he starts from a general theory of the origin of religions, and indeed, in true Enlightenment fashion, he derives the oldest form of religions from ignorance of the laws of nature, from the despotic condition of society and from the priests’ need for power. The advance to enlightenment then takes place through men of cleared reason who communicate better notions. Observations on some differences between the classical poets and the modern ones extol the superiority of the Greeks; in weighing the reasons for this, he already takes into view here chiefly the relation of art to the total life of the nation. The public of the Greek epic poet was the whole people; it had a common feeling for the subject of his art; “our great epic poet” (Klopstock), despite “the wise choice of his subject,” could arouse interest only in the more educated part of our people, the part at the same time still attached to Christian ideas.

Thus from the beginning Hegel grasps religion and art in connection with the life of nations. This was entirely in the spirit of the Enlightenment; but he held fast to it even in the times when our great poets detached the higher life from its national foundation.

[p. 8]

And to this insight corresponds the remarkable maturity of his political thinking, as it shows itself in a dialogue of the Roman triumvirs composed by him, which at the same time proves the influence of his reading of Shakespeare.

In the same way the pedagogical interest of the Enlightenment makes itself felt, its endeavor to ground the theory of education on the study of man and of his development. His whole thinking is dominated by the pragmatic, political and practical spirit of the Enlightenment; it probably met a basic trait in Hegel’s nature halfway: for in this he always remained true to himself.

The philosophical element in the boy’s disposition expresses itself at first in the universal urge to knowledge, as it speaks from his excerpts and his diary; his interest, like that of the young Leibniz, embraces all branches of human knowledge, and it becomes methodical and consistent where he seeks to comprehend antiquity, history, the intellectual connection in it. One can see with one’s own eyes how, out of the historical work of the Enlightenment, the direction toward the inwardization of history arose. “For a long time I have been wondering what pragmatic history is. Today I have received an idea of it, though a rather obscure and one-sided one.” It goes back behind the bare facts to the characters of famous men, customs, usages, religion, the whole character of nations. It investigates the influence of events upon the constitution and distinctive nature of states, the causes of their rise and of their decline. This is the history of Voltaire and Montesquieu. And its practical aim, the bringing about of a culture of the nations founded on enlightenment, is also the original passion of this political mind. From the history of religions we are to learn to subject to examination all inherited and propagated opinions, even those about which a doubt never seemed possible to us. In this so matter-of-fact spirit there glows at the same time the longing for the liberation of man from the whole pressure of the received forms of belief and conditions of life.

[p. 9]

#### University Years.

1.

In the autumn of 1788 his university study began in Tübingen, and it lasted five years, until the autumn of 1793.

Now the old Augustinian monastery at the foot of the Burgberg, the “Stift,” out of which a band of free and bold minds has come—besides Hegel himself, Hölderlin, Schelling, then Baur, Strauß, Vischer, Zeller, Schwegler—enclosed him. Here there prevails a unique union of external rule and inner freedom. The monastic quiet in the magnificent Neckar valley, the old regulations of the institution, the cultivation of the general studies, in particular of philosophy: all this furthers the development of an independent scientific spirit in the pupils. So the Stift worked upon Hegel too; here the basic trait of his nature was heightened: the relocation of his whole inwardness into the great realities of science, church and state. The reverse side was that the monastic Stift education beat down whatever there may have been in this heavy nature of the gift of presenting itself outwardly, and of the inclination to unfold itself personally.

An inner life that follows the world with eager curiosity from the study and yet must lack mastery over the outer forms of life, a strong, truly Swabian need of independence to the point of obstinacy, which yet lay under the pressure of pedantic regulations and of a backwardness of state and church—in such strangely mixed conditions of life these years at the Stift ran their course. Quietly youth asserted its rights. In the album of his dearest friend Fink he wrote verses of friendship and of fiery kisses, and on the back of the leaf later: “Beautifully the last summer closed, more beautifully the present one! The motto of that was—Wine, of this: Love! 7 Oct. 91.” But on an album leaf in the hand of his friend Fallot one saw him with bowed head, on crutches, and with it the words: “God stand by the old man!” For his comrades called him “the old man.” He belonged to those people who were never young and in whom even in old age a hidden fire still glows.

[p. 10]

2.

And how different now, compared with his Stuttgart years of apprenticeship, was the intellectual atmosphere in which Hegel, as a theological student, saw himself placed in the Stift! The pupil of the Greeks found himself enclosed by theological concepts. The cultivation of the understanding characteristic of the age did, it is true, penetrate into the Stift as well and take possession of its philosophers and theologians; but in this old seat of Lutheran strict orthodoxy they sought to find a compromise between it and the rights of the understanding. This compromise was supranaturalism, as it was represented in Halle by Baumgarten and Tieftrunk, in Tübingen by Storr and his school. Its basis was the conviction of the personality of the Godhead, of freedom, of the worth and imperishability of the human soul, as it underlies biblical Christianity. But this was now the new situation for these supranaturalists: they had to acknowledge that the working of the Godhead is bound to eternal, fixed laws of the order of nature. How could they now establish that in one corner of this universe interventions in its laws became necessary? How did there come into the God whose unchangeable, infinite essence manifests itself in the laws of nature the dark unrest of the will to punish and then the transformation into reconciliation? Natural science, advanced moral consciousness, criticism of the sources: everything stood opposed to this. What in the biblical writings was the natural expression of a worldview that set no limits to the divine power and filled the earth with supernatural forces, these prophecies, revelations, miracles, punishments and reconciliations: this was now to be defended by scientific concepts, in the midst of this enlightened age. What in the context of the dogma was dispensable was sacrificed in order to save the indispensable, and the primeval faith of humanity was converted into a system of thin concepts. Hence already to the honest youth of those days Nösselt, Knapp, Storr and Tieftrunk appeared so antiquated, threadbare and worn out.

One of the most acute among these supranaturalists was Storr, the recognized leader of the theological faculty. Hegel presumably went through the theological course with him from 1790 to 1793. Storr set out from the veracity of Jesus and the credibility of his disciples

[p. 11]

So there resulted for him the acknowledgment of the tradition, contained in the New Testament, of Christ’s life, teaching and work, and he then interpreted it in the sense of the Lutheran dogma and presented it as a sharply stamped conceptual nexus. Even in our own days Ritschl has seen in his procedure “a valuable specimen of biblical-theological method”; in reality it was the craftsman-like dissection of the greatest mystery of the heart that humanity possesses. Lutheran dogmatics rests on the Pauline connection of the Old Testament with the Gospel of Christ by means of the concepts of punitive justice, sacrifice and reconciliation. When now Storr laid out the remission of punishment and the justification through Christ’s voluntary suffering of death and through his total obedience in hard juridical concepts, taken from the sphere of subjection, of crime and of criminal procedure, the mystery of Christianity, received in the heart, was debased and annihilated and at the same time reason was degraded by its misuse. And this procedure was not improved by Storr’s also taking Kant’s critical standpoint into the service of his apologetics. This happened in a writing that followed hard upon the heels of Kant’s philosophy of religion. Tieftrunk in Halle, too, misused the critical philosophy in this way for his doctrine of justification, in repulsive artifices.

The young men felt with passionate aversion the pressure that this supranaturalism exerted on the studies in Tübingen. Over against all these sophists they asserted the true consequence of Kant, the moral sovereignty of the person. In this, however, Hegel differed from his companions, that he now concentrated the whole force of his thinking for a series of years on Christian religiosity. It was to be the course of this objective spirit that from his school years onward he re-experienced and dissected Greek antiquity, and now from his Tübingen years of apprenticeship onward Christianity, as the two greatest historical forces of the past: this became for him the entrance into his historical view of the world. And for this the years in Storr’s school became of great value to him. He took wholly into himself the subtle concepts by which the Pauline-Lutheran doctrine expounded by Storr had forced together the Jewish conceptual order of punitive justice with the Christian one of reconciliation. Only so could the whole depth of consciousness of Jewish and of Christian religiosity later dawn upon him.

[p. 12]

In long, arduous labor he was to grasp the living element that was contained in these concepts of a transcendent process in God. The law and the punitive justice posited by it is a religious conception of life of its own, and of a lower kind. For punishment can neither expiate the crime before the law nor reform; it evokes only the feeling of impotence against a master. The forgiveness of sins belongs to a region that lies beyond Jewish morality: it is “fate reconciled through love.” This is the profound confrontation with the Tübingen Lutheran orthodoxy at which Hegel arrived in his studies in Bern and Frankfurt. Thus Storr’s introduction to this harsh theological world of concepts became a significant moment of his development toward a new historical consciousness. And indeed Hegel already took the first step in this direction in Tübingen. He rejected the transcendent reality of this drama of punitive justice and reconciliation.

3.

New ideas penetrated into this solitary theological work. They did not originate in the lecture halls of the university: the great events out in the world came irresistibly into the monastic quiet of the Stift as well, and they awakened in the most gifted pupils a movement that bound them intimately together and called forth a common enthusiasm for the breaking new day of the spirit.

Three young men of very different dispositions but of equal genius were at that time together in the old Augustinian monastery. Hölderlin had entered the Stift at the same time as Hegel. The first friends of the young poet in the Stift were his fellow poets; until the autumn of 1790 we know of no intercourse of Hegel with him. At that time they became roommates, and gradually a fellowship formed which was founded on the same love of the Greeks and the same philosophical faith. A favorable fate thus brought the new poetry personally close to Hegel in one of its noblest manifestations. The first dream of this pure, great soul of love and happiness had come to an end, the urge to develop his powers freely had won, and in this epoch of decision, in which he struggled with full energy for

[p. 13]

a sound education of his mind, the philosophical studies began to gain influence over him. Together with other friends, Hölderlin and Hegel read Plato, Kant, and Jacobi’s letters on Spinoza, whose second edition had appeared in 1789. This book contained Lessing’s profession of the “One and All,” the ancient Greek formula of the presence of the Godhead in the universe; Hölderlin wrote it in Hegel’s album in February 1791.

In the autumn of 1790 Schelling then also entered the Stift. He was not yet sixteen years old. His genius lay in a power of combination of extraordinary strength. With ease he embraced great masses of knowledge and discovered in them a point of union that had a surprising and illuminating effect. Careful dissection, firm determination of concepts, generalizations of lasting value were not his gift, but he swept one along by the power of brilliant intuition. From early on he lived in the proud consciousness of his strength. At first the precocious genius of the theological student threw himself, under the guidance of the Semitist Schnurrer, into the oriental studies. So it was not his studies at first, but the common interest in the Revolution that brought him into relation with the two friends. As he then turned more and more to philosophy, there arose between him and Hegel that friendship of youth in which a part of the fate of both lay. Hölderlin did not gain with Schelling, the proud, the sure of victory, so intimate and simple a relation as with Hegel.

Precisely into these years from 1788 to 1793 fell the two world-historical events that brought the age of the Enlightenment to fulfillment and at the same time opened the gates of a new age. In Kant the upheaval of German thinking was accomplished, and the Revolution destroyed the old state in France and undertook the erection of a new order of society. With enthusiasm the young men seized upon these two mightiest manifestations of the departing great century. They left their teachers behind them in the worn-out, cheerless mixture of their rationally moderated faith and in their political subjection to the arbitrary rule of the Duke.

In Kant what gripped them above all was the sovereign position of reason over against sensible appearance, authority and tradition. His doctrine of the capacity of reason to give itself the law, this thought of Kant’s that concluded the Enlightenment,

[p. 14]

was what freed them from the dogma around them, as the letters show which the young men exchanged with one another after Hegel’s departure from Tübingen. Schelling writes at that time, with reference to the Tübingen attempts to prepare from Kant “strong broths for theology grown consumptive”: “We both want to go further—we both want to prevent the great thing that our age has brought forth from joining itself again with the stale leaven of past times—it shall remain among us pure, as it came from the spirit of its originator.” And Hegel in his answer goes into the church-political ground of this corruption of the Kantian philosophy at the university: “Orthodoxy cannot be shaken as long as its profession, bound up with worldly advantages, is interwoven into the whole of the state.” How deeply Hegel’s understanding of the philosophical connection in Kant’s system went at that time cannot be determined. Only in Bern did he take up its study anew, and the earlier mixture of its thoughts with those of the Enlightenment was now for the first time given up.

The Revolution was the other great event that gripped the Tübingen friends. The political innocence of the Germans made them enthusiastic spectators of this shattering spectacle. The students founded a political club to which Schelling, Hegel and Hölderlin also belonged. When the Duke heard of the revolutionary speeches, songs of freedom and the singing of the Marseillaise among his theologians in Tübingen, he appeared suddenly in the dining hall of the Stift and delivered a reprimand to them. At that time Schelling is said to have answered, when asked whether he was sorry for the affair: “Your Serene Highness, we all err in many ways.” In his hymns to freedom Hölderlin sang of the great day of harvest, when the league of heroes would have won the victory for their companions, the tyrants’ thrones would be desolate and the tyrants’ slaves mold. A new hour of creation had come, he said; the new century would be that of freedom. And Hegel himself is named by the companions of those days as one of the most zealous speakers for freedom and equality. The sovereign, progressing reason, as it was the soul of the Kantian philosophy, seemed to him to be at work in the Revolution, at last to realize its dominion. What a lived experience lay in it for the head that was destined to comprehend history as the development of the human race toward freedom!

[p. 15]

The expectations of the future received their ideal content for Hegel and his friends from Greek life, as the veneration of the age understood it. The recasting of it into the moral by Socratic rhetoric no longer had any effect. The essence of the Greek world now appeared as “quiet grandeur,” as “universal humanity.” Schiller’s “Gods of Greece” exerted an indescribable effect. Plato became for Hölderlin and Hegel the interpreter of the Greek past. The longing for it consumed Hölderlin’s soul. Into his intuition of it Christian inwardness mingled, as in Goethe’s Iphigenie. Longer than Hegel and Schelling he had been influenced by the Tübingen theology; he once believed with Jacobi that thinking leads inevitably to the atheism of Spinoza: only in the historically attested miraculous life of Christ did he see a refuge. From the influence of this apologetic theology Kant, Schiller and association with his philosophical friends freed him. Since then he held only to the purely human worth of Christianity, in which it appeared to him akin to Greek antiquity. In both, after all, he revered his personal ideal: beauty of his own existence and elevation of the human race, quiet gathered within itself and the longing for a life richer in danger and heroic deeds, for an art that transfigures the world in beauty and hallows passion, and for a free commonwealth for which one can live and die. It was a very general illusion at that time when he believed that what he thus hoped for from the future had already once been realized in Hellas. The same view of the Greek essence formed itself in his philosophical friend, only that Hegel, more realistic than Hölderlin, held fast therein to the conception of the Enlightenment, in that he saw in the political freedom of the Greek states the foundation of the higher humanity unfolded there.

4.

So the friends lived in the consciousness of the progress of the human race, and the whole heightening that it had undergone through the completion of philosophy in criticism and through the French Revolution now entered into Hegel’s theological work. In Kant’s moral faith of reason the scientific grounding of ethical life and religion had been definitively accomplished for him. His practical spirit put to itself

[p. 16]

the question: How is the further development of the existing Christian religiosity into a folk religion possible, which would become the bearer of a progressing religious-moral culture? A series of fragments, partly preserved in manuscript, partly handed down by Rosenkranz, undertakes to solve this task. Some of them certainly belong to this period, others will be from a later time; since corrections show that Hegel meant to use them as material in a more mature version, they too are to be treated in detail by us only there. Here only the connection of thoughts that formed itself in him at that time in Tübingen shall be indicated. In a folk religion that connects the religious convictions of the people, for the purpose of the ennobling of its spirit, with all the living motive forces of action, he saw the mediation between church faith and the religion of reason; the rigid opposition between these two, as the Enlightenment had set it up, dissolved for him in this historical-political concept. On the path that humanity traverses from fetish faith to the moral religion of reason, this folk religion is the last stage preparing the faith of reason, since the masses can raise themselves only slowly, and perhaps never, to the sublime moral principles of Kant. The historical thinking of the young philosopher, directed toward continuity, expresses itself in this view.

The center of such a folk religion is for him love. For if folk religion must bring into play the good-natured inclinations of man, his moral feeling, as ethical forces everywhere and universally effective: then these have their center in love, as the basic principle of the empirical character. Profoundly Hegel brings out how it has something analogous to reason and thereby constitutes the transition to the moral principle of reason. As reason, as the principle of universally valid laws, “recognizes itself again in every rational being,” so love lives in others, is active in them, finds itself in them. Similarly Schiller had already made love the center of the moral world in his Rhapsody, and Hölderlin had followed him in this. And in love Lessing had recognized the principle of the religion of Christ.

If now love is to produce an ethical life that fills the people, then education, example and governmental institutions must cooperate to that end: it must thus become the soul of the state. To this concept of religion, as the soul of the state, Hegel from then on always

[p. 17]

held fast. It is to be the power for the realization of ethical life in the political whole, so that in a modern way the political ethical life of the Greeks may be restored. Thus in this folk religion the imagination must be filled with great pure images and thereby satisfied, and in the heart sublime feelings must displace those affects of false humility through which church morality has a corrupting effect. Folk religion must “breathe into the soul the enthusiasm that is indispensable to great and sublime virtue.”

In this connection there arose for him already at that time the view of the historical whole that leads from the religion of Christ, through the corruption of the church, in which Protestant faith is for him contained just as much as Catholic, to the approaching new Gospel. The sublime ideal that Christ set up was well capable of determining the formation of individual men, but to realization in a community it could not attain; in its place there sprang up false humility, fetish faith, which strives to satisfy God by external service, the terror-devices of the church, its coercive measures inward and outward. Only when the private religion of Christ is remade into a folk religion can it become the bearer of a healthy ethical life.

This period in Hegel’s development extends still into his later stay in Bern, into the early part of 1795, in which, from the standpoint of Fichte and of the first two writings of Schelling, he sank himself anew into Kant. It is natural that the epochs of the inner development of the philosopher do not coincide with the successive changes of his residence. The reader must bear this in mind in the progress of this narrative. When it passes again and again from the inner to the outer development, the periods of the one nevertheless do not wholly coincide with those of the other.

#### The Years as Private Tutor in Bern.

Years as private tutor! That was the misery of the future clergymen and schoolmen of that time. The poet Lenz has described the dangers and sufferings of this higher servant’s position drastically; Hölderlin’s noble soul was destroyed by it. By the young generation of these

[p. 18]

days it was felt all the more oppressively as it grew up under the impressions of the Revolution. Hegel had to serve seven long years as a private tutor, three in Bern and four in Frankfurt, until, after his father’s death, the small inheritance freed him. The pleasant and formative element in such a relationship, as Schleiermacher and Herbart experienced it, was not granted to him.

In the autumn of 1793 he had concluded his period of study and passed the theological candidate’s examination. He now accepted a post in the Steiger family, which belonged to the Bernese aristocracy. Impassively and, as it seems, without any share of heart in the old proud family that made use of his services, he studied in this environment the Hippocratic countenance of the dying Bernese oligarchy. These were the last years before the collapse of the rule of these noble families; they too succumbed to the ideas of the Revolution. His interest extended from the electoral intrigues and the nepotism of this oligarchy to the tax constitution of the canton. At that time he drew up a detailed presentation of the financial relations of Bern and wrote an essay on the changes in the system of warfare in the transition from the monarchical to the republican form of state; and among his reading are Thucydides, the great political minds of the Enlightenment, Montesquieu, Hume and Gibbon, and Schiller’s historical works.

#### Relation to the Philosophical Movement.

His most intensive work advanced in the direction of the theological studies of his Tübingen years. The great theme that would not let go of him was the life and teaching of Jesus and the transition of his religion into positive dogmatic faith. Kant’s writing on religion had in 1793 given to the understanding of Christianity through the Enlightenment its most mature expression. In 1794 Hegel sank himself anew into Kant. With Reinhold and with “the more recent endeavors to penetrate into deeper depths” he was at that time not yet acquainted. His gaze was at first turned exclusively to the application of the new philosophy to the moral world. Only about the treatise of his friend Schelling on “the possibility of a form of philosophy” was he nonetheless curious, and when he then received it, it made, as a letter of 16 April 1795 shows, an extraordinary impression upon him.

[p. 19]

From then on a philosophical revolution began in him. He had just read Schiller’s Letters on Aesthetic Education and admired them as a masterpiece. With Hölderlin he stood in correspondence, and the latter’s enthusiasm for Fichte’s great plans affected him too. Thus he was prepared to take into himself the new turn of philosophy that Fichte accomplished and with which he now became acquainted in the treatise of his friend.

Schelling’s essay on the possibility of a form of philosophy followed directly upon Fichte’s On the Concept of the Doctrine of Science of 1794; it appeared in the same year. Starting from the connection of human knowledge, it demands of science that it stand under the form of unity; then its fundamental principle must be the condition of both its content and its form, yet itself unconditioned in content and form; and from this point the little essay develops Fichte’s doctrine of the I that posits itself, of the not-I, and of the mediation between the two.

A letter of Schelling to Hegel of 4 February 1795 enlightened him about the metaphysical core of the treatise and encouraged him to carry out his plan of determining the limits of the inferences from the moral law to the suprasensible order of the world. He himself, he wrote, had once thought of deriving, to spite Storr and his like, the whole Catholic-medieval dogmatics from this method. And to Hegel’s question whether Schelling believed that the moral proof did not suffice for the grounding of an individual, personal being, Schelling answers by reminding “Lessing’s confidant” of a famous saying of his in Jacobi: “For us too the orthodox concepts of God are no more. We reach further still than to the personal being.” He develops for him the Spinozism which he derives from Fichte’s absolute I, and Hegel finds in his answer to Schelling of 16 April 1795 that all the presentiments he himself already had were cleared up by this rejection of Kant’s whole method of grounding a suprasensible world.

All these stimuli heightened in him the consciousness of the sovereignty of the spirit. “The nimbus around the heads of the oppressors and gods of the earth vanishes.” The philosophy of the absolute I, as the highest completion of the Kantian system, as Fichte and

[p. 20]

Schelling strive to bring it about, will produce a spiritual revolution in Germany. The principles are there; all that matters is to work them out generally and to apply them to the knowledge hitherto. The lowering of man’s own power for the good appeared to Hegel as the common doctrine of religion and politics, which were in league with each other. Rousseau, Schiller and Fichte fill him with the will to work for a new order of society that rests on the consciousness of the dignity, the goodness and the creative power of humanity.

So he found himself only confirmed by the new insights in the direction that he had taken in Tübingen, and therefore also pursued further the works that had arisen there from the urge to shake off the yoke of positive religious faith. The reshaping of his philosophical conception of the world remained in the background: still indeterminate to himself in its outlines and in its reach. The religious liberation of humanity by means of knowledge of Christian religiosity and its history was also still the dominant passion of this Bern period. From Hippel’s Lebensläufe, which he loved, since they express Kant’s mood of life and ideas poetically, he called out to himself: “Strive toward the sun, friends, that the welfare of the human race may soon ripen.”

#### Three Writings on the Christian Religion.

#### I. The Life of Jesus.

In the service of this practical striving, which was directed toward religious enlightenment, the first writing, which he undertook, was now to stand—a Life of Jesus. The draft of it was written down from 9 May to 24 July 1795. It arose, then, just as Kant’s system began to take on a new form in his mind. The ferment in the head of the young student had been conditioned by forces of different directions. The ancients, the literature of the Enlightenment, Lessing, Schiller’s Rhapsody, finally Kant had pressed in upon him together at that time. But as transcendental philosophy now, in its form reformed in 1795 by Fichte and Schelling, worked upon him, the thought of a universally valid system of reason, in morals too, had to rule out all empirical

[p. 21]

determinations, such as love and sympathy. And in setting about to present the religion of Christ as a moral faith of reason, he had to hold fast, in the writings which were to work for it in the circle of the educated, to Kant’s theism, although he himself had already become wavering in it, for he wrote to Schelling at that time that he regarded the idea of God as the absolute I, as the friend had set it out for him, as the exclusive property of an esoteric philosophy. Both of these one must keep in mind if one wants to understand the peculiar position of Hegel’s notes from this time, on the one hand to his Tübingen thoughts and on the other to the content of his letters to Schelling.

1.

The task that Hegel sets himself in his “Life of Jesus” was marked out for him by Kant. In Kant’s work on the philosophy of religion there is a section: “Church faith has pure religious faith as its highest interpreter.” He recommends there a reinterpretation of the biblical writings in the sense of “a pure religion of reason,” appeals in this to the Stoic reinterpretation of belief in the gods and the Philonic one of the Old Testament, and finally justifies this procedure on the ground that the aim of all occupation with these writings lies in “making better men.” “Since the moral improvement of man constitutes the real purpose of all religion of reason, it will also contain the supreme principle of all scriptural interpretation.”

In these sentences of Kant lay the guideline for Hegel’s presentation. The Life of Jesus has a practical purpose and serves the realization of his “folk religion.” The teaching of Christ is reinterpreted into Kant’s moral faith, and the example of Christ is then to impart warmth and strength to this faith of reason.

The narrative begins with the activity of John the Baptist. It then turns to Jesus. More than John, he has rendered service to the improvement of the corrupted maxims of men and to the knowledge of true morality and of the purified veneration of God. His parents were Mary and Joseph; here the narrator silently lifts out the older stratum of our tradition and leaves the later overpaintings out of account. The legends that surround the birth of Jesus are likewise silently dropped. The Jordan baptism

[p. 22]

becomes a testimony to his great gifts. The strangest thing in the whole narrative is the brief report on the story of the temptation, in which voices of Goethe’s Faust poem resound. “In the hour of his reflection, in solitude, the thought once occurred to him whether it would not be worth the trouble, by study of nature and perhaps by connection with higher spirits, to seek to get so far as to transform less noble substances into nobler ones, more immediately usable for man, or to make himself altogether more independent of nature.” So Hegel interprets the solicitations of Satan. Jesus, however, wishes to remain within the limits of the power of man over nature. Another time there passed before the imagination of Jesus what among men is held to be great: to rule over millions, to make the world talk of oneself; but he rejected this too, in order to follow the eternal law of ethical life in his heart purely and with unviolated soul. Jesus’s teaching activity follows. The struggle of church faith, of the ceremonial service of the Pharisees, with the religion of reason of Christ—that is for him now the tragic conflict of this life: a conflict common to humanity, which was not fought out even in Hegel’s times, and to which he too had devoted himself. “If you hold your ecclesiastical statutes and positive commandments to be the highest law that is given to man, you misjudge the dignity of man and the capacity in him to draw from himself the concept of the Godhead and the knowledge of its will.” Into this Kantian formula Hegel converts the discourse of Christ. In the parable of the rich man he has Abraham say: “To man the law, his reason, is given; neither from heaven nor from the grave can any other instruction come to him.” And the Holy Spirit he determines as “the developed ethical life” of the disciples of Christ. In this they are to find their guide after Christ’s departure. The miracle stories are simply eliminated. Thus for Hegel the last collision in Jerusalem and the death of Jesus are the final act of a drama that is played out between statutory church faith and the religion of reason. Comparable to the tragedy of the Antigone with its conflict between the eternal right of nature, which embodies itself in Antigone, and the positive laws. As he also, at the death of Christ, thinks of the dying Socrates in the Phaedo. With the death of Jesus and his burial the presentation closes.

[p. 23]

2.

Thus this Life of Jesus by Hegel is related, in its rationalist standpoint, to the Life of Jesus of the theologian Paulus, which appeared in 1828 as a result of the critical studies that began in these nineties. Only that Paulus combined the interpretation according to moral faith employed here with the learned one: a procedure which Kant, alongside the simple moral interpretation, recommended in another place. Hegel was to traverse in a few years, in solitary development, the path which theology then ran through from the moral interpretation of the rationalist Paulus to the mythical explanation of Strauß.

The opposition of the solitary man by the Sea of Galilee, who hears inner voices and in the pure heart experiences the power of the intuition of God, to the Pharisaic submission to the legislation of God as to an external and historical authority, was without doubt an important side of the constitution of Jesus’s life. The pupil of Kant’s writing on religion has rightly brought out this opposition from the sources, but the other points in which Jesus opposed the Jewish religiosity of the time he has not appreciated in his draft. In the Tübingen student years Hegel had recognized the significance of love for moral development, although even in these notes it is already subordinated to the categorical imperative of duty. The Life of Jesus, however, speaks out far more sharply in the sense of Kant against every pathological motive of morality. “For if you love those who love you, what merit have you in that? This is a feeling of nature, which is not disowned even by the wicked. For duty you have thereby done nothing yet.” How then is this sharper carrying through of the rational character of ethical life to be explained? It was presumably conditioned by the influence which the development of the young Kantian school in Fichte and the first writings of Schelling exerted on Hegel. Here there took place a consistent working out of Kant’s doctrine of the a priori connection in the human mind. Under their influence he now everywhere stresses the self-active capacity of human reason to give itself its law: hence ethical life too was for him, in Kant’s very own sense, supra-empirical, grounded in the universally valid essence of reason; and now he relocates

[p. 24]

this standpoint of his and his deep, bitter personal opposition to statutory religion, its dogmas and its ceremonies into the time of primitive Christianity. He understands it from his own position in life, the great conflict between external authority and autonomy in which he stood. Resolute and dictatorial as he was by nature, he pushed aside everything in the Gospels that proclaims love as the true principle of the inner ethical life of Jesus. Nowhere, therefore, does the full chord of love from Lessing’s Testament of John or from Schiller’s Rhapsody any longer ring out in this Life of Jesus. When Jesus once asks God that love of the good may unite the disciples among themselves with God and with him, when he feels the spirit of love as the power operative in him and in the disciples: Hegel passes by all this in silence. This is the first case in which Hegel applies his device of understanding the past more deeply from what surrounds him as a still present historical life. This procedure will form an important part of his historical method. And in this first case Kant’s profound interpretation of Christianity in his writing on religion is his model. Thus at this point too the development of the new historical methods is connected with the Enlightenment and its greatest son.

#### II. The Essay on the Relation of the Religion of Reason to Positive Religion.

1.

The presentation of the life and teaching of Jesus becomes fully intelligible only through its relation to another manuscript, found in Hegel’s literary remains, “on the relation of the religion of reason to positive religion.” Its greater part was written by 2 November 1795, the rest bears the date 29 April 1796. So close does this essay also stand, in time, to the Life of Jesus. If the latter had shown his religion to be that of practical reason, Hegel now poses the question: What could in the teaching and life of Jesus give occasion for his religion to become a positive faith? A moral religion of reason consists of eternal truths: what moment in it made possible its transformation into a historical, and hence into an authoritative one?

[p. 25]

We find ourselves in the sphere of influence of Lessing and Kant. Lessing distinguished between the eternal and the historical. Natural religion comprises the general determinations about divine things and the most perfect human life; but as a positive right has been built up out of the right of nature, so out of the religion of reason a positive religion had to arise, which received its sanction from the authority of its founder. Lessing’s main proposition is now: Eternal truths cannot be accredited by historical traditions. Precisely this is the view of Kant, only that he grounds faith in divine things on the moral law within us. Pure religious faith recognizes the divine will from the ethical law that speaks in us, but from the imperfection of human nature springs the belief in particular statutes of religious life which are supposed to be accredited by external authority in revelation and miracle. And Kant too decides, like Lessing, that pure religious faith cannot be grounded on the unprovable, historical traditions. Exactly in this great tradition lies Hegel’s fundamental view. “Eternal truths must by their nature, if they are to be necessary and universally valid, be grounded on the essence of reason alone, not on appearances of the outer sensible world that are contingent for reason.”

To this conviction he remained essentially true throughout his life. “True faith has no finite, contingent content.” “The spiritual cannot be accredited externally.” It corresponds to these late utterances that he afterwards too never ascribed to miracles any value for the attestation of the eternal religious truths. “Whether at the wedding at Cana the guests got more or less wine is quite indifferent, and it is just as contingent whether the withered hand of that man was healed: for millions of men go about with withered and crippled limbs whom no one heals.” “Accreditation by miracles is a sphere that does not concern us.” And if in the early writing revelation is for him not a communication from without or above, but the language of reason in the moral law, so he still declares in the Berlin lectures too that God’s utterance in revelation takes place not as something superhuman that would come in the form of an external revelation, but in a man,

[p. 26]

as something human. What Lessing and Kant dissolved has not been set up again by Hegel either.

So the question of the origin of positive religion is transformed for him into the historical problem: where in the religion of Jesus lay the germ of its transformation into a positive, ecclesiastical, authoritarian system?

2.

In an introduction Hegel refuses to preface the answer to this question with a creed, and wishes to have made for it only the presupposition that the purposes and essence of true religion lie in morality and that all other determinations contained in it are to be assessed according to this purpose. He begins with a characterization of the declining Judaism as the foundation for the understanding of the work of Christ, who undertook “to raise religion and virtue to morality.” Once more Hegel sets this life before us in brief strokes. And yet in the person and teaching of Jesus there must now lie the germ for the fact that out of it there arose first a sect and then a positive faith. It is therefore necessary to sink oneself into the original form of the religion of Jesus and into the spirit of the times, and so to find the ground for this.

The germ of positive Christian faith lay in the religion of Jesus himself. The grounding of faith on the ethical consciousness was not consistently carried through by him. “He was a Jew; the principle of his faith and of his Gospel was the revealed will of God, as the traditions of the Jews had handed it down to him, but at the same time the living feeling of his own heart of duty and right. In the observance of this moral law he set the chief condition of God’s good pleasure.” And this germ of corruption in the religion of Jesus was then brought to unfolding by the spirit of the Jewish people; the Jews believed that they had received their laws, political as well as liturgical, from God, and were proud of it; if Jesus wished to work upon them, he too had to appeal to a divine accreditation. So there runs through the whole New Testament the assertion that Jesus’s teaching is the will of God, his Father, and that whoever follows him believes in the Father. Here Hegel leaves open as undecidable whether Jesus himself was conscious of a connection

[p. 27]

with God, or held the moral law in his breast to be the immediate revelation of the Godhead. In any case we find his conception within the rationalistic assumption of the influence of the milieu on Jesus and of his adaptation to it.

The Messiah idea came to meet faith in the person. Jesus found a hearing because he was regarded as the Messiah. He did not directly contradict a supposition which so greatly conditioned his entry among the Jews: he could only try to direct the expectations of his disciples concerning the Messiah more toward the moral, and he placed the time of the appearance of his greatness beyond his life, into the time after his departure. To the Messiah faith there was added the distinctive character of the personality of Jesus as another moment. His life and death engraved themselves deeply into the imagination of his disciples; his history became as important to them as his teaching: he himself, in order to defend himself, had seen himself compelled to speak of his person. And in the same direction, toward the singling out of the person of Jesus, worked the miracles ascribed to him. “However one may take them, so much is conceded by all, that these deeds of Jesus were miracles for his disciples and friends.” “The way from the history of miracles to faith in a person, from this, if all goes well, to ethical life” became thus “the highroad commanded by the symbols.” As though eternal truths could ever be proved by contingent appearances.

A further reason which led from the religion of Jesus to positive faith, authority, church service and ceremonies lay in the inner constitution of the disciples. Limited men, they found indeed in Jesus an enlargement of their horizon but were nevertheless not able to overcome all Jewish ideas and prejudices. They had not themselves won truth and freedom; their conviction rested on attachment to the person of Jesus, and their capacity, like their wish, went only to faithful transmission. The comparison between Socrates and Jesus was constantly present to Hegel, and so he also contrasts the disciples of Jesus with the pupils of Socrates. In the latter, Athens’s freedom, civic life and culture developed the spirit of independence; they loved their teacher for the sake of truth, not truth out of attachment to Socrates. From the opposite behavior of the disciples there arose external tradition and from this then the exceptional position

[p. 28]

of the disciples and their successors. Thus after Jesus’ death there formed a sect with peculiar doctrines and customs, and as it spread it became a state. Two remarks of Hegel’s are noteworthy here. With regard to the number twelve of the disciples as something positive, he observes: “In a religion of virtue numbers have no place.” And he disapproves the traditional obligation of the disciples to faith and to baptism after the Resurrection so strongly that one must doubt whether he can have regarded this important passage as historical.

The advance to positive religion was then furthered by the spread of the community. Community of goods had been possible in a small sect; now there stepped into its place the levy for priests and monasteries; “the equality of the brothers before God became such an equality in heaven and for heaven, and today it is prudently added that it is so only in the eyes of heaven, and in this earthly life no further notice is taken of it.” The Lord’s Supper, a voluntary memorial meal of friends, became a religious duty and a mysterious act. A mania for expansion, proselytizing and intolerance arose, for positive religion cannot, as virtue does, ground its propositions on inner necessity. And since it cannot suppress the feeling of impotence and of constraint, the hatred and envy of the sects spring up. This picture of positive church religion reminds Hegel of the conversation of the lay brother with Nathan, and enthusiastically he exclaims: “Yes, happy you! For purity of heart was for both of you the essential thing in faith.”

The development goes on, however; the community of faith becomes an organized society, a contractual state. Thus arises the absurdity that the individual submits to the general will where the issue is not person and property but faith. And when at last a priestly oligarchy grows out of the free constitution, the last remnant of one’s own willing and opining is extinguished, and with obedience the duty is taken over of holding something to be true because the rulers command it. The church becomes a state and creates for itself a church law. The state itself renounces one right after another, above all that of education. Thus it becomes “a traitor to the right of children to the free cultivation of their faculties.” Reason is killed and the imagination filled with terrifying images. The freedom

[p. 29]

of development in the souls of children is destroyed; “for only in youth is it possible to impress faith so into the marrow of the soul, so to wrap all the branches of human concepts and faculties, of human striving and willing, around with it.” That is why the Patriarch in Nathan is so agitated when he hears of Recha’s irreligious upbringing.

In such a church morality must degenerate; for religion here is not grounded on facts of our spirit that can be developed out of our consciousness. One begins here with historical knowledge, and on it is built a system of propositions and feelings whose criterion is the good pleasure of God. Whoever has not been brought up in this system “finds himself here in an enchanted world”; in these Christians he is unable to recognize any beings of his own kind: sooner will he find nature in the fairy tales of the Orient and in our romances of chivalry, and a physics or psychology grounded on these would stand closer to nature than a morality abstracted from these degenerate Christians. At this point Hegel stresses in particular that this morality, contrary to nature, of asceticism and self-deception is at present being destroyed by the cultivation of the moral sense and the knowledge of the nature of the soul, as the novels of Marivaux, among other things, disseminate it. “The incorruptible power of the I,” which feels its freedom, expresses itself in the sects; but in them too the disposition quickly becomes law and formula again; they grow into churches; new sects arise in these, “and so it must go on, so long as the state does not know the extent of its rights”; for in the positive faith and the system of a church there will always lie the fundamental error that here the right of reason is misjudged and contempt for man is made the foundation of an authoritative faith. And from this there will always arise a church morality that confronts man as an external law.

But it is contrary to the right of reason that man submit to an alien law. “The whole power of the church is illegitimate; the right of giving oneself one’s own law, of being accountable to oneself alone, no man can renounce; for with this alienation he would cease to be man.” Centuries will pass before the European spirit recognizes this. The alienation of man to an alien power sprang from an inflamed, unruly imagination, in barbarous times or in the lower class of the people.

[p. 30]

Here the manuscript breaks off. It was evidently Hegel’s intention to go on to present the transformation of positive religion into the faith of reason.

#### 3. Folk Religion.

There are three problems that Hegel already saw in their connection at Tübingen and that he now, from his new standpoint as well, treated one after another: the life and teaching of Jesus as a presentation of the struggle between moral faith and Jewish legality; the false development of this faith into positive religion, into church power and the service of ceremonies; and now, on the basis of philosophical knowledge, of moral faith, the task of bringing about a folk religion with the means of Christianity. This last and most difficult of his problems of that time is the subject of notes that stand, in part at least, in visible connection with one another; and the marking of the sheets shows that they were meant to attach to the essay on positive religion. And here the works of earlier years were then also to find their place. Hegel did not carry the drafts through to the end, but the fragments show how large was the scale on which he laid out his plan. For, as we shall see, he wanted to prepare his doctrine of folk religion by a presentation of the process in which the imaginative world of genuine folk religion perished.

A gentle but unmistakable change in mood and in language sets these leaves apart from the earlier ones. Hegel stands unshaken on the ground of Kant and of his moral faith. But Kant’s writing on religion was not merely the consummation of the rationalism of Lessing and Semler: its countenance is at the same time turned toward the future. The connection of the dogmas of Christianity is grasped as the, so to speak, visible presentation of the inward process in which our sensible nature wrestles with our ethical capacity. With this, to be sure, the relation of the positive faith to the faith of reason is given, but at the same time also the inner connection that exists between religious life and its mythical projection through the power of imagination.

Slowly Hegel now begins to see this inner connection ever more clearly. When he has grasped it in its whole significance, he will have to

[p. 31]

rework his essay on the relation of the religion of reason to the positive. He is still at the beginning of this path. And at this point on it the influence of Herder upon him is everywhere visible. He cites him; his expositions now echo him, even into the very words, almost as before they echoed Lessing.

1.

How beautifully Hegel depicts, in the spirit of Herder, above all of his oldest document, the childlike spirit of the peoples and the forms of religiosity at this stage! The religious stage corresponding to the patriarchal order of the state has lasted longer than it. “This childlike sense in religion regards God as a mighty lord who has passions, indeed whims, does not always punish according to the rule of right, and whom one can ingratiate oneself with.” The imagination believes him nearer here and there; he is around good, venerable men, as in the mighty events of nature. This childlike sense gave rise to religious institutions and customs. Reason may find them ridiculous or abominable: to the imagination, which transposes itself back into that sense, they appear sublime and touching. Hegel then describes how the “spirit that originally breathed in these institutions”—an expression of Herder’s—evaporates; how the estates separate, how the congregation, which had stepped with one accord before the altars of its gods, now became a crowd from which its leaders coaxed sacred feelings. At this point he grasps, with historical profundity, the strange mixture of theatrical priestly deceit and of the need, even as reason advances, to satisfy sense and imagination and to brighten duty through beauty. And his main proposition is that folk religion remains alive as long as the images that sprang from the depths of the folk imagination still maintain their relation to the heart; instead of “lending the ears every seventh day to phrases and images that, some thousands of years ago, were intelligible and in place in Syria.”

2.

Another note then depicts more closely the derangement of the religious imagination that has been brought about in the modern peoples, and so among us, by the transplanting of the alien oriental world of images of Christianity and the

[p. 32]

displacement of our native heroes and gods. We were never a nation; even the process of the Reformation, still alive among the people today, has produced no religious festival of popular strength. We have no founders of the national state whom we could celebrate in public festivals. No religious and no political world of imagination has grown on our soil. “Christianity has depopulated Valhalla, has rooted out the imagination of the people as superstition, and has brought a faith whose climate, culture, legislation are alien to us and whose history has no connection at all with us.” Only among the lower people does a remnant of an imagination of our own still creep about as superstition. Our writers and artists work in materials that are alien to the people, whereas in England at least Shakespeare has made present to the whole people the figures of its past. The Christian materials are uncomfortable to us because of the catechism cut and the constraint that cling to them. Throughout, here, we go with Hegel in the footsteps of Herder. And in the spirit of Herder’s Fragmente he now sets forth how the renewal of Greek and Germanic mythology and heroic legend could win no permanence.

Among the influences that have corrupted the Germanic imagination he singles out, more bluntly than Herder, that of the Old Testament and of Jewish history. One could recast Klopstock’s words and say: “Is then Judea the fatherland of the Teutons?” This Jewish history can never be a living present for our religious imagination; it is not attached, as the Greek religion is, to visible, sacred places; “what in our holy books is history is so alien to our customs, our constitution, culture, our bodily and spiritual powers that there is almost no point at which we would meet it, except now and then universal human nature.” The moral we must read into it. The edification that attaches to it calls forth chiefly misunderstood zeal for God’s honor, pious conceit and a drowsy resignation to God’s will.

In this connection he raises the question: how is the “wonderful revolution,” in which Christianity won the victory over the religion of the ancient peoples, to be explained? Rationalist historiography had not solved this problem, and here springs one of the living sources of historical vision, which then flowed together into

[p. 33]

the broad stream of his Phenomenology and Philosophy of Spirit. The ripe beauty of this fragment sometimes suggests, despite the pagination, a later composition, were it not that tone and style, as well as notes that belong with it and certainly date from this time, speak against it.

In the famous fifteenth chapter of his History of the Decline and Fall of the Roman Empire, Gibbon tried to resolve this question pragmatically, by enumerating the individual psychologically effective forces. Hegel wants to grasp the change that took place in the spiritual constitution of the age itself and made the spread of Christianity possible. This is his peculiar procedure, which develops pragmatic history further. Everywhere he embraces the whole of a culture and explains from the relations of the external political organization to the inner spiritual conditions. The belief in the gods, which had grown deep into it, detached itself from the connection of ancient life and disappeared; this presupposes, according to him, absolutely that this connection itself had lost its firmness: the displacement of the Greco-Roman belief in the gods by Christianity must have been conditioned by “a quiet, secret revolution in the spirit of the age,” which is “as hard to present in words as to grasp.”

And now the explanation. Where lay the causes of this revolution in the ancient spirit? “The Greek and Roman religion was only a religion for free peoples, and with the loss of freedom its sense, its force, had to be lost as well.” “What use are nets to the fisherman when the stream has dried up?” The free man of antiquity obeyed laws that he had given himself. The idea of his fatherland, of his state, was the higher thing for which he worked. “Before this idea his individuality vanished,” it could not occur to him to beg for his person “continuation or eternal life.” “Cato turned to Plato’s Phaedo only when that which had hitherto been for him the highest order of things, his world, his republic, was destroyed; then he fled to a still higher order.” Against his gods of nature, who themselves ruled by might, the Greek could set himself, his freedom, when he came into collision with them; he knew no divine commandments, and when he called his moral law a divine one, it governed him invisibly,

[p. 34]

as Antigone acts according to the will of the gods. At this point we meet a profound glance of Hegel’s into the classical age of the Greek man. Living as a free man among the free, this Greek man grants no one the right to improve or alter him; no positive standard for his ethical life exists. The right of each to have his will was recognized, whether it be good or evil. The good recognized for themselves the duty of being good, but honored at the same time the freedom of the other not to be so. There was no abstract morality: neither an autonomous nor an authoritative one. Here we first meet the distinction between ethical life realized in the state and abstract morality, as a lower stage of ethical life.

Into this conduct of ancient man the break came when fortunate wars, wealth and increased comfort produced an aristocracy to which the people, at first voluntarily and later compelled by its power, left the government. With that “the image of the state, as a product of his activity, vanished from the soul of the citizen,” and with it that capacity which Montesquieu, under the name of virtue, made the principle of republics: the capacity to sacrifice the individual for the fatherland.

Now the way was free for the development of Christianity. The individual had now become his own center. The rights of the citizen were restricted to the security of property. For the man whose life was thus absorbed in his private ends, death was bound to become something terrible. For the republican the state had been his soul, and it was eternal for him; but if this relation to the eternal was lost to him, he found no refuge with his gods; they were single, imperfect beings, who could not satisfy an idea, and with whom the free man of antiquity had been content because he had the eternal, the independent in his own bosom. And this is now the presupposition of the inner dialectic of religiosity that Hegel develops here: “reason could never cease to find the absolute, the independent, the practical somewhere.” Accustomed to obey an alien will, these men of declining antiquity were thus ready to submit to an alien divine power. In this condition Christianity offered itself. It was either already suited to the needs of the time—“for this religion arose among a people of similar

[p. 35]

corruption and of a similar emptiness and want, only differently constituted”—or men could form out of this religion that which they needed.

And now Hegel develops the basic features of the new Christian religiosity, with a historical sense for its greatness that left Voltaire, Hume and Gibbon far behind, yet at the same time with an unrelenting consciousness of the relativity of every historical phenomenon, which constitutes the other side of this new historical worldview. In keeping with the stage of his thinking, he separates the religion of Jesus from its corruption. It was “the altar on which the powerless generation found and worshipped independence and morality,” but under the conditions of the time positive religion had to emerge from it. He characterizes it by three basic features.

First, in it the realization of the ideal is no longer willed but wished for. And this hope takes refuge more and more in the end of time. With almost cruel severity he speaks here of the “sluggish Messianic hope” that arose among the Jews in the time of their political corruption, that was adorned with oriental power of imagination, and to which this empty wishing then attached itself.

The second basic feature of positive Christian religion is the doctrine of the wickedness of human nature, “as it was generated in the bosom of this corrupted humanity, which had to despise itself from the moral side.” Even the possibility of a power for faith was made into sin, and the good appeared as the work of a being that is outside us.

The last is then that even from the ideal of perfection, namely the representation of God, the moral vanished and was forgotten. To the infinite object were applied perceptual representations of coming-to-be, creating, begetting; numerical concepts such as the Trinity and concepts of reflection such as difference were transferred to it. The divine final end of the world was restricted to the spread of the Christian religion, indeed finally to the passionate ends of the priests. From the misfortune of the time sprang the flight into heaven. The loss of freedom compelled men to rescue the absolute from life into the Godhead. Thus with the decay of the this-worldly world the objectification of the Godhead and its presentation in formulas steadily increased. For

[p. 36]

this God men murdered, slandered, stole and cheated. And interest in the state was restricted to the selfish hope of preserving existence, property and enjoyment. When the barbarians drew near, Ambrose and the holy people prayed instead of fighting on the ramparts.

Here we have the basic features of that famous depiction of unhappy consciousness which Hegel set up in the Phenomenology of Spirit as a definite stage of the development of humanity: the self-estrangement of spirit, which surrenders the unchangeable in itself to a Godhead beyond, and keeps back for itself finitude, singularity, contingency; the misery of this diremption of consciousness; the sensualization of the beyond, to which the singular existence, without deed or enjoyment, submits and sacrifices itself to the point of asceticism. But the inner dialectic by virtue of which this unhappy consciousness arises is here still set in the historical connection with the dissolution of ancient ethical life. Hegel’s historical vision emerges here directly. The reader of this and kindred presentations must be convinced that in this young Hegel lay the disposition to a great historian, and indeed even before he undertook to fix the connection of history in relations of concepts.

Especially remarkable is another note, which proceeds from here to the further stages as the Phenomenology of Spirit later developed them. In these the faith in an individual, which constitutes positive Christianity, is transformed into a higher form. That which made Jesus significant as a person now emerges “as idea in its beauty.” What man put into the individual Jesus he now recognizes joyfully as his own work. With this the view into the future opens. “The system of religion, which always took on the color of the time and of the constitutions of states, whose highest virtue was humility, will now receive its own true, independent dignity.”

3.

And now arises the task of founding the new folk religion on the faith of reason. We gather together at this point everything that has come down to us of Hegel’s work on this theme; in detail the notes naturally show, as they come from different times,

[p. 37]

divergent determinations. Some are still very immature and must belong to his very first works. One notices his dependence on the writers of the Enlightenment, Spalding, Mendelssohn, Lessing, and on the playful prose of Wieland. Hegel still inclines, in the manner of youth, to images, in whose elaboration down to the last detail his hard sense of reality still has a comic effect, whereas later it was precisely from this that effects of special power arose in his language. His thoughts then become firm, his style simpler. All these fragments, however, are inwardly connected by a few basic thoughts that run through them.

“The highest end of man is morality,” “among his dispositions, that to religion is one of the most excellent for promoting morality.” Since religion has its core in the ideas of God and immortality, but these would in themselves be dead if they did not spring from practical need and have their goal in ethical life, the point is to make the religious representations and the cult as effective as possible, at the same time yet to subordinate them to the moral goal and to shape them in keeping with the needs of developed thinking. From this Hegel develops the requirements for the representations and cultic acts of folk religion.

It may contain nothing that the universal reason of man does not recognize. Every proposition that oversteps this limit will sooner or later be attacked by reason. Spinoza, Shaftesbury, Rousseau and Kant are designated by him as the thinkers who “developed the idea of morality purely out of their own hearts and in this heart, as in a mirror, beheld the beauty of this idea and were enraptured by it”: but the higher their reverence for this morality, as it is contained in the teaching of Christ, rose, the more dispensable the dogmas seemed to them. When it is designated as the goal of human ethical life to please God, this may mean only to stand the test before him, as the ideal of holiness. Even the grounding of immortality on the practical need of reason, which is a necessary component of this folk religion, nevertheless requires careful treatment, “in order to establish it in the faith of the people.” For the expectation of rewards and punishments in another world can easily degenerate into sensuous imaginings and so suppress the moral motives.

[p. 38]

Of the greatest practical importance is the use made of the life of Jesus. In itself the sacrifice of life in Socrates is as admirable as in Christ, “but imagination pays no heed to the ratiocination of cold understanding”: precisely the combination of individual human traits with an ethical ideal, and of this ideal with the aura of the superhuman, “answers to our inclination toward ideals that are more than human.” But when the imitation of Christ is transferred to that of his suffering, there arises an unreal virtue that is only borrowed in feeling. At this point Hegel’s treatment of Christ in folk religion touches Schleiermacher’s doctrine of faith in passing; both are here conditioned by Kant. The faith in Christ is in this period for Hegel the faith in a personified ideal; he sees the education of the people to receptivity for the moral idea in the closest connection with this faith.
