So he was then still far from seeing the essence of Christianity in the objectification of the appearance of absolute spirit in the human by means of the representation of the God-humanity.

He then began to subject the usefulness of the Christian representations for a folk religion to examination. In particular he subjects the doctrine of reconciliation to a bitter criticism. Elsewhere too he has set the cheerful sacrifice of life for the fatherland in the great age of antiquity beside the dying of Christ: for him it is not a single incomparable event in history, for history has the death of many heroes to report. Here again one sees Hegel’s strongly felt opposition to the Christian world of feeling. Whenever he makes conscious to himself the deepest sense of what he understood by folk religion, which went beyond the heightening of subjective religiosity, he turns away from Christianity and the image of Greek antiquity rises before his soul. The “chief” effect of folk religion is for him “elevation and ennoblement of the spirit of a nation, so that the so often slumbering feeling of its dignity be awakened, so that the people does not throw itself away and does not let itself be thrown away.” For this there is need first of a natural relation to the divine powers above us, such as he finds realized among the Greeks. In the Christian doctrine of providence, which measures out fates by the standard of trust in God grounded on the right faith, man seems to him to be in an intolerable position.

[p. 39]

Ill humor, disappointed expectation, even doubt of this connection, must arise within Christianity and are heightened by the preponderance of doctrine over simple feeling. The Greek, in the consciousness of “iron necessity,” learns “to follow this unalterable fate without murmuring.” “With them misfortune was misfortune, pain was pain—what had happened and could not be changed, they could not brood over its purpose, for their Tyche was blind—but to this necessity they then also submitted willingly, with all possible resignation.” This thought became of the greatest importance for the circle of ideas of the next period; in this time the question of the organization of religious life is for him almost more decisive still. Its inwardness is not to appear in abstract opposition to the customs, the festivals, the political life of a nation; the opposition between cheerfulness in its worldly expressions of life and a holiness separated from life, which hampers the free enjoyment of its self-conscious power, is not to run through the life of the people: always there stands before Hegel the living connection of religious inwardness with all expressions of national life, with the orders of the state itself, as it existed in Greece. So he demands for us too that the customs, festivals and ceremonies be connected with the views and feelings of the people. “Such essential customs of religion must really be connected no more closely with the latter than with the spirit of the people, and must really have sprouted from this—otherwise their practice is lifeless, cold, powerless.” “If the joys, the cheerfulness of men, have to be ashamed before religion—if one who made merry at a public festival has to slink into the temple, then the form of religion has an outward side that is too gloomy.” The happiness of the people, above all the unity of its whole being, is to come to presentation in the religious festivals, in religious plays. In this connection Hegel speaks with expressions of strong aversion of the Lord’s Supper, which ought really to be enjoyment of the community and now can only be taken with disgust. There seemed to him, for his time, “as such a pure means of elevating the holy feeling, which is least capable of abuse, perhaps only holy music and the singing of a whole people to remain.”

[p. 40]

#### The Turn to Pantheism.

1.

While Hegel was living in this great connection of his labors on Christianity, the philosophical movement was advancing from the idealism of freedom of Kant and Fichte, in Schelling, to objective idealism—the system that conceives the universe as the natural effect of the divine power. Through Kant and Christianity Hegel had been held longer than Schelling in the idealism of freedom. When he was now, under the influence of his friend, swept into the new movement, he entered it as the stronger. He had lived through the Enlightenment in himself in order to overcome it, but at the same time had also taken up into himself what was lasting in it; and this gave him, as it did all those minds of his generation who in the nineteenth century upheld science against Romanticism—Wilhelm von Humboldt, Niebuhr, Schleiermacher, Herbart, the great investigators of nature—spiritual security and compact firmness, while Schelling, like Friedrich Schlegel, was to lose himself in mysticism.

In the days in which Hegel was writing down the last lines of his draft “The Life of Jesus,” a letter from Schelling arrived for him; at the same time Schelling sent him his second philosophical essay, on the I as principle of philosophy. The letter from Tübingen shows a deep depression, intensified by illness and loneliness. Fichte’s activity was interrupted, the opponents of the new philosophy were triumphing, Schelling himself, after a passionately productive epoch, felt weariness and distrust of his own achievements. So he was grateful for the warmth with which Hegel attached himself to him.

After Hegel had studied himself into Fichte’s Foundation and read the friend’s new essay, he expressed (30 August 1795) his agreement. He had once, he wrote, wanted to make clear in an essay what it could mean to draw near to God; this inner process he had wanted to put in the place of the Kantian method of inferring from postulates: “what hovered before me dark and undeveloped, your essay has clarified for me in the most splendid and satisfying way.” He consoles the friend with noble, deep words. “You have silently thrown your word into the

[p. 41]

infinite time; to be grinned at here and there, that, I know, you despise—but with regard to others, who recoil before the results, your essay is as good as unwritten. Your system will have the fate of all the systems of those men whose spirit has run ahead of the faith and prejudices of their times,” namely, to be decried and refuted at first, then half a century later to be acknowledged by the public as self-evident.

Schelling’s essay contains the first presentation of the pantheism as he now derived it from the absolute I. It was conceived as a counterpart to Spinoza’s Ethics. The causality of the infinite I may not be determined as morality or wisdom, but only as absolute power. Thus the contradiction in Kant is sublated; for if the supersensible world could become an object for practical philosophy as something outside the I, it would also have to be able to be an object, that is, knowable, for theoretical philosophy. In this infinite I there is no personality and no consciousness. “If substance is the unconditioned, then the I is the only substance.”

Hegel agrees with these ideas, in particular with the polemic against the attributes of God. Yet even then he rejected the application of the concept of substance to the absolute I. The concept of substance seems to him inapplicable to the absolute I, because it cannot be separated from that of the attributes. Thus the beginning of his lasting difference from Schelling is already here. Modestly he adds: “Of my own labors it is not worth the trouble to speak.” Perhaps in some time he would send him the plan of something that he intended to work out; it was his labors on Christian religiosity.

2.

Between these utterances of Hegel, which show him only as yet drawn as if from afar to the new pantheism, and a poem, Eleusis, to Hölderlin, from August 1796, in which the pantheistic feeling expresses itself with wonderfully immediate power, lies a year. No utterance from it that would throw light on the further development of his worldview has been preserved—at least none that could be placed with certainty in this time. One can only conjecture how, once through Schelling’s influence the restriction had fallen,

[p. 42]

which he had given to his spirit by the long-sustained direction of his gaze on the philosophical presuppositions of Christianity, his basic nature—the surrender to the objectivity of the whole of reality, the urge to grasp it with all his powers—became free and now, on an occasion that promised to free him outwardly too and awakened the sunken energy of life in him (Hölderlin had asked him whether he would accept a private-tutor’s post in Frankfurt), broke out with all force. One feels in these verses too just what Hölderlin meant for the friend’s frame of mind, how strongly Hölderlin’s enthusiasm worked upon him. So they show the philosophical mood in which Hegel was bound to the two companions of his youth, and at the same time how peculiarly it revealed itself in his imagination. For just as his objective immersion remained turned, now too, toward the religious problems, so we see his basic pantheistic conception conditioned by them as well. And therefore this poem, in linking the intuition of the all-one as something incomprehensible and unutterable, which can only be revered in silence and presented through deeds, to the Eleusinian mysteries, in which the eternal had won form in the imagination, marks also an important turning point in the long, solitary theological labor that we have followed. Hegel wrote the poem shortly before his departure from Bern, at the family’s country seat between Lake Neuchâtel and Lake Biel.

It is evening, the mood of Faust after his return from the Easter walk to his study. “Around me, in me dwells calm.” He reminds the friend of the old covenant, “to live only for free truth, never, never to enter into peace with the statute that regulates opinion and feeling”! From the nocturnal heaven streams forgetting of all wishes.

“What I called mine vanishes.

I give myself up to the immeasurable.

I am in it, am All, am only It.

Estranged to the returning thought,

It shudders at the infinite, and astonished

It grasps not the depth of this beholding.”

Then there appears to him the embodiment of this “One and All” in the divine figures of the Greek religion of nature. The myth and cult of the Eleusinian mysteries present themselves to the imagination of the solitary

[p. 43]

thinker. “To the senses imagination brings the eternal near, weds it with form.”

“Welcome, you,

Sublime spirits, lofty shades,

From whose brow perfection shines.

It does not terrify. I feel it is my home too,

The splendor, the gravity that flows about you.”

Might the portals of the sanctuary at Eleusis open, the hymns resound! But “no tone of the holy consecrations has saved itself for us.” “To the son of initiation the fullness of the lofty teachings, the depth of the unutterable feeling, was far too holy for him to deem dry signs worthy of them.”

What an opposition between the divine mystery of infinite nature presented in the mysteries and the verbiage of Christian dogmatics, which has become “the cloak of the eloquent hypocrite,” “the rod even of the merry boy”!

The vision ends in the ideal that at this time wholly filled him. To the Greeks the mystery of the infinite appeared in the ideal ethical order of their state and not in words.

“Therefore you did not live upon their lips.

Their life honored you. In their deeds you live still.

This night too I perceived you, holy Godhead,

You, too, your children’s life often reveals to me,

You I often sense as the soul of their deeds!

You are the lofty sense, the faithful belief,

That, though all else go under, wavers not from a godhead.”

[p. 44]

## Part Two.

### The Genesis of Hegel’s Worldview in Connection with His Theological Studies.

#### The Years as Private Tutor in Frankfurt.

Hegel had not felt happy in Bern. As early as the summer Schelling urged him in friendly words to rise out of the state of irresolution and dejection, which was unworthy of his great powers, and he and Hölderlin made plans for him. Hegel himself was thinking of a post as Repetent in Tübingen, but Hölderlin was of the opinion that his friend should shut himself up in that grave only when nothing else was left but to deal in boot-blacking and pomade. And at last he was able to offer him, in Frankfurt am Main, where he himself was private tutor with the banker Gontard, a post in the house of the rich merchant Gogel. “If you come here, there lives not far from you a person who, amid rather motley changes in his situation and his character, has nonetheless remained true to you with heart and memory and mind, and who lacks nothing but you.” We already know how this invitation affected Hegel; he followed the proposal. “How large a share,” he wrote, “my longing for you had in my swift decision—of that, nothing”; “from every line of your letter speaks your unalterable friendship for me.” Before taking up his post in Frankfurt, however, he went first, in the autumn of 1796, to Stuttgart once more, to see his family again. They found him deeply withdrawn into himself, and only in a very narrow circle was anything of his old cheerfulness to be felt. It is the same deep and heavy earnestness of thought that speaks from the notes of this epoch as well.

In January 1797 he arrived in Frankfurt. The Gogel family lived in a stately house on the Rossmarkt. The private tutor had two

[p. 45]

boys to instruct, his relation to the members of the family was agreeable, his pecuniary situation favorable, and the fair, cheerful city was at that time politically interesting as well. Hegel’s melancholy, however, did not lift here either. The pressure of his circumstances still tormented him. His manuscripts from this time attest the immense concentration of mind in which he then lived; a new understanding of Christianity arose in him, and out of it there grew for him the formula of his mystical metaphysics. The deep sufferings of a power of will of genius that was confined from without heightened his capacity to penetrate into the secret of these conditions in the history of religion, and the dark subject, heavy with pain and profundity, weighed upon his soul. “Greet for me also,” he wrote later to Sinclair, “the high Feldberg and Alkin, toward which I looked across so often and so gladly from unfortunate Frankfurt, because I knew you at their foot.”

Two friendly relations accompanied and furthered Hegel’s work in these Frankfurt years. Near Homburg lived the Legation Councillor Sinclair, who was the adviser of the Landgrave of Homburg in personal as well as political affairs. He had studied in Tübingen and was already there a friend of Hegel and Hölderlin. His poetic attempts are akin to Romanticism and to Hölderlin, and as a philosopher he developed, starting from Fichte, whose hearer he had been, a philosophical system of his own, of a Christian-mystical character. And in Frankfurt itself there was Hölderlin. Hegel found him in the midst of that tragic entanglement of his life which was determined by his love for Frau Gontard. “Hegel’s company”—so Hölderlin wrote soon after his arrival—“is very beneficial to me. I love the calm men of understanding, because one can orient oneself so well among them when one does not quite know how one stands with oneself and the world.” The fine philosophical intercourse of the Tübingen years renewed itself for them; it rested on the intimate kinship of their ideas. Soon after Hegel had arrived in Frankfurt, the first volume of Hyperion appeared, which proclaimed Hölderlin’s poetic pantheism. This novel was at the same time filled with the longing for the lost beauty of Greek life and with the hope for a new age of the world that was to realize a lofty humanity and a free order of society. How heavy it must have been for the friends to bear the contradiction

[p. 46]

of these ideals with the life around them and with their personal fate! And how natural it was that Hölderlin’s poetry was influenced by the philosophical profundity of his friend, while Hegel for his part sought a poetic expression for such moods and ideals, and his prose transformed itself in order to give expression to the power of the new ideas!

Indeed, the force alone with which pantheism then unfolded in him was bound to make him a poet. We recall his verses to Hölderlin. Among the notes of this time Hegel’s biographer has seen still other poetic attempts, from which he communicates some. The sublime becomes baroque here, profundity dark. In impossible distichs Hegel depicts a scene with his poodle, which thus accompanied him, a genuine Faust, at that time. He describes a bath in the moonlight; Luna lays her rays caressingly on the surfaces and the trees: “for the immortals, growing no poorer, no lower, give themselves to the earth and live in it.” Spring breaks in, and as the buds swell toward it, it awakens in him an infinite longing to dissolve wholly into nature; “the spirit should indeed unite with nature, but not too fast, nor unconsecrated.”

More important, however, than such passing poetic attempts is the effect that the inner movement of this time exerted on his prose language. The new style, peculiar to him, begins to form from the last Bern period on. It arises in connection with the poetic prose in which the new worldview then expressed itself—the prose of Hölderlin, Hülsen, Berger, Schelling, and then of the Speeches and Soliloquies of Schleiermacher; but it shows from the start, in the union of an untiring, objectively determined reflection with the power of mood, a character of its own. In the energy of its expression, in the soulful presentation of the abstract, it is more capable of communicating inner movements than Hegel’s awkward verses.

In this Frankfurt period the complete transformation of his mind took place, which had already begun in Bern and arose quite gradually out of the deepening into the historical world under the impulse of the age and the influence of the forward-striding Schelling. This transformation and the new position that Hegel won from it toward the theological and philosophical problems presents itself to us in a

[p. 47]

large complex of manuscripts, which form a unity according to their subject matter, their standpoint and inner connection, and mark a new epoch of Hegel. It can be determined only relatively chronologically, and least of all can Hegel’s places of residence be used for this. The limit backward is given by the end of the dependence on Kant, of the interpretation of Christianity under the viewpoint of Kant’s religion of reason, and of the determination of the concept of positive religion by its opposition to the religion of reason. Into this new epoch we now enter.

#### The Foundations of Hegel’s Mystical Pantheism and His New Conception of History in the Context of German Thought.

The problem that arises for this epoch of Hegel is the inner development in which he advanced from Kant’s critical philosophy to a new metaphysical system and from his theological labors to comprehensive historical conceptions: a process in which these two sides of his scientific work, the systematic and the historical, constantly influenced each other. Only a few letters from this time have been preserved, and their yield for Hegel’s developmental history is slight. Hegel was at that time wholly lost in his work; he suffered from his relation to the world, all the more as his contemporaries, Schelling in the lead, were beginning to take their place in life: hence there falls in this time his long silence toward Schelling, and of a correspondence between him and Hölderlin, since the latter had moved from Frankfurt to Homburg, no trace is found either. So we are referred, for the understanding of this epoch of Hegel too, to his manuscripts.

Hegel passed in this period out of the sphere of dominion of Kant into that of Fichte and Schelling. He took part in the philosophical movement that ran its course from Fichte’s programmatic essay (1794) to Schelling’s philosophy of nature (1797) and his System of Transcendental Idealism (1800). Simply determined by the course of this development, however, he was not; even for the history of his own formation of thought the historical course refutes the method of linear, logical construction, as Hegel applied it to this period and the historians

[p. 48]

of philosophy from his school afterwards applied it to him himself. At no moment of his development was he a Fichtean or a Schellingian, and alongside the influence of Fichte influences of quite another kind also made themselves felt in his thinking. Moreover, he came to the study of Fichte’s Wissenschaftslehre only in the summer of 1795, when he had already become acquainted with Schelling’s essay on the I, in which the turn to a metaphysical monism had been accomplished, and already in the summer of 1796, in the poem to Hölderlin and then in the manuscripts, this monism confronts us in Hegel in a form differing from Schelling’s standpoint at that time. But the presuppositions under which Hegel’s development took place lay nonetheless in the connection of philosophical thinking that led from Kant to Schelling; in this development lay the grounds on account of which he could pass from the standpoint of critical epistemology, which he had thought through so thoroughly, to work on a new metaphysics. Besides, the material of concepts with which he built up this new metaphysics was taken for the most part from the thinkers from Kant to Schelling. So the narrative must halt at this point and seek to grasp the inner connection of German philosophical thinking, so far as it determined Hegel.

1.

Since Plato no one had achieved more for the justification of thinking before itself, in which the nearest task of recent German philosophy lay, than Kant. He had discovered the epistemological-critical procedure that goes back from the universal validity and necessity of knowledge to the conditions in the thinking subject that make knowledge possible, and had found the most general of these conditions in the synthetic faculty that combines the manifold of the given into systematic unity. He had demonstrated irrefutably that experience and empirical science come about only through the synthetic power of thinking, and that, on the other hand, this thinking arrives at valid cognitions only within the domain of the experienceable. And beyond these firm results he handed down to the philosophy after him so universal a formulation of the problem of the cognitive value of the assumptions contained in empirical knowledge, as

[p. 49]

no investigator before him had found it, together with concepts belonging to the theory of knowledge, such as form of intuition, schematism, intellectual intuition, amphiboly of the concepts of reflection, ideas of reason, antinomies in the region of the unconditioned, regulative principles, postulates, whose influence has been immeasurable.

But of the methods which he applied in order to establish the conditions of valid knowledge in consciousness, only the first was probative, the one that proceeded from the point that what itself first makes experience possible may not be conceived as a product of experience. Other modes of procedure, with which he went far beyond the yield of this method, are exposed to considerable objections. That holds for his doctrine of space and time as the forms of intuition, as for his theory of the functions, the categories and the axioms of the knowledge of the understanding. It has here been downright fatal for the correctness of his results that he believed he might make use of the distinctions which formal logic had found in judgment, in order to derive from them a system of the functions of the understanding a priori. Not only is the system of the a priori which he established by this method open to challenge; his analysis thus halted at the connection of the logical forms and laws, which ought to have formed precisely the decisive object of the epistemological dissection and to have been examined as to its relation to lived experiences and external data. If one separates out the elementary logical operations, such as those of comparing, combining and separating, these can be regarded just as much as conditioned by the structure of the given as conditions of the objective apprehension of it. The real categories, however, above all substance and causality, show properties that must make their origin from functions of the understanding problematic. Thus Kant, in undertaking to enumerate and order, detached from this material, the forms of intuition and of thinking which, in their application to the material of the given, make experience possible, overstepped the limits of what can be demonstrated by exact proof. And just as questionable as his system of the a priori functions of intuition and of thinking was his concept of a thing in itself, as the condition of external experience independent of the subject. For by means of the derivation of the categories from judgment

[p. 50]

it resulted for Kant that existence, reality, substance, cause and effect are categories of the understanding; but with that his concept of a thing in itself fell away; for precisely in that he derived from the formal distinctions of judgment functions of the understanding a priori, among them causality, even reality and existence, he blocked his own way to any assertion about something independent of the subject. Indeed, in the end his whole fundamental assumption, which referred consciousness, for which the contents are there, to the individual subject, was thus not grounded.

From the Critique of Pure Reason Kant’s work of laying a foundation for philosophy proceeded to the Critique of Practical Reason and to the Critique of Judgment. Whereas in the former he had sought out the principles that make experience and empirical science possible, in his second major work he sought to show the certainty of ethical action in the moral law, which, independently of the matter of the individual ends, would pronounce the universal condition to which the ethical character of actions is bound. The Critique of Judgment then brought the ideas which practical reason had derived from the ethical law into unity with the thought of a purpose of nature, which proved unavoidable within the knowledge of nature. Thus there opens up here a connection created by Kant that has exercised the strongest effect upon the age. For here was the first beginning of a history of the human spirit that is grounded upon a necessary connection within it. In that the intellect oversteps the limits of the experienceable, it falls, according to Kant’s great conception, into contradictions in the region of the unconditioned: metaphysics as the science of God, world and soul proves impossible; only in the domain of practical conduct is there found in the ethical law an unconditioned that opens an access to the transcendent order of things; the connection of the conditions of its realization demanded from the ethical law with the principle of purpose required for the interpretation of the organic world finally makes possible a coherent worldview. In the relations thus arising between the various achievements of spirit, through which they are joined into the whole of an inner history in which spirit realizes the essence dwelling in it and constituting its worth, a foundation was given for the profound works of Schiller and Wilhelm von Humboldt. With this there were combined

[p. 51]

effects of Kant upon the historical sciences that stemmed from another part of his life’s work. His teachings on the origin and constitution of the universe and the position of the earth in it, his physical geography, anthropology and philosophy of history stimulated, from Herder on, the attempts at a comprehensive philosophical consideration of history. Empirical knowledge, art, religion, the world of ethical life, which acquires its outer form in the legal order, were already conceived in Kant’s school as the interrelated parts of the realization of the human essence in an inner development. It is of decisive importance for the understanding of Hegel how a new comprehension of the historical world was here already being prepared, starting from Kant.

But here too, as everywhere with this thinker, who in the highest truthfulness ever anew wrestled with the difficulties and advanced to the last, deficiencies of his demonstration and contradictions in his thinking made themselves felt, which did not permit one to stop with him. Certainly there lay a deep truth in the fact that the binding of the will in the ethical law possesses an unconditional validity, in contrast to the ethical demand to realize the ideal of perfection, or to the moral satisfaction which acts of kindness and benevolence produce. But Kant’s grounding of this was insufficient. Other faults resulted from the rigid separations within the spiritual connection, which were justified as methodological aids but nonetheless hardened in Kant into separations in spiritual life itself.

These traits in Kant’s life-work now condition his effect upon the next generation. He revolutionized the whole of German thinking. Each of the significant philosophical minds of this time stood under his influence and at the same time had to advance beyond him. Above all there were two moments in him that gave philosophy another turn. The deficient grounding and incompatibility of his results called forth within the theory of knowledge a restless dialectic that pursued the possibilities of a consistent epistemological grounding of knowledge, without the skepticism of a Maimon, Beck or Schulze having really been refuted; and so there arose a weariness of this kind of work, still heightened by the perception of how little it

[p. 52]

advanced the positive tasks of knowledge. And then in Kant’s three principal works themselves the entrance into a hitherto unknown world became visible, which drew irresistibly away from epistemology back again into objective apprehension. If the psychology of the Enlightenment that preceded Kant had led spirit back into itself, and if poetry, joined with it, had let new peculiarities and depths of the inner world be seen, Kant and his school now stepped beyond this, toward something new. They went back from the psychic life of individuals to the conditions in it that make possible a world common to individuals, in universally valid cognitions and in universal and necessary orders of life. This was the first step toward grasping a spiritual unity pervading our whole species and its development, as that development was necessarily conditioned by the connection of its functions. And when Kant then in the Critique of Judgment erected, above the categories of the understanding which govern the empirical sciences, the form of apprehension of a whole in which an immanent purpose realizes itself—as an all-embracing form of the understanding of the world—there was thereby given a moment decisive for objective idealism. Thought began to go back behind the given of the spiritual-historical particulars into a reality that cannot be grasped, which rests in the depth of our being—into something metaphysical of a new character.

2.

Here Fichte was the first to intervene. In his Wissenschaftslehre he set himself the same task as Kant in his Critiques. But in that he excluded the thing in itself from the domain of theoretical knowledge, and extended the creative faculty of the I also to the manifold of experience, which Kant had let be given to the I, the concept of this creative I received a new sense in his hands. By overstepping Kant’s analytic method he arrived at a connection contained in this creative I, which could be derived. And he supplemented Kant’s method of inference by intellectual intuition. Thus there arose for him, on the basis of lived experience, a self-contained connection of the acts of the pure I, which, self-active, creating, distinguished from the empirical individual subject in which it can be shown, produces an order of appearances according to laws,

[p. 53]

which is the same for every individual. The acts of this I form for him a developmental series, which advances from the unconscious production of the world of appearances to the self-knowledge and self-realization of spirit. With this the concept of a connection was prepared in which, advancing through all individuals, spirit realizes its essence—thus the concept of the development of universal spirit. Fichte, the most convinced and most vigorous representative of the idealism of freedom, paved for the new generation the way to a pantheism which, proceeding from the pure to the absolute I, grasps in the connection of the universally valid, necessary acts of spirit the principle of the explanation of the world. Thus in this period Hegel designated his principle as spirit.

And Fichte also created the dialectical method in which Schelling and Hegel were to present the development of spirit.

At first the task that this dialectic set itself was still wholly determined by Kant’s critiques of reason. Fichte’s dialectic is, in the general part of the Wissenschaftslehre, the method through which the philosophizing subject makes itself master of the fundamental determinations of consciousness that are valid for all rational beings, and thinks in addition the connection of their conditions. This method reaches its goal when it has grasped the connection of the I in which the necessary determinations of reason are grounded. From propositions that appear in consciousness as necessary it goes back to what makes these propositions possible. The novelty of this method lay in the fact that here, starting from propositions appearing in consciousness, on the ground of the way in which the relation of the parts in the structure of a whole conditions these parts, an inference was drawn back to the connection of consciousness. And in the solution of this task there results, as a closer determination of this method, that it advances from thesis through antithesis to synthesis. In this dialectic of Fichte the contradiction has a different meaning from that which it has in Hegel’s dialectic, as it was then preparing itself. When Fichte discovers a contradiction in the fact that the positing of the not-I, just as much as that of the I, is a positing in the I, but insofar as the not-I is posited the I is not posited, he does not mean thereby to show a real contradiction which would be overcome by the synthesis.

[p. 54]

He himself describes the procedure of his idealism thus: this idealism shows that what is first set up as a principle and directly demonstrated in consciousness is not possible unless something else at the same time also happens, and this other not unless a third happens; until the conditions of what was first pointed out are completely exhausted and it has become fully comprehensible as to its possibility. His course is an uninterrupted advance from the conditioned to the condition. Accordingly the contradiction on which the dialectic of the foundation of the Wissenschaftslehre rests does not subsist between the acts of the I, but between propositions that express them incompletely and therefore must be supplemented. And his method is given by Kant’s procedure, which goes back from the given to its conditions. If, however, one now steps from the general laying of the foundation of the Wissenschaftslehre into its theoretical and practical parts, and follows the course in which the I, as determined by the not-I, develops the stages of the apprehension of the world, and the I, as determining the not-I, in infinite progress extends the spheres of its independence: then we find here development of spirit and in it a dialectic really effective in this development. There, where Fichte, within the second part of his Wissenschaftslehre, the foundation of theoretical knowledge, enters into the deduction of representation, he himself brings out the difference of the newly entering procedure from the one applied before. On the basis of the results thus obtained it is only to raise its object to consciousness; what it shows are realities which are derived from the absolute productive faculty of the I as the principle of the theoretical Wissenschaftslehre and which constitute the connection of our theoretical conduct; in the practical part of the Wissenschaftslehre the absolute productive faculty will then be traced back to a still higher principle. “The Wissenschaftslehre is to be a pragmatic history of the human spirit. Until now we have worked only in order to gain an entrance into it; only in order to be able to point to an undoubted fact. We have this fact; and from now on our perception, to be sure not blind but experimenting, may calmly follow the course of events.” This is the starting point of Hegel’s method.

[p. 55]

3.

So there lay in critical idealism itself moments that pressed toward a new metaphysics. In Kant and Fichte themselves the conditions for the emergence of objective idealism were contained, as the generation of Schelling, Hegel, Schleiermacher and Schopenhauer has brought it forth. Not as if this objective idealism had been the logically necessary consequence of the critical systems. Had the methods of analysis which Lambert, Kant and Maimon developed been pursued further and applied in particular to the logic that Kant took over uncritically, the course of our philosophy would have become quite another. Instead, Fichte sought to complete Kant by comprehending his Critiques in a system. And this comprehension was accomplished through such problematic concepts as the positing of the I, the opposition of the not-I—concepts which possess sufficient grounding neither in Fichte’s inferences nor in experience. From the demand to think conditions that make possible the cooperation of individuals toward universally valid knowledge, a principle of the pure I had become.

We now transpose ourselves into the generation that followed Fichte and to which Hegel belonged, in order to grasp the historical necessities by virtue of which it brought forth the new pantheism.

Schelling developed in the little essay “On the I as Principle of Philosophy, or on the Unconditioned in Human Knowledge” (1795) the program of this standpoint. If there is to be knowledge, this presupposes that “we know One thing at least, to which we do not arrive again through another knowledge, and which itself contains the real ground of all our knowledge.” In this unconditioned the principle of being must coincide with that of thinking. These formulas of Spinoza now find in the connection of the necessary determinations in the pure I, which transcendental philosophy shows, a real foundation. Dogmatism proceeds from a not-I posited before all I; it is unable to ground knowledge from it. The new system of science proceeds from the absolute I excluding everything opposed; to this I unconditional independence belongs, it contains all reality, it is the one substance in which everything is that is,

[p. 56]

and absolute power; in it teleology and mechanism coincide; thus the I is the principle of the connection of the world. Upon this principle the new objective idealism is now grounded. The physical world is for it the manifestation of something spiritual. The connection that links the achievements of the spiritual into a whole becomes here the key to the understanding of the physical and the historical world; the universe is placed, by a well-grounded and conscious method, under the schema of the connection found in spirit. With this the first step had been taken on a fateful road. From the facts of life and of science there follows only some relation between thinking and nature, according to which the latter is so represented in the human spirit that our orientation in the world becomes possible. The view into the universe admonishes us to leave room for quite different relations of the same nature to thinking beings. In the place of such a relation between spirit and nature there now steps their identity, which has as its consequence the anthropocentric character of Hegelian metaphysics.

The metaphysics of objective idealism entered a new stage with Schelling’s essay. The constructive metaphysics of the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries had laid definitions and axioms at the foundation of the knowledge of reality. These elements, isolated by the understanding and separated out in abstraction, were set, by the constructive procedure tested in mathematical natural science, in relations to one another and to given reality, and thus arose the interpretation of this reality in the metaphysical systems. The classic example of this constructive procedure was the system of Spinoza. From the standpoint of transcendental philosophy, however, all his concepts of substance, mode and causal relation presented themselves as torn loose from the living connection of the spiritual functions, in which they nonetheless have their reality and their determinateness.

For the new objective idealism, which grasps the universe as a whole whose inwardness extends itself in nature and history, the One divine power is present in every part of the world as a connection between the parts and the whole and between the parts themselves, which is grounded in the connection of spirit. Since now this divine power acts according to the law of this connection dwelling in it, it acts

[p. 57]

necessarily; and since its inner lawful necessity determines every part of the universe, it is the same as ground of the world and as life of its parts. For the connection in which it acts is everywhere the same, and therefore the essence of this power presents itself in every finite part of the world. It fills every part of the world with its acting, and everywhere its acting is the same.

So the new objective idealism becomes pantheism, and it is a matter of indifference here how the nature of the One divine ground is determined, in particular whether the spiritual connection assumed in it is posited, say, as unconscious or as conscious. Only on two sides must this pantheism, its modification in panentheism included, be delimited: on the one hand against the unclear assumptions of those thinkers who mix the concept of creation into such a system—for creation designates a free manner of acting, which is distinguished from the causal necessity subsisting in any part of the world—on the other hand against a standpoint that so defines the world-determining power that the concept of the Godhead can no longer be applied to it. This is the case in the system of Schopenhauer, in which the influences of German idealism crossed with those of Voltaire and the French naturalists.

The new pantheism differs from that of the preceding age first in that the interpretation of the given from a spiritual connection has become a conscious method, well grounded in the epistemology of Kant and Fichte and in their transcendental-philosophical concepts of a universal and necessary connection of spirit. Through this, this pantheism is distinguished from the otherwise often kindred Stoic and Neoplatonic systems. A second difference consists in the tendency, conditioned by this foundation, to conceive the acting of the divine power in the world as development. The natural-scientific doctrine of the evolution of the universe was transformed, where this evolution was regarded as determined by an immanent teleology, into a doctrine of development, and this now reached its completion in that a teleologically determined connection of a spiritual kind could be laid at its foundation.

The power which this new pantheism exercised, the wealth of its forms, its connection with the whole spiritual constitution of the age

[p. 58]

become visible, however, only when we extend our horizon over the whole development of modern pantheism. It extended from Shaftesbury and Hemsterhuis through Herder, Goethe, the letters of Julius to Raphael, to the generation that expressed and grounded it in its own way simultaneously with Schelling’s essay of 1795 and soon after. This movement was borne by poets, writers, investigators of nature, alongside the philosophers.

There were three moments that gave it the overpowering expansive force with which it then appeared.

Above all there joined itself with it a drive toward the heightening of human power, which was alive in all these men. The conception of man’s relation to God was to be freed from the old religious schema of lordship and subjection. With head erect man was to go through life, reverent toward the divine powers, but in the feeling of kinship with them. The piety that finds expression in Greek tragedy became the model for this frame of mind. The young men who in Jena, Berlin and Tübingen advanced from Kant to Fichte and now entered the pantheistic movement all brought with them the same faith in the coming heightening of humanity, which now received its metaphysical grounding through the doctrine of the development of the universe.

Another moment that was effective in this pantheistic movement lay in the increasing opposition to the categories of the understanding under which the age of Enlightenment had thought nature and history. Indeed, it was recognized that the progress of the scientific spirit itself, as it was accomplished in the abstract concepts of mathematical natural science, bore within it a tendency to displace from the world poetry and the religious feeling full of presentiment, weaving in mystery. In Goethe the opposition of the poetic genius, in whom imagination had been active from childhood on to build up a poetic world, against the mathematical conception of nature and its lightless, lifeless world of concepts had emerged with force. In Hamann, Lavater, Herder the protest of religious natures against the concepts of the European Enlightenment made itself felt. This too now entered into the pantheistic movement. Hölderlin proclaimed, since the year 1794, in which his Hyperion fragment appeared in the Thalia,

[p. 59]

thus before Schelling’s essay on the I, a pantheism that was grounded on the enthusiasm of the artist. Understanding and reason, according to him, are unable to grasp the infinite; that dawns only on the inspiration of the artist, who experiences beauty: for beauty is the appearance of unity in manifoldness. And so the philosophical expression for this lived experience of beauty is the pantheistic formula of the immanence of the divine unity in the manifoldness of appearances. The inspiration of the poet is the starting point of true philosophy, and this expresses only, in abstract concepts, dividing, combining, separating out, what artistic inspiration has found. This standpoint was grounded by Hölderlin through the critique of Fichte and of the monism that proceeded from him. The regression from the facts of consciousness to an absolute I abolishes consciousness itself and with it every possible content of this I; for such a content can after all be only for a consciousness. And as Hölderlin traced the new pantheistic metaphysics back to the lived experience of the poet, so Schleiermacher in his Speeches on Religion derived and justified it from the lived experience of the religious genius. He too rejected the progress from the facts of consciousness through the pure I to pantheistic metaphysics. It is religious lived experience that leads out beyond the barriers of the facts of consciousness and discloses a world of intuitions and concepts which are not attainable for the mere understanding.

The third moment that asserted itself in this new pantheistic movement stood in direct relation to Goethe: intuition asserted its right against the abstract concepts. In Berger’s letters on nature this showed itself, still more decidedly in the philosophers of nature such as Steffens, Ritter, Oken, who pursue development in organic nature. And with this trait of the age there met a vehement striving of the philosophical spirit to break through Kant’s epistemological abstractions into an objective apprehension. The connection of reality had been sundered by Kant’s critical work into a metaphysics of nature a priori, a teleological interpretation of the organic world, an empirical doctrine of the soul, a philosophy of history, in which deficient empirical knowledge was combined with an insufficient teleological hypothesis, and, separated from this,

[p. 60]

a morality a priori. Moreover, Kant’s scientific caution had set up, at the transitional points from inorganic to organic nature and from this to the human spirit, barriers that seemed to make the carrying through of the idea of development impossible. Such restrictions and limitations called forth a dangerous passion to win a unified, natural relation of thinking to reality and to make this comprehensible as a unified organic whole through the idea of development. The thinkers who took this path, Schelling, Schleiermacher, Hegel, have toward Kant a mood peculiarly mixed of reverence and of deep aversion. From this striving into the objective world arose Schelling’s philosophy of nature and Hegel’s philosophy of spirit. The former won only a transitory validity; in the philosophy of spirit and of history, on the other hand, through the connection of the spiritual achievements in the I, as Kant and Fichte had made it visible, the foundation was won for a real progress of historical knowledge.

4.

In the midst of this movement, which took place in Hegel’s generation and had the new objective idealism as its consequence, there arose then, in the years from 1795 to 1800, Hegel’s mystical pantheism. Literary Germany was in a process of transformation since poetry, the study of antiquity and philosophy entered into ever closer connection with one another; in this connection of poetry, still ever rising, with philosophy, history and criticism, beginnings of something new arose at the most various points, first in Berlin, Jena and Dresden; Hölderlin, Friedrich Schlegel, Novalis, Schleiermacher, Hülsen, Berger went forward in the same direction as Schelling and Hegel. Hegel’s manuscripts and his sparse letters give no information as to what of this acted upon him. Even the influence of Schelling on Hegel, the most important that a contemporary exerted on him, can be elucidated only by uncertain inferences.

Above all, the often discussed question must occupy us here, how far Schelling determined Hegel’s pantheistic worldview, as it confronts us from the manuscripts of this period. Let us call to mind

[p. 61]

how strong, irresistible and universal the movement was in which pantheism came to prevail. Likewise the moments that led in Kant and Fichte to the new metaphysics worked simultaneously in each single one of the thinkers and writers who went ahead in the new generation. If this state of affairs already makes difficult the determination of what Hegel took over from Schelling, there lies a perhaps insuperable difficulty for the solution of this question in the fact that, to all appearance, any research will be able only to refine the relative chronology of Hegel’s manuscripts: but so long as it does not succeed in extracting real datings from them, their temporal relation to Schelling’s works cannot be established with certainty.

Certain to us from the developmental history so far is at first only the one proposition, that Hegel was determined by Schelling to go on from Fichte’s pure I to the conception of an absolute I—thus to construct the universe from the transcendental connection that is effective in the subject as universally valid and linking all thinking beings. If we wish to create further clarity beyond this about the relations of the two to one another, we must, in order not to fall into an external literary method, start from Hegel himself, as he had formed himself at this time.

Every metaphysical genius expresses in concepts a side of reality that had not yet been seen in this way. This side dawns on him in metaphysical lived experience. Biographically regarded, this consists in a series of processes of lived experience, but they become philosophical lived experience in that a general state of affairs is apprehended in them. The energy of experiencing, joined with this peculiar capacity to perceive, in impersonal conduct, the general state of affairs in lived experience, constitutes the genius of the metaphysician. And from the succession of such lived experiences there arises metaphysical experience, whose object is an experienceable quite different from that of the positive sciences. So it was also in Hegel, one of the greatest metaphysicians of all times. He shakes off, together with his friends, every subjection to the authority alien to spirit; he then also denies every relation of command and obedience effective among the powers of the soul; he likewise rejects, in the relation of the person to the divine powers, every form of lordship and subordination, and replaces this

[p. 62]

relation by that of the kinship of men among themselves, with nature and with the divine powers—of love, of harmony, of unity. He wants to feel himself only as part of the divine whole. He hopes for a society in which the existing relations of subjection are replaced by a kinship conditioned from within, by a union of the persons cooperating in society which is grounded in the consciousness of their inner belonging together; precisely in opposition to the legal delimitation of rigid, indissoluble units, to external relations of domination, the Germanic consciousness of law and state asserts itself in him, and he reinterprets the Greek polis in this sense. Each of these traits of his lived experience can be documented by corresponding traits in Hölderlin. Some of them he has in common with Schelling. In a modified form they appear in the whole young generation at that time. Certainly the writers of this generation acted upon one another in manifold ways, but the kinship in the same lived experience is conditioned above all by the fact that this sprang in like manner from the historical movement. With such lived experiences the opposition too was given in which Hegel and his companions felt themselves toward the Enlightenment. What was contained in the German Enlightenment as the setting in opposition of this world and the beyond, of God and world, of freedom and nature, as the struggle of the understanding with imagination and heart, as the opposition of reason and positive determinations, they had to strive to overcome: in poetry this overcoming had been accomplished; philosophical thinking had to show itself equal to poetry.

How could one now fully express this relation between the philosophical genius, the literary environment that acted upon it, the historical situation under which it stood! Personal lived experience, external conditions, literary relations are by no means factors separate from one another. Hegel’s genius received, in the interworking of these moments, the form, common to him with kindred thinkers and poets, of comporting oneself toward everything. It could be furthered in this to the strongest degree by the slightest personal or literary contact, because it itself and its time formed a historical unity that is not further resolvable: it determined what in the mode of procedure of his mind he had in common with his companions.

[p. 63]

And in what seemed to constitute Hegel’s personal genius, too, particular conditions of his situation, influences from without, peculiarities of his stock and individual endowment can be held apart only very imperfectly. He preserved the firmly fixed habits and maxims of life of his Swabian homeland and his father’s house. He worked without haste and in a tenacious, long-winded continuity of thinking. Ponderous, with a sober worldly understanding, bound to reliable friends, he slowly widened, in a quiet conduct of life, the circles of his activity. Thus he kept his own existence untouched by the problematic of the ethical world that moved his thinking and that disturbed or shattered the existence of so many of his fellow strivers. For he had, to a degree that before him only Kant had, the philosophical capacity to bring to consciousness his most personal lived experiences, as well as the movements of the time in which he took part, at the same time detached from himself, as a general state of affairs. If his lived experiences, like those of his poet companion, possessed a peculiar depth in the relation of every present to memory, in the holding together of consciousness, in the continued working of the present thus arising, to the point of suffering from memory, there nevertheless accrued to him from this also a peculiar power of metaphysical generalization, of elevation above the personal of the moment. As a genuine Swabian he was endowed with a strong political sense; his interests spread over all domains of the spiritual world; once more the universality of the philosophers of the earlier time asserted itself in him. In the power of expressing the general states of affairs in concepts he was akin to Kant, and his original interest in the forms of thinking fed on the persistent study of Kant.

Above all, however, there was joined in him with the metaphysical genius that of the historian. In this his mode of working differed from the beginning from that of his fellow strivers; for even if the historical knowledge of Friedrich Schlegel was more comprehensive and more methodical, this latter lacked purity, strength and steadiness in grasping the general state of affairs in concepts. Here Hegel’s nature asserted itself in the way that he strove here too toward impersonal, objective and universal apprehension. He put into all that happens the depth of his experience and yet comported himself withal quite objectively. While he re-felt the great historical figures of consciousness down to the last

[p. 64]

depths, he was able to comprehend within himself, in the connection of them all, the wealth of the historical world. The means of such comprehension became for him the necessary and universal connection of consciousness, as transcendental philosophy presented it. Having found in it, following Schelling, the key to the understanding of the universe, he applied it also to the conceiving of the realm of history. The same relations of concepts in which he developed the connection of spirit he found again in the connection of the universe and in that of history. The identity of this threefold connection became for Hegel, as soon as he found himself in this period, the foundation of his thinking. And he learned already from history to grasp this connection as development. With this the direction of his system was given. Where he sees development, he will have to derive it from the relation of the concepts, which is the same in all three regions of his experience. And so there opens to him the possibility of finding an objective criterion for the heightening of value in the region of subjective spirit, of the universe and of history: what was realized on an earlier stage is preserved in the following one and at the same time raised into a structure that contains a More. The problem becomes for him thus that of a new, higher logic.

All this lay in Hegel’s genius and had to emerge consistently as soon as Schelling’s essay of 1795 had led him to the pantheism of the absolute I. Forcefully, boldly, swiftly Schelling had taken this step before him; through him the direction of Hegel’s pantheism was determined; that Schelling’s subsequent writings passed by him without a trace is not to be expected. The fact of a continuing influence of Schelling can be established from the comparison of the manuscripts with the literary dates, and at least the extent within which it is possible can be circumscribed. The criticism which Schelling’s “Treatises for the Elucidation of the Wissenschaftslehre” directed at the prevailing Kantianism, which abolished knowledge by tearing subject and thing in itself asunder, has without doubt been adopted by Hegel. When Schelling in the year 1797 declared nature to be visible spirit, that too was Hegel’s doctrine, and Hegel too had no need of a theory of knowledge, since in this selfsameness of the connection in subject and object the problem of knowledge seemed

[p. 65]

solved. The concept by which Schelling then in 1798, in the essay “On the World-Soul,” expresses this ideal connection of the universe is the dominant concept of Hegel in this whole period up to the autumn of 1800—the concept of life. “Life,” says Schelling, “is common to all living individuals; what distinguishes them from one another is only the manner of life.” “The universal principle of life individualizes itself in every single living being.” “The essential of all things (which are not mere appearances but approach, in an infinite series of stages, individuality) is life.” Such propositions recur almost literally in many places in the manuscripts. And as Schelling in his essay on the World-Soul determines universal life more closely as organism and defines this, with Kant, by the relation of the whole to its parts—exactly so do we find it again in Hegel; only that in him—an important difference—this concept of a world-totality, in its logical opposition to the relations in which the understanding thinks, becomes the center of his whole world of thought and maintains this significance. One thinks one can grasp his dependence when, at the beginning of the manuscript on the Gospel of John, he puts in the revision, in place of pure self-consciousness, which is timeless, unlimited and is limited only in the single individuals, the concept of pure life, as if he were going on from Fichte and the earlier Schelling to that of 1798; but such certainty diminishes again when one considers that the first volume of the Hyperion is wholly permeated by the concepts of life, of the whole, of the One that articulates itself in the manifold, of the organization of nature. There are further strong agreements between Schelling and Hegel in the closer determinations of this universal life, such as unity, opposition, manifoldness, union, reflection into itself; but these too could result of themselves, without Schelling’s influence, in the progress from Fichte to pantheism.

With this there is in any case indicated what of important components of Hegel’s metaphysics of that time can be pointed out in Schelling, and what from Schelling’s doctrines of that time can be found again in Hegel. On the other hand, the further, so effective conceptions of Schelling, in which he grounded the doctrine of the development of the universe, cannot yet be demonstrated in Hegel’s manuscripts of this epoch. This doctrine

[p. 66]

had to arise consistently as soon as the proposition of the selfsameness of the spiritual connection and of the connection in nature was linked with the doctrine of evolution that had been developed, on the ground of geology and paleontology, by Buffon, Daubenton, Kant, Herder. As soon as this happened, the concept of evolution in nature, first thought through scientifically by Descartes, had to convert itself into that of its development. And under the presuppositions of the new pantheism there followed then further, with inner necessity, the doctrine which Schelling always regarded as his very own invention—the conception of world-development as a progress from the objective through the stages of nature to the subjective. But this decisive advance of Schelling to the developmental-historical conception of nature is taken into account at no point in Hegel’s manuscripts of the time. It must not be concluded from this that he had rejected this doctrine at that time, for soon afterward it appears in him, with some qualifications; but at this time there is found in the manuscripts the application of the principle of development prepared only in the domain of history, and even here this principle is not expressed in conceptual clarity.

This is the historical connection that had to be sent ahead for the understanding of this epoch of Hegel. His work, however, was even now a theological one; the forces that had determined him since his university years remained effective in him even now, except that the new movement into which he entered modified them. Before we consider the fragments themselves, we must therefore once more call to mind the particular conditions under which his theological views developed, and the shape that they had assumed at this stage of his thinking.

#### Foundations of the Theological-Historical Work.

With tenacious energy Hegel, even when the overall direction of his mind changed, put the great ideas that he had taken into himself in relation to it.

Kant’s influence continued, only that its effect was now grounded on other moments in him. He now set himself against Kant’s moral law. He no longer found in it the kernel of Christianity.

[p. 67]

And as it was at all times the character of his polemic to conceive the philosophical writers against whom he turned as the representatives of a general direction of spirit, indeed as an embodiment of moments that ultimately lay in the essence of spirit itself, as his polemic received from this identification of a man with a standpoint, of a standpoint with a category of spirit itself, at once its greatness and its hard, personal character, so there runs through all the works of this period a struggle against Kant, in which the latter becomes for him the embodiment of the separating nature of the understanding, of the boundless, never satisfied striving of reason, of separation in thinking, and of transcendence in the worldview.

His whole theological work is accomplished in the confrontation with Kant’s Religion within the Limits of Mere Reason. Past is his subordination to the identification of Christianity, accomplished in this writing, with the autonomous religion of reason grounded on the moral consciousness. The opposition of Judaism and Christianity is no longer conceived as that of a legality that stands under the dominion of external authority and of an ethical life grounded in conscience. But Hegel combats only the result of Kant’s interpretation of religion; the procedure itself which the latter had applied therein he grasps more deeply than before, and the beginnings contained in Kant of an investigation of the formation of religious representations are also developed further by him. What he now rejected in Kant’s writing was its mortal part, in which it summed up the German Enlightenment. For this too had referred the religion of Jesus to moral improvement: Semler too had distinguished between private (moral) religion and public (statutory) religion; with a tenacious uniformity that in the end bored even that century he had pursued this distinction: how much had been removed from this standpoint as an opinion of the age not serviceable to morals, as accommodation! So Kant’s principle, that the historical system must absolutely be reduced to the moral system of reason, and only insofar as it is reducible to this has it a right to existence, was nonetheless only the completed consequence of this standpoint. Whoever is familiar with the theological writings of that time recognizes the joints of the various Enlightenment ideas combined in him. But the content of his writing is thereby designated only from one side; it signifies at the same time a decisive

[p. 68]

turning point in the determination of the Christianity contained in the biblical writings. According to Kant, fact, dogma, article of faith are, as such, nothing; they are something only insofar as the moral religious idea appears in them; since this principle is taken with the most rigorous seriousness, the content of the Bible too can have its value only in this relation, and so it is now the business of the teacher of religion to refer every passage to it; it must receive this relation. But in that this happens, in that the mightiest spirit since Leibniz gathers the ideal content of Scripture into a unity, there is shown in it here for the first time since the Reformation a unitary basic view: the biblical theology of the idealism of freedom, on the basis of the opposition of radical evil and the holiness of the moral law; for the first time the whole of Scripture is again explained from the spirit that pervades this whole. This direction of exegesis, which grasps Scripture as an organic whole grown from a unitary substance, takes its place as an equal beside the philological treatment of the individual writings.

The basic moral idea of Christianity contained in the New Testament is, “that man is called by the moral law to a good conduct of life; that through the indelible respect for it that lies within him he also finds in himself a promise of confidence toward this good spirit and of hope to be able, however it may come about, to satisfy it; and finally that, holding this latter expectation together with the strict commandment of the first, he must, as summoned before a judge to give account, constantly examine himself; reason, heart and conscience at once instruct him about this and drive him to it. It is immodest to demand that still more be disclosed to us, and if this should have happened, he would not have to count it among the universal human needs.” “Now, however, the positive religions exist; they contain far more than the religion of reason. Philosophical interpretation does not dispute the possibility or actuality of their objects, but it cannot take them up into its maxims of thinking and acting, as indeed it cannot take up anything supernatural at all.” When we therefore ascribe to God self-giving love, self-sacrifice, this is “the schematism of analogy, which we cannot do without; but to transform it into a schematism of the determination of the object is anthropomorphism, which in moral intention is of the most detrimental

[p. 69]

consequences.” With this Kant explains the “visible representation (schema) of a God on earth.” In this way, further, according to a psychological law, there arises the religious transformation of the intelligible moral relation of an opposition of the good and evil dispositions in man into the opposition of two kingdoms, of the good and the evil. “Holy Scripture presents this intelligible moral relation in the form of a history. Christ becomes the personification of the good principle, or of humanity in its moral perfection.” In the representations of heaven and hell the complete dissimilarity of the principles of good and evil is presented. In the representation of the evil spirit the unfathomability of radical evil becomes intuitable.

It is evident that such an interpretation had to lead, in its course, onto entirely new paths. Kant agreed with Semler in the exclusive valuation of the moral element in Christianity; but whereas Semler derived the other components of the New Testament from the opinions of the time, and thus set them down incoherently beside this basic element, with Kant it becomes the center of a coherent explanation of the whole of Scripture: for those components alien to him are now derived from certain forms, indwelling in the spirit, of representing moral ideas, and the origin of the dogmatic content of Scripture is explained by the fact that the basic moral ideas have taken on, in representation, the form of powers and persons existing outside man. A remarkable example of this is how for him the Trinity becomes the symbol of faith of the whole pure religion: in it, according to him, the threefold moral quality of the ruler of the world finds expression. One need only bring these scattered and rather receding discussions into the foreground in order to recognize in this essay the foundation of all mythical treatment of the Bible, as indeed Lorenz Bauer, the first to place the whole Old Testament under the viewpoint of myth, proceeded at once from Eichhorn, from Heyne’s mythological investigations and from Kant’s philosophical treatment of Scripture. That the mythical conception, however, is still so little emphasized in Kant’s system of the history of religion had various reasons. The historical view of the Enlightenment, concerning the contingency of changeable dogmas, lies in Kant’s essay in

[p. 70]

strange conflict with the new thought of the necessary connection between the moral worldview of Christianity and its dogmas. And for Kant no historical connection leads from Judaism to the form of Christian religiosity. Only outward accommodation to Jewish representations explains to him their penetration into Christian thought. It is precisely here that Hegel begins. He applies the same procedure of a unitary interpretation of Scripture that he found in Kant; he too pursues the process in which an inner world of the heart converts itself into representations of faith, but this conversion now becomes for him a historical problem. The progress from a spiritual constitution to a world of faith and from it to a positive religiosity, as a historical question, the task of finding the connection that explains this course of the history of religion—that is Hegel’s problem. Kant had not been able to solve it; now, through the means of the new historical understanding, it was in Hegel carried a good distance toward its solution. Fichte’s Critique of All Revelation is mentioned in a manuscript of Hegel’s, and one notices that the determinations of its introduction on religion and theology occupied him; but neither this immature work nor the odd anonymous essay on revelation and mythology that appeared in 1799 advanced such a science of religion in any way. Schelling’s fruitful youthful work, which Hegel of course knew, did discuss the philosophical myths but did not touch Christianity. Hegel is the middle link that leads over from Semler and Kant to Baur, Strauss, Zeller and Schwegler.

A second moment, which had determined Hegel from his student days on and which retained an equal influence on his works in the history of religion, lay in his veneration of Hellenism. Conditioned by the new humanism, it nevertheless rested on Hegel’s inner kinship with the Greek spirit. Thus Greek life and Greek religiosity become for him the standard by which he measures every period of the Jewish-Christian development. In the plan of his work only the presentation of the period of decline of Greek religiosity was included: its history, its high point, its true essence had no place in it. For Hegel derived the origin of Christian piety and of its dogmas exclusively from states of the heart and concepts that ran their course wholly within the Jewish world. But in his

[p. 71]

view of the general history of religion Greek religiosity occupied a significant place. Standing at a stage parallel to the Jewish, it was for him the type of a happier, more normal development than that. And when it was a matter of recognizing and overcoming the limits of the Christian religiosity hitherto, the view of the height of Greek life likewise contained an indication for this. Thus the reference to the Greek character runs through the whole of Hegel’s historical presentation. But in particular his history of Jewish religion becomes truly intelligible only when one keeps present to mind the ideal image of Hellenism as it lived in him.

In a hymn whose style and echoes of Wieland and Herder point to Hegel’s early period, I find his first presentation of the Greek character. “Ah! from the distant days of the past there shines toward the soul that has a feeling for human beauty and greatness an image—the image of a genius of the peoples, a son of fortune, of freedom, and a ward of beautiful imagination; him too the brazen bond of needs fettered to mother earth, but he has so worked it through his feeling, through his imagination, so refined it, beautified it, wreathed it with roses with the help of the Graces, that he takes pleasure in these fetters as in his work, as in a part of himself, so that it seemed to be entirely his work. His servants were joy, cheerfulness, grace, his soul filled with the consciousness of their power and their freedom.” This presentation receives its complement in a characterization of Socrates, which likewise stems from an earlier time. Socrates is for him the embodiment of the Greek spirit in the philosophical field. Hegel sets him in opposition to the Christian doctrines, and from the experiences of his Tübingen years this opposition receives the utmost sharpness and bitterness. Socrates formed “not heroes in martyrdom and suffering, but in acting and living.” He attached himself in everything to the civic community of his native city. For him there was no need of a resurrection and of a mediatorship in order to lead man into himself, and he never overstepped the nexus contained in human consciousness when he spoke of the highest things.

More developed, the characterization of the Greek spirit then meets us in the fragment on the decline of the Greek religion of imagination

[p. 72]

The Greeks formed themselves in unity with nature; in the continuity of their development they remained in connection with their beginnings; their existence ran its course in free commonwealths, and these, by their small size, as Hegel explains on another occasion, secured to the individual the constant consciousness of his belonging to the whole. Thus the unique beauty of their existence became possible, which they show at the high point of their development. They lived at that time in the unconstrained unity of body and soul, God and nature, nature and man. Their Fatum lay within nature itself, and the relation to it was free resignation, which subordinates itself to the connection of divine and human things. One recalls here a passage in Hegel’s Bern diary in which he depicts the childlike disposition of the Swiss pastoral peoples. “The inhabitants of these regions live in the feeling of their dependence on the power of nature, and this gives them a calm submission to its destructive outbreaks”; thus their closer relation to the unreflected unity of life shows itself everywhere. In the poem Eleusis, too, Hegel had spoken of the “genius of innocence” in the old Greek faith.

Into this connection there now fit the passages of the history of Jewish religion, written down in our epoch, that deal with Greek piety. Taken together, they express the structure of Greek life from its outward existence to its religiosity. Hegel now seeks, going beyond that earlier characterization, to grasp the structure of the Greek spirit through a relation of concepts. Opposition to the other nations, to nature, indeed within itself—that is the basic concept under which he places the Jewish development: unity within itself, with nature and with the historical world is the ruling idea that makes Greek existence comprehensible to him. In his way of seeing historically, he proceeds from the political conditions of life of the Greeks. He has pointed out elsewhere how, according to their geographical position, free and in accord with nature, conscious of their strength, self-governing in small states, and thus in the most intimate association, they were able to develop: from this there sprang for him, as the basic properties of Greek existence, the unbroken natural development, unshifted by any external force, civic freedom, and an extraordinary strength of the

[p. 73]
