union of individuals in the state. In all these traits Greek development is the counterpart of the Jewish. Cadmus and Danaus sought a life “in beautiful unions” in foreign parts, because it was “no longer granted them in their own land,” and wherever they came they entered into an inner relation with the natives. Cato, Cleomenes and others took their lives after the destruction of the free constitution of their fatherland, because it was no longer possible for them to work for the idea they had grasped. Property and political rights were regulated in the Greek states from purely political points of view. The sphere of action in the state gave the Greek citizens an impulse to activity, courage and happiness. These are the conditions out of which Greek religiosity grew. For religiosity is the complement of the human-civic condition of a nation and yet at the same time conditioned by it in the representation of divine things. The Jewish religion and its God arose from the oppositions in the spiritual constitution of the Jews, from dissatisfaction with their political existence, from the striving springing from it to seek the strength they needed in a transcendent God, the community that was lacking to them, the unity of being that they had lost, in the foreignness of the beyond: unity with nature, energy and wholeness of existence, and satisfaction in civic community was the point of departure for Greek piety and its gods. “If the infinite object is everything, then man is nothing”—in contrast to this the earlier fragment had already shown that for the Greeks the gods were only a complement of their vigorous civic existence, and from this Hegel explains why, after the political decline, they could no longer satisfy; the positive qualities of Greek religiosity too he derives from the conditions of their life. Satisfaction, happiness, beauty of existence are the foundation of the piety of the Greeks. Thus they are the imperishable model for the solution of the task of a folk religion. Their religion “accompanies the people amicably everywhere, in their business and the serious affairs of life as in their festivals and joys it stands at their side”—not obtrusive and schoolmasterly, but humanly familiar. “The folk festivals of the Greeks were probably all religious festivals—in honor of a god, or of a man who had deserved well of their state and was therefore deified. —Everything, even the excesses

[p. 74]

of the Bacchants, were consecrated to a god, even their public spectacles had a religious origin—which they never disowned in their further development.” Their gods were not, like the God of the Jews, the absolute power into which their impotence had taken flight, but a complement of the beauty, strength and unity of their existence at its individual points. “Abraham had to have a God for himself who guided and led him, not a Greek god, a play with nature, to whom he gives thanks for particular things, but a God who grants him security against it, who protects him, who is the lord of his whole life.” Hegel infers yet another religious difference from the diversity of life. The relation of a people to other peoples is mirrored in its relation to their gods. “Therefore Abraham’s God is essentially different from the Lares and national gods; a family that venerates its Lares, a nation that venerates its national god, has indeed also isolated itself, has divided the One and excluded the others from its part, but it admits other parts at the same time, it grants the others equal rights with itself and recognizes the Lares and gods of the others as Lares and gods.” The most thoroughgoing difference, however, lies between the transcendence of the Jewish God, his foreignness to the relations of nature, and the kinship of the Greek gods with the workings of the forces of nature and above all with human beings. This equality of human and divine life finds its deepest expression in the mysteries. In the Jewish faith “the secret was something altogether alien, into which no man was initiated, on which he could only depend”; and the concealment of the God in the Holy of Holies has an entirely different sense from the secret of the Eleusinian gods. From the feelings of rapture and devotion at Eleusis, from these revelations of the god, no one was excluded, but they were not to be spoken of, for they were profaned by words; “of the things and actions and the laws of their service the Israelites might well chatter, for there is nothing holy in them; the holy was eternally outside them, unseen and unfelt.”

If Hegel thus pursued the idea of a complete transformation of religious community, if his criticism of what existed unhesitatingly pronounced the sharpest word and the last consequence, this would not have been possible if even now, during these studies, he had not still stood under

[p. 75]

the influence of the French Revolution, to which as a student he had already surrendered himself with enthusiasm. He was the most attentive spectator of all the phases it ran through and of the shocks it produced at that time in the European system of states. The opening of his essay on the internal conditions of Württemberg states bluntly that the collapse of the orders of state around him was unavoidable if one did not resolutely give up what was untenable and satisfy the needs of the time. It was precisely in this sense that he wrote his theological works as well.

#### The Theological-Historical Fragments.

So let these fragments now themselves confront the reader. Hegel has written nothing more beautiful. Long and persistently I have contemplated them. In them the whole historical genius of Hegel is revealed in its first freshness and still free of the fetters of the system. In the form in which they lie before us, unfinished, in a whole series of stages of development that lie one upon another like strata, beginnings, expansions, revisions, the sentences and words often struck through two and three times and rewritten, they are a unique document of philosophical thought-formation. Of Hegel’s other manuscripts, too, none gives such a picture of his brooding, probing thinking, always setting out anew. And these notes do not represent an elaboration for the public. Hegel never thought of having them printed as we have them. They are materials, arisen in the course of his thinking, the individual piece at times independent of the whole, where he merely follows the drift of his thoughts, losing himself in ever deeper digging, intent only on becoming clear and on following the ever further-reaching consequence of his new view; later he would insert such sheets at a suitable place. For these fragments do form a whole, laborious as it is—especially given the arbitrary order in which they have been bound together—to see their connection. A veritable thicket of cross-references binds the individual pieces to one another. From a note of Hegel’s on the last page of the concluding fragment one may conjecture that for him the draft was once completely closed in 24 sheets. His work no longer lies before us so,

[p. 76]

and in several places gaps yawn. If it has therefore not everywhere proved possible to bring the corresponding members together, the order as Hegel planned it is nonetheless clear. A developmental history of Jewish religiosity, from its beginning in Abraham to its complete dissolution, is followed by an account of the presuppositions of Jesus’s appearance and of his new standpoint. First his opposition to the positivity of the Jews, as it emerges in the discourses, above all in the Sermon on the Mount; then, rising higher, the dissolution of the representation of punitive justice through the nexus of life that opens up in fate, and his new ideal of love. The altered consciousness of the relation of man to God and to nature, in which the new ethical life is grounded, then explains the religion of Jesus and the concepts in which it is expressed: Logos, Son of God, Son of Man, spirit and Kingdom of God. But as his teaching and his work are subject to historical conditions that set limits to the pure development of his religiosity, there arises his fate, which intensifies in the community and produces in it the form of the Christian religiosity of the community.

The justification of this arrangement from the state of the manuscripts will be given later in detail; our division of the connected work into separate chapters was conditioned not only by the fact that the individual parts no longer everywhere come together, but by the fact that they are in themselves such “wholes of far-radiating meaning,” containing history, metaphysics and ideal so interwoven, that only an exposition which, within the connection, pursues the intrinsic value and the diverse relations of the individual pieces is able to exhaust their profound content.

#### 1. History of Jewish Religiosity.

According to Hegel’s plan, his work was to begin with the history of Jewish religiosity, for the presentation of Christianity required, for its foundation, its opposition to the Jewish law, and in order to understand the whole content and scope of this standpoint Hegel was led ever deeper into the study of Judaism. Three layers of his work can still be distinguished in the manuscripts: a first draft, its revision, and finally, in the margin of it,

[p. 77]

written, a third version that forms an almost complete connection. For the supplementation of the biblical writings he used Josephus. On the occasion of the exodus of the Israelites from Egypt he pronounces his method of regarding tradition. “How we might grasp this event of the liberation of the Israelites with our understanding—of that, as with the foregoing, there can be no question here at all, but as it was present in the imagination and in the remembering life of the Jews, so their spirit acted in it.” His conception of the religiosity contained in the Old Testament rests on a significant critical-historical insight, through which he set himself in opposition to Herder’s conception: the Jewish religion, as it lies before us, is not the expression of a childlike people, but there lies in it the end of a long development. Thus he says on one occasion: “One cannot call the state of Jewish culture a state of childhood and its language an undeveloped childlike language; some deep childlike sounds are still preserved in it, or rather have been restored, but the remaining heavy, constrained way of expressing itself is rather a consequence of the supreme malformation of the people.”

With Abraham the history of the Jewish people begins for him, for “his spirit is the unity, the soul, that governed all the fates of his posterity; he appears in different shapes, according as he fought against different forces, or, if he succumbed to force or seduction, defiled himself by taking in an alien being; thus in different form of armor and of strife, or of the way in which he bears the fetters of the stronger, which form is called fate.” But already in him Jewish religiosity appears quite remote from the stage of naive consciousness that finds itself in unity with nature. Hegel expressly assumes such a first state, since he speaks of the loss of the state of nature. If in the totality of the actual—so we must interpret this—the parts are bound together in the whole, then this unity is mirrored, as it were, in the first state of the human race. He follows the “few dark traces” that have been preserved of the course of the human race up to Abraham. A first moment of separation he finds in the Flood. In that man here experiences nature as something hostile, his faith in it is destroyed, and in its place steps “the most monstrous

[p. 78]

unbelief.” “For there is nothing more revolting to a purely attuned man than the sight of a man—whether justly or unjustly—killed by physical superior force, against which he can have no stirring of defense.” Now between those thus separated only the relation of ruler and ruled is still possible. And as this can be attained in two ways, “in a thought or in an actual”, so Hegel constructs a twofold development from the Flood onward. Noah restored the unity of the torn world in a transcendent relation of lordship, in the ideal of God conceived in thought and in his legislation; Nimrod put himself in a state of defense against nature, subjected the animals to the law of the stronger, held men together by force, and even rose up against God in order to ward off a new flood. Neither by the one nor by the other were the alien powers that confronted the Jewish spirit as fate reconciled. Already here the opposition between the Jewish and the Greek development enters Hegel’s presentation; he recalls Deucalion and Pyrrha, who made the peace of love with nature and so became the progenitors of fairer nations.

As the perfected type of the Jewish spirit in its lovelessness and estrangement from nature there then appears to him, as already said, Abraham, to whom he now turns at once in a detailed history and characterization. As the latter had torn himself away without ground from his fatherland and his family, so separation and opposition henceforth governed his life. Again Hegel sets the Jew beside the Greek: when Cadmus and Danaus left their homeland, “they sought out a soil where they would be free, in order to be able to love,” “Abraham did not want to love.” To the soil on which he lived the nomad gained no relation. He went through men as a stranger; where he came upon them, he used them without befriending them. And as for Hegel religion is always only the expression of a human disposition of the heart, and in it actual life is supplemented to totality, so he derives from such traits in the life of Abraham his belief in God. Abraham needs a ruler, a helper, a being beyond, at the thought of whom he feels himself raised above the poverty of his this-worldly existence. “This looking out beyond the present, this reflection

[p. 79]

upon a whole of existence characterizes the life of Abraham, and the image of this in the mirror is his Godhead, who guides his steps and actions, who makes him promises for the future, presents to him his whole realized, whom he, thinking the future, sees in sacred groves, and to whom, in faith in the whole, he sacrifices every single thing. The only love that he felt caused him scruples, which in a moment could go so far that he felt ready to destroy even this.” The God of Abraham is the lord of the nascent Jewish people; this has its unity in him, its powerlessness finds its complement in the power of God, and this God behaves toward the other peoples just as alien and hostile as Abraham himself. Here Hegel points to the Lares and national gods of antiquity.

From Abraham Jewish history leads to a further stage of religiosity in the legislation of Moses. The estrangement from men as from nature grows, the poverty of life, the helplessness amid the powers that surround the people, and so the need for complement must at the same time bring forth a heightened energy of transcendence and power of God. The exodus from Egypt was only the work of the Jewish God and of Moses; the people behaved passively, and this exodus is not accomplished in heroic deeds; great things are done not by the Jews but for them, in their imagination, by their God, who lets Egypt suffer for them. The relation of this people to Moses himself did not rest on his being the bringer of their freedom, but on his having “worked some tricks as wonders before them.” Toward the Egyptians it had only relations of deceit. On this stage too Hegel contrasts the spiritual constitution of the Jews with that of the Greeks. The Greeks live in the consciousness of the divine content of nature and of the state, and so beauty, life, love and happiness prevail among them; through the life of the Jews runs infinite separation and opposition, passivity of the mass, unhappiness. And again he shows how from this condition Jewish religiosity and its opposition to the Greek grows out. The faith of the Jews is “a religion out of unhappiness and for unhappiness.” It is a faith in the alien helpful power. If the infinite object is everything, then man is nothing, he is “only through the grace of that.” This faith cannot do enough in giving the invisible remoteness of this God

[p. 80]

expression, for everything visible becomes, as such, something limited. Thus Moses could show his God to the people only in the symbol of the indeterminate, formless fire. Here a very remarkable passage is found. Hegel offers the sharpest opposition to the customary high esteem for Jewish monotheism in contrast to the Greek belief in gods; to the litanies of the Jews on the invisibility of their Godhead he opposes the Greek deification of actuality in the intuition of love, of enjoyment, of beauty as a higher form of life. The Jews’ sad faith in God then in turn exerts a pressure backward on their life. Over against this God man is insignificant; if the infinite subject is All, then his people is in itself empty of content, void and without life. It is something only so far as the Godhead makes it so—something made, which has no right and no love for itself and for which only spiritual dependence and an animal existence remain; everything ideal, free, beautiful is banished from life as something unreal. Hegel has finally grasped this relation under the logical relation of antithesis and synthesis; the antitheses are the Jewish people on the one hand and the whole remaining human race on the other; the synthesis of the two is the infinite object, the sum of all truth and of all relation, which is therefore really the infinite subject.

And now Hegel derives from this Jewish religiosity the general principle of the Mosaic legislation. “This whole legislation flows from the idea: God is the Lord, all your doing is either his service or your enjoyment, which he has permitted you. Accordingly the Jews in this legislation are nothing but citizens of a state; each receives a worth only through his relation to God, and accordingly their actions must be brought into relation to religion as far as possible: the state something alien outside the men.” A whole series of provisions contained in the legislation of Moses are treated from this point of view. Only a few especially characteristic moments shall be singled out. The system of purifications must here likewise be determined by the relation to the Godhead; it does not rest on the unconstrained purity of the heart, and with all its prescriptions it attains only the purity of corruptedness. “The body, which did not properly belong to them and was only lent, must be kept clean, as the servant has to keep clean the livery that his master gives him.” The sacrifice can in this legislation

[p. 81]

only mean that man has no property of his own, but receives and possesses it only from the grace of his God. The priests form the household servants of this God. How Hegel opposes the Eleusinian gods to the secret of the Holy of Holies has already been mentioned above. The complete Sabbath rest as well, which left one day a week passive and empty, seems to Hegel characteristic of the religion of a people “to whom the sad, unfelt unity is the highest.” Likewise it follows from the Jewish relation of man to God that for Moses God does not make himself known in truths that express his essence, but only in commands that proceed from his hidden will. “That on which one is dependent cannot have the form of a truth, for truth is beauty represented with the understanding; the negative character of truth is freedom.”

The next stage of the history of Jewish religion, which Hegel describes in detail, leads up to the age of Jesus. The historical line that he draws to this age runs through the following remarkable passages. The Solomonic period appears to him, in contrast to the prevailing judgment, as a worse period when regarded politically, as a happier and more perfect one when regarded religiously. The Jews united with the foreigners, indeed with nature itself, by appropriating the cult of the neighboring peoples. “More human feelings rose in their hearts, and with them friendlier relations came forth; they divined fairer spirits and served foreign gods.” But the form of their religious life transformed the form of amity here too into the relation of servitude. The decline and fall of their independence was then the point of departure of the transformation that led over to the religion of Christ. “The pressure awakened hatred again, and with it their God awoke again.” Yet the strength of the people now turned inward, upon itself. From time to time, to be sure, the inspired tried to hold fast the old genius. “But the Jewish prophets kindled their flame at the torch of an exhausted demon.” The time of imagination, of the theophanies, was past; the people stood on different levels of reflection. Thus there formed sects, opinions, parties. “But because this activity within man himself and upon himself, this inner life, does not, like the interest of a great citizen, have its object outside itself and is able to show it at the same time,” so it could only in

[p. 82]

externality present itself, which pointed to life without being able to give it shape. “To flee this dreadful actuality, men sought consolation in ideas; the common Jew in the hope of a coming Messiah, the Pharisee in the busy activity of service and the doing of the present objective, the Sadducees in the whole manifoldness of their existence of a changeable being-there, the Essenes in something eternal, in a brotherhood that would exclude all dividing property and make of it one living One without manifoldness.” “The hope of the Romans that the fanaticism would soften under their moderate rule failed. It flared up once more and buried itself under its own destruction.”

Thus ends “the great tragedy of the Jewish people”; it is for Hegel “no Greek one”; “it can arouse neither fear nor pity, for both spring only from the fate of the necessary misstep of a beautiful being. It can arouse only abhorrence.” In harsh words he sums up his whole presentation in the concluding sentence: “The fate of the Jewish people is the fate of Macbeth, who stepped out of nature itself, attached himself to alien beings, and so, in their service, had to trample down and murder everything holy in human nature, to be finally forsaken by his gods (for they were objects, he was a slave) and to be shattered by his own faith.”

#### 2. The Basic Fragment.

To this history of Judaism up to the time of Jesus there now attaches a fragment that presents the upheaval which led over from the Jewish consciousness to that of Christ. The first words, “At the time when Jesus appeared among the Jewish nation,” show that the beginning of the history of Jesus lies before us here; for five pages a flowing presentation runs on: its object is the proof of the necessity of this upheaval. Whenever the spirit has come into contradiction with the outward orders of life, these must dissolve. That was the situation at the time of Christ. It was conditioned by the constant increase of the rent condition, opposition and disorder, as Hegel had shown it in the history of Jewish society.

From this situation Hegel now understands the origin of the idea of the Messiah, the necessity of the appearance of Jesus, his unavoidable

[p. 83]

fate and the sectarian character of his community. “Thus the Jewish people at the time of Jesus no longer gives us the image of a whole; a universal still holds it together barely, but there is so much alien and manifold material, so many kinds of life and ideals present, so much unsatisfied striving, looking about curiously for something new, that every reformer who comes forward with confidence and hopes may hold himself as sure of a following as of a hostile party. The external independence of the Jewish state was lost; the Romans and the kings tolerated or given by the Romans therefore united fairly well upon themselves the general secret hatred of the Jews; the demand for independence lay too deep in their religion, which scarcely granted other peoples their coexistence alongside it; how should it find the rule of one of them over its children bearable? The people, whose other actuality remained as yet unimpaired, was not yet at the point of having to will the sacrifice of this, and therefore waited for a foreign Messiah equipped with power, who would do for it what it did not itself dare, or would inspire it to the daring and carry it along by this force. Many distinguished themselves by stricter, more exact observance of all religious punctilios, and the very fact that they distinguished themselves thereby shows us the loss of unconstrainedness, the effort and a struggle to achieve something that did not proceed from itself. The service in which they stood was the service of a blind Fatum, not, like the Greek one, lying within nature, and their greater religiosity a constant clinging to and depending on something more manifold, which would relate itself to the One but exclude every other consciousness. The Pharisees strove with exertion to be perfect Jews, and this proves that they knew the possibility of not being so. The Sadducees let their Jewishness subsist in them as an actual thing because it was once there, and were content with little, but it seemed to have no immediate interest for them except insofar as it was once a condition of their further enjoyment; otherwise they and their existence were to themselves the highest law. The Essenes, too, did not enter into struggle with it (the Jewish-actual), but let it lie to one side; for to flee the strife they threw themselves into their uniform way of life. At last one had to appear who attacked Judaism itself directly, but because he found nothing in the Jews that could have helped him to combat it, nothing he could have held fast and with which he might have

[p. 84]

overthrown it, he had to perish and could directly have founded only a sect.”

After these words, having arrived at the point where the upheaval of religious consciousness accomplished in Jesus is to be presented, Hegel begins to bring to consciousness this mightiest event of world history through historical categories in which he sums up the moments of this change. Narrative gives way to the conceptual dissection of this historical revolution. And this dissection leads him beyond his purpose into the ultimate problems in which morality and religion cohere. The smooth and finished presentation gives way to broken, tentative sentences. The manuscript itself becomes uneven. In the margins of the pages schemata appear which sum up the events as relations of concepts. He sketches a schema that articulates the moments of servitude under the law. Then another, which develops the moments of the process of the union of the opposed. Finally one that presents the whole inner relation of morality, love and religion in which the religious upheaval is accomplished. One sees him wrestling with the task of sketching a systematic of concepts that could serve as the foundation of historical understanding and of historical presentation. The cross-references by which he brings the leaves together become ever more entangled. He then goes on to derive also the change in the metaphysical consciousness of Jesus and finally its place in the history of religiosity. I should like to designate these notes as the Basic Fragment, since in it Hegel brings to consciousness the connection of the concepts that then gives his presentation of the religiosity of Jesus its most general foundation.

I shall try to bring out the essential from this Basic Fragment.

Anew Hegel sinks himself into the opposition of Christ to Judaism. A short time before, he had seen in the Kantian law of reason the overcoming of that dominion of external commandments. Now we read the question: “Did he set morality against the law? Morality is, according to Kant, the subjugation of the individual under the universal, the victory of the universal over its opposed individual.” The deed of Christ and true ethical life, however, is: the “elevation of the individual to the universal, the sublation of the two opposed by union”—love.

[p. 85]

And now he attacks the Kantian principle sharply, almost cruelly. Here we have the first formulation of that critique of Kant’s practical philosophy which the profound essay on natural law contains. Schleiermacher and Hegel meet in the passion of the polemic against the mighty one who had dominated their youth.

The moral action, according to Kant, because it is a choice, contains the complete exclusion of something opposed. “The more bound up this excluded thing is, the greater the sacrifice, the separation, the more unhappy the fate.” The commandment of duty has the form of universality. Thus arises the intolerable consequence that its content, a determinate duty, “has the contradiction in itself: of being limited and universal at once, and, for the sake of the form of universality, makes the harshest pretensions on behalf of its one-sidedness.” “Woe to the human relation,” he exclaims, “that is not found precisely in the concept of duty, which, as soon as it is not merely the empty thought of universality but is to present itself in an action, excludes or dominates all other relations.” True “morality is sublation of a separation in life,” that is, in the totality designated by this expression; “the principle of morality is love.”

Let it be permitted to insert here sentences from the manuscript on Jesus’s discourses and Sermon on the Mount which carry this polemic against Kant further. Kantian morality is rooted in a power of man, his reason, the capacity of universality, and because there is self-activity here, the commandments admittedly lose thereby a part of their positivity—it is one’s own power that rules over us. With it one could well have abolished the Jewish “service of an alien.” But a man who wanted to restore man in his wholeness could not possibly take this path, which tears the heart apart; for here too there is opposition, and indeed within the living being itself, opposition of one power against the other. Hegel sees, from this standpoint, in the Kantian respect for duty hardly any advantage any longer over Jewish obedience. “Kant’s practical reason is the capacity of universality, that is, the capacity of excluding; the incentive respect; this excluded thing is subjugated in fear—a disorganization, the exclusion of something still united.” Mockingly he now applies the comparison with the Tungus shamans and the Mogulitzes, which Kant had used in his work on religion

[p. 86]

for the adherent of the statutory church faith, now to the man who obeys Kant’s commandment of duty. The difference is not “that those made themselves slaves, while this one would be free; but that those bear the lord outside themselves, this one, however, bears the lord within himself, but is at the same time his own slave; for the particular, drives, inclinations, pathological love, sensibility or whatever one calls it, the universal is necessarily and eternally something alien, something objective; an indestructible positivity remains.” Again Hegel opposes a unity to this: “the oneness of the whole man.” Indeed, he rejects the commandment altogether. When Jesus set above the law a new commandment, “Love God and thy neighbor,” this turn is a commandment in an entirely different sense from the ought of Kantian duty—it bears this character only because “the living is thought and expressed in the form of the concept, alien to it, whereas the commandment of duty, as a universal, is by its essence a concept.” In the ethics of the religion of Jesus, therefore, the form of commanding is only a manner of expression; in Kant’s morality it is grounded in the essence of ethical life itself. Exalted above all separation that the commandment of duty presupposes, and above all dominion of the concept that announces itself in the ought, is “a being, a modification of life.” Thus Hegel goes beyond Kant to a freedom of spirit that no longer knows any limitation and opposition, and grasps an “ideal of oneness” in which all diremption ceases.

Hegel took up again the critique of Kant that lies before us here in the essay on natural law of the summer of 1802, and the content of this essay was then expanded in the Phenomenology into the presentation and critique of the standpoint of morality; the decisive sentences from these fragments, however, remained always fundamental. The essay on natural law brings out as the valuable thing in Kant’s practical reason that it transfers the Absolute into the will: to practical reason belongs the character of infinity; but it constitutes its limit to have over against itself the finite manifoldness of the empirical drives. The real thus stands alien to the ideal, in rigid opposition. The counter-striving of sensibility against reason, the limitation of sensibility by the autonomy of moral reason, is in fact contained in universal experience; but the isolating of these sides of life is

[p. 87]

nothing less than the abolition of ethical life. For in it the essence of reason is realized as the inner unitary connection of the manifold with the unity, of the empirical with the ideas, of the drives with reason. It corresponds to this that in Kant’s ethical theory the universal formula and the determinate duty cannot be placed in any inner relation to one another. Our fragment expresses this by saying that the determinate duty has the contradiction in itself of being determinate and at the same time universal; the essay says: our interest is to know what ought to be done; every maxim is, according to its matter, something determinate, it excludes other determinacies, but the empty form of universality in the ethical rule cannot enter into any inner relation to a single determinacy. Thus here too the guiding thought of the critique of Kant shows itself to be that the opposition which tears matter and form, maxim and universal formula, the determinate and the universal asunder makes a system of ethical life impossible. From our fragment on, Hegel’s critique of the Kantian moral law always proceeds from two simple propositions. Kant’s strength is the recognition of an autonomous unconditioned in man, which has its highest expression in practical reason. His limit is that he possesses this unconditioned only in the form of the opposition of the One, the universal, to the many and particular.

In three stages the new ethical life now rises before Hegel in our Basic Fragment. The first is that of “disposition, that is, the inclination so to act.” “Inclination is grounded in itself, has its ideal object in itself, not in something alien”—neither in the commandment of an alien lord nor in the moral law of reason. It was set by Jesus over against the joyless obedience of the Jews: that which is precisely the opposite of it, a drive, even a need. Jesus abolished the commandments by grounding them on the nature of man. But this disposition is limited, it is conditioned by its object, by the particular kind of separation that it sublates. It properly rests and acts only when the condition occurs; then it unites. Only in acting is it visible, and even in the action it attains presentation only incompletely. For the action shows always only the effected objective relation of what is present in the action: not the union that is the living. And the union is,

[p. 88]

because it exists only in this action, always single, always something isolated: no more has been united than has happened in this action.

There must therefore be something higher: out of the resting disposition a striving rises to multiply these isolated acts; the need of a whole of union arises: love. It seeks to create the whole in an infinite manifold of actions, to give to the limitedness of the single action, through their multitude, the appearance of the whole, of the infinite. “It ties points together in moments, but the world, and in it man, and its dominion still persists.” Here too, then, there is a barrier. And it conditions the fate of the beautiful soul. The whole fullness of its love does not satisfy it. “It has beautiful moments of enjoyment, but only moments too, and the tears of compassion, of emotion over such a beautiful action, are melancholy over its limitedness,” and its “concealed magnanimity,” which will know of no thanks, is “a shame over the deficiency of the condition.”

One last thing is still needed. Religion knows nothing further even of the limits of this love. In the most living union of man there is still separation—“this is the law of humanity”—, while the “beautiful religion” lives in the “ideal that is completely united,” and “the religious action, the most spiritual, the most beautiful, strives to unite even the separations made necessary by development and presents the union in the ideal as entirely existing, no longer opposed to actuality.” Thus the religious consciousness of the connection of all life in love goes beyond love as a state of life.

The development of spirit presented in this fragment, from the moral consciousness to love and the beautiful soul and from this to religion, contains also the first sketch of the developmental history of the ethical-religious consciousness as it is set forth in the essay on natural law and then in the Phenomenology. Out of opposition, ethical life, which has become aware of its infinity, must advance to reconciliation, to the consciousness of the unconditioned, which includes within itself the connection of the single, the many, the contingent with the one, the universal and the necessary.

From this upheaval in the consciousness of Jesus, which led over from faith in law to the new religiosity, there had to follow, according to

[p. 89]

Hegel, a change in the whole relation of Jesus to God, one might say in his metaphysical consciousness. From the sublation of the objectivity of laws, of heteronomy, there follows, according to him, consistently an immanent nexus of life that binds God, Jesus and his community, the whole totality of existence, in love.

“If man has will himself, he stands in an entirely different relation to God than the merely passive man; two independent wills, two substances there are not, God and man must be one, but man the Son and God the Father; man not independent and subsisting on his own; he is only, insofar as he is opposed, a modification, and therefore the Father also in him.” This was the faith that Jesus demanded of his disciples, that they too be one with him, “a real transubstantiation, a real indwelling of the Father in the Son and of the Son in his pupils; these all not substances, absolutely separated and united only in the general concept, but like a vine and its branches; a living life of the Godhead in them.” This religiosity of life and of love comes to full expression in the words: “God is love, love is God, there is no other Godhead than love. Only what is not divine, what does not love, must have the deity in the idea, outside itself. Whoever cannot believe that God was in Jesus, that he dwells in men, despises men. If love dwells, if God dwells among men, then there can be gods—if not, then he must be spoken of, and no gods are possible. If everything is separated, there is only One ideal.” But the whole, though separated, “must always be there.”

Let it be permitted to append here a passage from another presentation of the transition from the moral consciousness to love, which carries our train of thought further with profundity. Only from this metaphysical consciousness of God in Jesus do the two highest commandments of Christ receive their sense: “‘To love God’ is to feel oneself in the All of life, without limits in the infinite; in this feeling of harmony there is, to be sure, no universality; for in harmony the particular is not conflicting but harmonizing, otherwise there would be no harmony. And ‘love thy neighbor as thyself’ does not mean to love him as much as oneself; for to love oneself is a word without sense; but love him as the one who is you: a feeling of equal life, not more powerful, not weaker.”

[p. 90]

This, then, is the sum and the final yield of this Basic Fragment. Love is in the separated as union, as the dissolution of opposites in unity. Thus the religion of Jesus, as the religion of love, is also the lived experience of the unity of the human with the divine spirit. And this unity is realized not once and singularly in this person of Jesus; rather, in the human spirit the divine manifests itself, and this unity of both is raised to consciousness in the religion of Jesus. With this the metaphysical interpretation of Christianity is pronounced, which makes up the core of Hegel’s philosophy of religion. Christian dogma is, according to it, the symbolic expression of the unity of the divine and the human. And if, according to the philosophy of religion, in the Christian religion the absolute is known as the process in which it distinguishes itself from itself and sublates this distinction, if this immanent relation in God is determined as love—an eternal mystery for sensuous apprehension and the understanding—then this interpretation of the Trinity too is already prepared here.

From this standpoint of the fragment Hegel then already at that time sets himself against enthusiastic mysticism just as much as against the rationalism of his time. Both have at their ground the separation of man from God. The former seeks the sublation of this separation in a supernatural act, and the latter soberly remains within it. “The passivity of the enthusiasts wants to bring about an indwelling of God and of Christ in themselves”: thus it distinguishes itself and the being that governs in the religious process, and so stands under the concept of the dominion of an object alien to the spirit. Hegel stands still further from rationalism. “To make Jesus a mere teacher of men means to take the Godhead out of the world, nature and men”; rationalism fails to recognize that the unity of God and man is nothing supernatural.

Hegel has connected with this fragment, by signs, interpretations of discourses of Christ. How here already he enters more deeply, so to speak, into a metaphysical interpretation of these discourses, in contrast to the moral one of Kant, will be shown by passages of these notes which we shall use in the later presentation of his mystical pantheism. He then brings to bear the concept of Christian freedom, which redeems from the custody of the moral laws and from moral judging. And it is very remarkable how already here everywhere the references to the

[p. 91]

great fragment on fate resonate, so that from the Basic Fragment one is led over into this one.

#### 3. Discourses and the Sermon on the Mount.

The interpretations of individual passages of the discourses, with which the Basic Fragment closed, were the preliminary work for notes which are connected with one another into a presentation of Christ’s teaching activity, and which have their culmination in the grasp of the connection of the Sermon on the Mount and of its sense. Only the most important thoughts from them are to be singled out. Hegel distinguishes the commandments of divine worship, which as such bear a purely positive character, and the moral and civil laws, which are determined by the natural relations of man. He treats first Jesus’ relation to the former, then passes over to Jesus’ conduct toward the civil and moral laws, and that leads him to the Sermon on the Mount, in which Jesus has given the most perfect expression to his position toward them.

His standard for judging the commandments of divine worship of the Jews is, without his speaking directly of the Greeks, Greek cult. As the gods of the Greeks were for him nothing other than a supplement of satisfied communal existence at the points at which a need of union still made itself felt, so he conceives also their cultic acts and sets Jewish worship in opposition to them. “Since religious actions are the most spiritual, the most beautiful, that which strives to unite even the separations made necessary by development and to present the union in the ideal as entirely existing, no longer opposed to actuality—thus to express and confirm it in a doing—, religious actions, if that spirit of beauty is lacking to them, are the emptiest, the most senseless servitude, and above this the satisfaction of the commonest human need is exalted, because in it there lies immediately, after all, the feeling of the preservation of a being, even if an empty one.” With this the justification of Jesus’ conduct toward the Jewish ceremonial and Temple service is given. Even when Hegel discusses his position toward the holy objects and days of the Jews, the Greeks are present to him. “If that in which a people is united is at the same time something shared,

[p. 92]

a property of all,” then is “the violation of the sanctuary at the same time an unjust violation of the right of all.” At this place there follows a bitter sally against Jewish and Christian conduct toward the pious service of another faith. “Pious zeal, which breaks up the temples and altars of a foreign worship, drives away its priests, desecrates common sanctuaries belonging to all. But if a holy thing is uniting all only insofar as all renounce, as all serve, then everyone who separates himself from the others takes back his right in this.” This he will indeed not do on account of slight causes, “only if the whole of the community is an object of contempt, and since Jesus stepped out of the whole existence of his people, there fell away that kind of consideration with which a friend otherwise restricts himself in matters of indifference toward one with whom he is one heart and one soul, and for the sake of a Jewish holiness he did not forgo, did not even postpone the satisfaction of a very common need, of a caprice. He let it be read in this, his separation from his people, his whole contempt for servitude under objective commandments, that he himself broke them through the most arbitrary actions or let it happen that they were broken.” As examples of this Hegel cites the prohibition of plucking ears of grain on the Sabbath and of eating the showbread. Thus Jesus set the whole subjectivity of man over against the external commandments. “He made the indeterminate subjectivity, the character, into an entirely different sphere, which has nothing at all in common with the punctilious observance of objective commandments.”

Next follows the relation of Jesus to the moral and civil commandments. These are, so an excursus of Hegel’s begins, grounded in the human essence itself, for the civil laws too are at the same time moral; both express natural relations of man in the form of commandments. Laws are unions of opposites in a concept. This lets them stand, according to its nature, in their opposition, but it itself consists in opposition to what is actual and thus expresses an ought: from these moments arises the form of the commandment. The commandment becomes civil when its content is imposed by an external power. The purely moral commandments determine the limit of the opposition in One living being, the civil laws on the other hand this limit between several living beings, so that these can still subsist alongside it. Those

[p. 93]

are a restriction of the opposition of one side or power of the living being against other sides or powers of the same, and these are such in the relation of living being to living being. The moral laws too are by their nature partly positive, insofar as a one-sided, alien power dominates the remaining powers, and they can become entirely positive if man has this master not even in himself, but outside himself. Therefore Jesus, in order to sublate the positivity of the Jewish laws, could not take the path of showing that these same laws are universal expressions of a human faculty, the faculty of universality, expressions of reason. At this place there is found that critique of Kant’s moral doctrine which was already inserted above to elucidate what the Basic Fragment contains on the matter. It is not the full expression of the ethical consciousness, and with this there falls also Kant’s conception of the religiosity of Jesus, which Hegel himself had still adopted in the preceding period. On Kant’s moral standpoint there simply remains a separation of the legislating universal reason and our drives and inclinations, but Jesus wanted to restore man in his wholeness. This spirit of his, exalted above morality, has found its perfect expression in the Sermon on the Mount; it is “an attempt, carried out in several examples of laws, to take from the laws the legal character, the form of laws.” In his kingdom what the laws demand happens, but the new ethical life, in sublating the form of laws, supplements at the same time the Jewish moral laws. What thus raises above the separation of the universal and the particular, of reason and drive, is a being, a modification of life: that is the ethical life of Jesus, and the limitation in which it appears in the individual propositions is given only by the restriction to the determinateness of the object, just as the form of the commandment is grounded only in the necessity of conceptual expression. Propositions which are in fact of lasting value for the understanding of the discourses of Jesus.

Hegel divides the Sermon on the Mount of Jesus into three parts. The first is formed by paradoxes which announce that something entirely new is to be expected from him. “They are cries in which he, inspired, at once distances himself from the common creation of virtue, announces, inspired, another right and light, another region of life, whose relation to the world can only be to be hated and persecuted by it.”

[p. 94]

The second part of the Sermon on the Mount, its main mass, stands under the point of view of the fulfillment of the law by the new ethical life, and so its form here is the opposition of the individual laws and the new religiosity. Hegel rejects here for the teaching of Jesus also the expression “concord of the inclinations with the law,” which he had himself earlier used in Schiller’s sense, for in it law and inclination still occur as particular and opposed. This new ethical life is not agreement but unity—“life only as relation of different love, a being which, expressed as concept, law, is necessarily equal to the law or, as actual, as inclination opposed to the concept, is likewise equal to itself, to inclination.” What love lacks, since in it the law loses its form and the concept is displaced by life, “in universality, which grasps everything particular in itself in the concept, is only an apparent loss and a true infinite gain through the wealth of living relations with the perhaps few individuals with whom it comes into relation. It excludes not what is actual but what is thought, possibilities, and this wealth of possibilities in the universality of the concept is itself a lacerating of life.” In this sense Hegel now treats the individual teachings of Christ on the relation of law to fulfillment. Only one remarkable passage on property shall be singled out. “About the demand of casting off the cares of life and contempt of riches there is probably nothing to be said; it is a litany that is forgiven only in sermons or in rhymes, for such a demand has no truth for us. The fate of property has become too powerful for us for a reflection on it to be tolerable, for its separation from us to be thinkable.” The justification of Jesus’ utterances on riches lies in this, that ethical life, which is supposed to be determined only from within, is in property bound to something external to it, that legal relations intrude into the sphere of love. Jesus demands “elevation above the domain of right, of justice, of equity, of services of friendship that men can render one another in this domain, above the whole sphere of property.”

The last part of the Sermon on the Mount is treated only briefly. “What still follows is no longer a setting over against the laws of what is higher than they, but the recording of some utterances of life in its beautiful free region, as the union of men in asking,

[p. 95]

giving and taking. The whole closes with the endeavor to present the image of man, as it is drawn in what precedes in opposition to the determinacies, purely outside this sphere, which then, to be sure, can happen only in incomplete parables.”

#### 4. Reconciliation with Fate through Love.

Reconciliation of fate through love—so Hegel himself has designated the theme of this manuscript at the place where he sets another note, on the connection of the plurality of the virtues in love, in relation to it. An external connection that would link these two internally fully coherent pieces with the preceding has not been found. They develop further the nexus of life of the higher ethical life, as it is contained in the religiosity of Jesus, and belong therefore by their content at this place. Besides, no other place remains for them. Earlier they naturally could not stand, and later not, because we then have, up to the close of the work, a connection demonstrable even externally, for which the concepts worked out in this fragment are the foundation.

If the relations of individuals to one another posited in life are disturbed, the question arises how their restoration is possible. The answer which Jewish religiosity gives to this question is rejected, and, linking up with the idea of fate of Greek tragedy, the relation of the religiosity of Jesus to this question is set forth. But Hegel goes in this most profound of his theological fragments far beyond the task of treating a piece from the teaching of Jesus. Already the connection in which Jesus’ teaching and his fate are here grasped gives the fragment a distinctive independence. But a still further-reaching relation is added. This fragment is at the same time a coming to terms with Protestant dogma. Hegel’s metaphysical conception, according to which the unity of God with men is a nexus of life, and his conception of Christian religiosity demanded the hard work of dissolving the Protestant dogmatics founded on Paul and Luther. The same has its foundation in the punitive justice of Yahweh and in the abolition of punishment through the death of Christ. It is accordingly determined by Jewish concepts.

[p. 96]

Hegel has, like Schleiermacher, cut the threads which Paul so artificially drew from the Jewish law to Jesus. For him too Christianity rests on the positive foundations of Judaism in no other sense than that in which it has its foundation also in classical culture. The strong expression of this is that for him Judaism as a religious stage stands lower than Greek and Roman religiosity. His historical work, which accomplishes the separation of the Jewish representations from the revealed religion of Jesus, began already, as we have seen, when he sat under Storr’s lectern in Tübingen. In these lectures his problem was given him. For the juridical doctrine of justification contained in them led back to the formation of concepts of Paul, which rested on an arbitrary combination of the teaching of Christ with the Jewish concepts of Yahweh, his law and his system of punishment. At that time Hegel had grasped the opposition of Judaism and the religion of Jesus under the point of view of Kant’s writing on religion: heteronomy and autonomy, the dominion of the priest over consciences and the free power of the ethical consciousness. Now, however, he found in the moral law itself an inner opposition of reason and sensibility, of rule and inclination, of concept and life. Law and love, punitive justice and reconciliation of fate—the separation of these categories is that on which the fragment, unique in its historical genius, rests.

It thinks further an opposition that was already developed by him in the Tübingen years. The speculative moralist deduces the concepts law and virtue. He holds these concepts up to the living, demands that it satisfy them and struggles with the living to subject it to the concept. The popular teacher, the improver of men, turns to these themselves. And here he now finds the destructive power of vice and crime as the old problem which Judaism and Christianity attempt to solve.

Hegel has in this distinction above all Kant in view, and so the first attack too, which he makes against the doctrine of reconciliation that is founded on punitive justice, aims at first not properly at Paul, Luther and the renewers of this orthodox doctrine in his time, but at Kant’s doctrine of the law and of the function of punishment as fulfillment of justice.

[p. 97]

The opposition of duty and inclination had found its union in the modifications of love, the law had been taken up into love, its content remained preserved in it, only its form was lost. The question that now arose was much more difficult, the distress of life came much nearer to him here; here his standpoint had to prove itself practically over against Kant and orthodoxy.

Of all the fragments this is the one most difficult to decipher. Again and again Hegel has corrected into it, indifferent to all purposes of re-reading, only concerned to become clear to himself; one feels along with him the enormous agitation that seized him at that time over against this problem.

First he seeks to live himself entirely into that state in which the criminal stands over against the violated law. What has happened there? The law, as thought union of oppositions, stands outside life. If in the crime one of the opposed elements combined in the concept is destroyed, the concept remains standing, but then expresses only what is missing, the gap, and is now called punishing law. And now not merely the form but the content of the law is opposed to life, for it now aims at the destruction of life. If the law is to be sublated also in this form as punitive justice, then its content too must be removed: punishment.

The criminal, viewed from this standpoint, has destroyed something external to him; punishment corresponds to the crime; the necessity that it follow lies in something external, in the offended law. The criminal has stepped out of the sphere of right, which was the content of the law, and has thereby also lost his right. Here the first consequence shows itself: since the concept of the law is only something thought, the punishing law can realize itself only if it combines with the living. It must clothe itself with power. And there at once the whole incongruence of concept and life comes to light. For this executor of the law, the judge, is not an abstract justice, but a being, and justice is only his modification. Hence the avenger can give up avenging, he can forgive, the judge can pardon. “But with that justice is not satisfied; it is inflexible, and as long as laws are the highest, so long it cannot be escaped, so long the individual must be sacrificed to the universal, i.e. it must be killed.” “The law persists in its

[p. 98]

fearful majesty and cannot be got at by love”: it remits no punishment, otherwise it would sublate itself.

Thus Hegel uncovers the cruelty lying in this legal standpoint. “Punishment follows the deed, their connection is indissoluble; if there is no way to make an action undone, if its actuality is eternal, then no reconciliation is possible, not even through undergoing the punishment.” The punishment suffered alters nothing in the consciousness of the evil action, in the judgment of conscience, in the hostile position of justice toward the continuing external and inner effect of the action. “The criminal always sees himself as a criminal; he has no power over his action as an actuality, and this his actuality is in contradiction with his consciousness of the law.” This condition man cannot endure. He flees from this terrible actuality of evil and from the unchangeability of the laws into the bosom of grace. This, then, is the curse of this standpoint, that in the end it forces to immorality, drives to dishonesty. Man seeks to run away from the law by taking flight to God as the guardian of abstract justice and of his goodness, and hopes that he will look at him differently from what he is. “He finds comfort in the thought, in the untrue representation, which (an)other being makes of him.” “And so there would be no return to oneness of consciousness on a pure path, no sublation of the punishment of the threatening law and of the bad conscience other than as a dishonest begging.”

In this note the identification of Kant’s morality with the Old Testament law may be said to be completely accomplished. And as a consequence of the standpoint of law it has resulted that reconciliation is not possible here. Even the vicarious death of Christ cannot satisfy the law. For Christ as representative of sinners would be as such a universal; but the law is posited only by confronting, as the universal, the particular of the individual acting men.

The whole standpoint itself must be abandoned. Consciousness must adopt a position in which the laws and punitive justice are recognized as a subordinate relation of divine life to guilt. This new standpoint, on which reconciliation becomes reality, is that of Jesus, which Hegel however expands into a general one, by

[p. 99]

drawing in from Greek tragedy the concept of fate. At this place the dialectic of history, as Hegel’s method of grasping the spiritual world, confronts us in its wholly original shape. In the consciousness that thinks itself, its actions and its guilt under the idea of punitive justice, there is an inner relation of its states to one another. This leads from the striving for liberation from guilt to the recognized impossibility of ever satisfying the rigid law. The law stands over against life as a universal, therefore inflexible and alien to it. The profound presentation lacks only the logical formula: according to this, a contradiction opens up in life itself; it rests on the opposition of the universal and the particular; this contradiction drives forth a higher stage, in which the urge for reconciliation realizes itself. This higher stage of the moral consciousness expresses itself in new relations which are contained in it. The united life, its destruction through guilt, fate as reaction of life against it, and love as feeling of life that finds itself again: in these relations the real reconciliation is accomplished.

“The law is later than life and stands lower than it.” Hegel proceeds from life, as the highest concept of his mysticism. “Life is not different from life, because life is in the united Godhead.” The criminal believes he destroys alien life, but since all life is one, he has injured his own. “In his arrogance he has indeed destroyed, but only the friendliness of life; he has turned it into an enemy.” To the murderer the killed life, which however is immortal, now appears as his enemy, “as a terrifying specter that looses all its Eumenides.” “The departed spirit of the injured life steps up against him like Banquo, who came to Macbeth as a friend, was not annihilated in his murder, but in the next moment nonetheless took his chair, not as a table-companion, but as an evil spirit.” “From the point at which the criminal feels the destruction of his own life or recognizes himself in the bad conscience as destroyed, the working of his fate begins, and this feeling of the destroyed life must become a longing for what is lost.” Not the fear of an alien law but the dread of himself, the consciousness of the injury of divine life in himself, is his inner fate. “In fate

[p. 100]

man recognizes his own life, and his supplication to it is not supplication to a lord, but a returning and drawing near to himself. Fate brings about a longing for the lost life. This longing, if one is to speak of becoming better and being made better, can already be called a betterment, because, being a feeling of the loss of life, it recognizes what was lost as life, as what was once friendly to it.” It is “conscientious,” in that it “prolongs the bad conscience and the feeling of pain and stirs it up every moment, in order not to reunite with life frivolously, but from the depths of the soul, to greet it again as a friend.” Thus pilgrims in hair shirts, barefoot, prolonged and multiplied with every step on the hot sand the consciousness of evil and the pain, and yet at the same time beheld in this pain life itself, though as something hostile to them, and so made reacceptance possible. “For the opposition is the possibility of reunion, and insofar as life was opposed in pain, it is capable of being taken up again.” Even what is hostile is felt in this state as life, and therein lies the possibility of the reconciliation of fate. “This feeling of life, which finds itself again, is love, and in love fate is reconciled.”

“Guilt and fate” is also the theme of a chapter of Hegel’s Phenomenology; it treats the same problem: the relations between character, action, guilt and fate, which belong to a region into which the relations of law, virtues and punishment do not reach. In these relations the nexus of life itself speaks to us: here, then, is found the famous passage on the Antigone, in which this nexus of life is developed from the tragedy of Sophocles.

But this ideal of Hegel’s reveals its whole greatness in the last part of our fragment, which deals with the guilt of innocence and its conflict with fate. Punishment exercises its dominion only as far as the rule and the delimited virtues reach: “fate, on the other hand, is incorruptible and unbounded like life.” “Where life has been injured, however rightfully, however much with self-satisfaction it was done, there fate appears, and one can therefore say: innocence has never suffered; all suffering is guilt. But the

[p. 101]

honor of a pure soul is all the greater, the more consciously it has injured life in order to preserve the highest.”

The acting man steps onto the battlefield of power against power; he consciously takes the guilt upon himself and abandons himself to fate: the suffering of valor strikes him as just fate, because he has entered upon the domain of right and of power. Over against him stands passivity with its powerless, painful enduring. Between the two stands the beauty of the soul. At this place Hegel speaks of the contradiction in those two opposed states, valor and passivity, and “of the truth of both opposites.” Already so early the view of dialectic in the spiritual world receives its logical formulation. The beautiful soul expresses itself in the free elevation above the loss of right and above struggle without suffering, in voluntary renunciation of life. It withdraws from relations that are stained, since it could not remain in them without defiling itself. It takes upon itself with its own will the unhappy fate and the pains arising from it. With this, however, such a man has raised himself above all fate. “Life has become unfaithful to him, but he not to life; he has fled it, but not injured it; and he may long for it as for an absent friend, but it cannot pursue him as an enemy; and he is vulnerable on no side, like the bashful plant he draws into himself at every touch.”

Thus the negative attribute of the beauty of soul is “the highest freedom, i.e. the possibility of renouncing everything in order to preserve oneself.” And now Hegel utilizes these traits in order to make Jesus understandable through them. He demanded of his friends to leave everything, in order not to enter into a bond with the degraded world. Entangled in nothing objective, he had nothing to forgive the offender. No hostile feeling was in him, no pride and no demand on others. But precisely in this withdrawnness from the relations of life and from the demands of right the highest guilt is joined with the highest guiltlessness: with the sublimity above all fate the most unhappy fate was thus compatible in him.

Hegel then depicts the state of the heart, as it is contained in the religion of Jesus, first in its conduct toward injuries which

[p. 102]

come from outside. “A heart that is so exalted above the relations of right, entangled in nothing objective, has nothing to forgive the offender; for he has violated no right of it; for it gave it up as soon as its object was assailed. It is open to reconciliation, for it is possible for it immediately to take up again every living relation, to re-enter into the relations of friendship, of love, since it has injured no life in itself; from its own side no hostile feeling stands in its way within it, no consciousness, no demand on the other to restore the violated right, no pride that would demand of the other the confession of having been beneath it in a lower sphere, the sphere of right. The forgiveness of faults, the readiness to reconcile oneself with the other, Jesus makes so determinately the condition of the forgiveness of one’s own faults, of the sublation of one’s own hostile fate. Both are only different applications of the same character of the soul. In reconciliation toward offenders the heart no longer insists on the legal opposition which it acquired against them, and in giving up, as its hostile fate, the right, the evil genius of the other, it reconciles itself with him and has won for itself just as much in the domain, just as much life that was hostile to it has made a friend, has reconciled the divine with itself; and the fate armed against itself by its own deed has dissolved into the airs of night.” This reconcilability is confronted not only by personal hatred, which strives to bring to fulfillment against the other the right grown out of the injury, but “besides this hatred there is also a wrath of rectitude, a hating strictness of dutifulness, which has to be angry not over an injury to its individual but over an injury to its concepts, the commandments of duty.” Yet even this righteous hatred attains to no real reconciliation with fate.

For the reconciliation of fate which one has oneself awakened against oneself through unlawful injury of life, Hegel here advances a new point of view. The relation of law and punishment rests on the principle of equalization. Thereby the standpoint of the law lifts out of the totality of the man who has carried out the injury of life this injury; before the law the criminal is precisely nothing but a criminal.

[p. 103]

In relation to human nature itself, however, law and crime are only cuttings, parts, fragments. Thus this standpoint is overcome by going back to the wholeness of human nature. “Since the punishment does not come upon man from an alien law, but the law and right of fate first arises out of man—so the return to the original fate, to wholeness, is possible, for the sinner is more than an existing sin, a crime having personality; he is man, crime and fate is in him, he can return again to himself, and when he returns, under him; the elements of actuality have dissolved, spirit and body have separated; the deed does indeed still exist, but as something past, as a fragment, as a dead rubble; that part of it which was bad conscience has vanished, and the memory of the deed is no longer an intuition of himself; life has found life again in love.” In this sense Jesus too grasped the connection between sin and forgiveness; for him too it was contained in nature itself, in life; he posited reconciliation in love and fullness of life; he tied it to faith. “To have faith in Jesus means more than to know his actuality and to feel one’s own less in power and strength and to be a servant; faith is a knowledge of spirit by spirit, and only like spirits can know and understand each other, unlike ones only know that they are not what the other is; difference of the power of spirit, of the degrees of strength, is not inequality; the weaker, however, attaches himself to the higher as a child or can be drawn up to him.” The closer proof that his presentation of the nexus of life of fate, reconciliation and love corresponds to the religiosity of Jesus, Hegel wants to supply later.

So the line of the train of thought returns to its beginning; the problem was reconciliation. The presuppositions of consciousness under which it is actuality in Jesus have now been found. Not as a one-time achievement of a God or man for others, but as an inner nexus of life of the higher ethical life.

It may have been about the thirtieth year of his life when Hegel wrote down this fragment. Schopenhauer was about as old when he put together, out of fragments of a similar kind, The World as Will and Representation.

[p. 104]

The profundity of a great poet in the conception of life, in the imaginative power to set it forth, is in Schopenhauer no greater than in Hegel, however dark and awkward the language that stands at the latter’s disposal as expression for this.

#### 5. The Virtues and Love.

“Love, however, reconciles not only the criminal with fate, it also reconciles man with virtue”: with these words begins the presentation, directly connected to the fate fragment, of the union of the virtues through love. The next sentences then show that here too we are within the presentation of the religiosity of Jesus; and indeed Hegel now at once sets the conception of virtues contained in it over against Kant’s doctrine of virtue. Kant had in his Metaphysics of Morals distinguished the doctrine of right, which is capable of external coercion, from the doctrine of virtue, which treats the domain of ethical life inaccessible to external power; here self-coercion rules, for virtue is for him the moral strength to fulfill the moral law in accordance with duty. Hegel’s critique of this standpoint, which we already know, recurs here. “To the complete servitude under the law of a foreign master Jesus opposed not a partial servitude under a law of one’s own, the self-coercion of Kantian virtue, but virtues without dominion and without subjection, modifications of love.” There follows then a dialectical dissolution of Kant’s doctrine of virtue, in which as its complement the principle of love is demonstrated. The main propositions of this digression will be used later for the presentation of the form of Hegel’s ethical cognition in the chapter on his ideal. To this is joined a presentation of the organic nexus of ethical life, as it springs from the dominion of love in the human spirit. “A living bond of the virtues, a living unity, is something quite other than the unity of the concept; it does not set up for determinate relations a determinate virtue, but appears, even in the most motley mixture of relations, unrent and simple; its external shape can modify itself in the most infinite way, it will never have the same twice, and its utterance can never give a rule, for it never has the form of a universal against the particular.” This force of union operative in life

[p. 105]

Hegel calls love. “It is the living relation of the beings themselves, in it all separations, all limited relations have vanished.” He designates love also as “feeling of the whole.”

According to Jesus, this love is to be the soul of his friends, the new commandment, the mark of recognition of the relation to him. It is now one of the basic thoughts of Hegel that the highest energy of this relation can prevail only in a real organized community: so he sets over against such a community here, drastically, the general love of mankind of the eighteenth century. “This general love of mankind is an insipid but characteristic invention of the times, which cannot help setting up ideal demands, virtues, against a thing of thought, in order to appear quite splendid in such thought objects, since their actuality is so poor. Love of the neighbors is love of the men with whom one, as everyone, comes into relation. What is merely thought cannot be a beloved.” The difficulty that in the teaching of Jesus love appears as a commandment is resolved here, as at other places in the manuscripts, by the claim that such an imperative is only the mode of expression for an inner conduct that corresponds to the ethical ideal. “Love cannot be commanded in the sense that its essence is no dominion over something alien to it; yet it is so little under duty and right that it is rather its triumph to rule over nothing and to be without hostile power against another.” “Utterance of love is not spirit, but opposed to it, and only as a name, as a word can it be commanded, it can only be said: You shall love; love itself utters no ought; it is no universal opposed to a particularity; not a unity of the concept but oneness of spirit, divinity.” At this place there is found that dissolution, used above, of the commandments of Christ concerning love of God and of neighbor into the unity of infinite life, in whose harmony everything conflicting vanishes. “Only through love is the power of the objective broken, for through it its whole domain is overthrown. The virtues by their limit still always posited something objective outside themselves, and the plurality of the virtues a so much greater, insuperable manifold of the objective; only love has no limit, what it has not united is not objective to it, it has overlooked it, or not yet developed it, it does not stand over against it.”

[p. 106]

The Lord’s Supper is a celebration of love. The act of the Lord’s Supper goes beyond the character of a meal of love and approaches a religious act, without however being able to raise itself to one. Love is not yet religion; for the object of a religious veneration can only be “a union in love objectified by the power of imagination”: the feeling of love, as it pervades the Lord’s Supper, would have to be united with the representation of it through imagination. The common supper is an act of friendship, “still more connecting is the solemn eating of the same bread, the drinking from the same cup.” In all this there is only a presentation of friendship. The declaration of Jesus: this is my body, and the distribution make the feeling in part objective. It is tied to something actual and given and enjoyed in it. And the act becomes mystical through the relation between bread and wine, body and blood of Jesus and his spirit; those who partake are bound with Jesus through the object, and this union produces the commonality of spirit. But this mystical act cannot become a religious act, because the objective, in which it is accomplished, vanishes in the act itself. Here the limit makes itself felt, within which the Lord’s Supper is capable of producing a satisfying religious effect. This side of the Christian sacrament in Hegel’s conception will be treated at a later place.

#### 6. The Ideal of Love.

Here a fragment may now find its place, of which we do not know whether Hegel meant to fit it into the plan of this work at all, since no relation of any kind to it has been demonstrable. By its content, however, it belongs in this series, and although nowhere on these leaves is reference made to the teaching of Jesus, they can yet stand here as the highest metaphysical expression which Hegel found at that time for the unity of life in love. Like a hymn the fragment sounds, and in its feeling it is akin to the rhapsodies of Shaftesbury and Schiller and the hymns of Hölderlin, but reaches beyond them into the depths of the old mysticism.
