It sets up the ideal of the higher ethical life, in which the union with God and with fellow men is accomplished in love, against the manner in which the ordinary

[p. 107]

Christian consciousness, in which Jewish egoism lives on, seeks to attain this union.

At its base lies the distinction of the stages of the apprehension by the understanding and of the metaphysical consciousness of all-life. On the standpoint of reflection man finds himself in separation over against the external world; between the two there obtains the relation of reciprocal action of independent forces, they condition each other mutually, and as now both are independent only relatively in relation to each other, they need an alien power through which they are and have their connection. Propositions which recall the theoretical grounding of such a worldview of the understanding in Descartes. “Love, however, is not understanding, whose relations let the manifold always count as a manifold and whose unity itself is oppositions; it is not reason, which sets its determining absolutely over against the determined; it is nothing delimiting, nothing delimited, nothing finite.” At this height of the conduct of life there is nothing finite any more, no opposition of the subject against anything else. The view of the understanding is overcome, in that the spirit has transposed itself above the oppositions into the unity of all-life. It is the same ascent from individual consciousness to that of all-life as forms also the mystical core of Spinoza’s Ethics.

On this basis Hegel presents the stages of the union of man with God and with his fellow men.

The union of what is separated in its innermost nature, on the stage of the view of the understanding, is accomplished through subordination under alien power, and so it remains within the barriers of a relation of dominion that connects from outside. Man here sees himself as the center of things. He feels himself master over the objects and in the favor of the ruling God. But the further the community with his equals extends—from Abraham and his family to Israel, and from there to the whole of Christendom, indeed to the human race as such—, the more the individual loses in his worth, in his claims, for his worth was the share in dominion. The whole misery of this condition now makes itself felt. Love prevails for the sake of what is dead. Everything is to man external world, stuff, in itself indifferent, he himself something independent, opposed in his innermost to nature. For him matter is

[p. 108]

absolute. “But of course, if he himself were no more, then there would also be nothing more for him.” Thus he needs his individual existence. “That he should wish to be is very comprehensible; for outside his collection of limitations, his consciousness, there lies not the self-complete eternal union, only the arid nothing”; and in this he cannot bear to think himself. He is here only as something opposed: what is opposed is mutually condition and conditioned; neither bears the root of its essence in itself; each is only relatively necessary. “The one is for the other, and so also for itself, only through an alien power; the other is allotted to it by its favor and grace; it is everywhere nowhere but in an alien thing an independent being, from which alien thing everything is given to man as a gift and to which he must give thanks for himself and immortality, for which he begs with trembling and quaking.” Here, then, one finds the same dialectic, driving on from a given situation of consciousness to ever further consequences, which Hegel had already shown in the account of the dissolution of the Greek world of imagination and in that of the advance from Judaism to Christianity.

This necessary consequence can be escaped only by giving up the whole standpoint of the understanding. In love the individual rises to the unity of all-life and is able to dissolve the view of the understanding. Genuine love takes place only among living beings who are equal in power. Here is the true union, which excludes all oppositions. It is feeling, and in it the feeling and the felt are not distinguished: “It is a feeling of the living, and as living the lovers are one.” It is no single feeling, for this would be only a part-life, not the whole life: “Out of the single feeling life presses, by dissolution, to dispersion in the manifoldness of feelings, and in order to find itself in this whole of manifoldness. In love this whole is not contained as in the sum of many particulars, separated; in it life itself finds itself, as a doubling of itself and a oneness of itself.”

Here the coming basic thought of the system suddenly looms darkly toward one, and it is Fichte whose concepts of the absolute I, of reflection into itself, of opposition and of union are carried into this mystical sphere of love and life, and perhaps Schelling’s reshaping of Fichte is already at work in it. “Life has

[p. 109]

run through the circle from undeveloped oneness, by way of culture, to a completed oneness. Over against the undeveloped oneness stood the possibility of separation and the world; in the course of development reflection produced ever more that is opposed, which was united in the satisfied drive, until it set the whole of man himself in opposition to him, until love sublates reflection in complete objectlessness, robs what is opposed of all character of an alien thing, and life finds itself without further lack. In love the separated is still there, but no longer as separated, as one, and the living feels the living.”

And now he plunges into last depths, where, as in Plato’s Symposium, the sensuous and the spiritual mingle. The wedding of Greekdom and Christianity is accomplished for him here too. According to Plato there rules in love the unconscious striving of transient life after immortality: from the union of the lovers a continuation of life is to proceed, which realizes immortality in the succession of generations. Hegel says that love strives “to unite the mortal and to make it immortal.” Shame is, according to him, love’s wrath against individuality. “That which takes does not thereby become richer than the other; that which gives does not make itself poorer; in giving to the other it has increased its own treasures by just as much. This wealth of life love brings about in the exchange of all thoughts, of all manifoldnesses of the soul, in that it seeks infinite differences and finds out for itself infinite unions, turns to the whole manifoldness of nature, in order to drink love from each of its lives.” And every stage of development “is a separation, in order to win again the whole wealth of life.” In the first draft there was to follow upon this a discussion, not carried to its end, of the relation of property, gift and community of goods to love; in the revision it was then detached.

If these propositions are lifted out of the religious sphere of the heart more completely, the formulas of Hegel’s first writings arise. The essay on the Difference between the Fichtean and Schellingian Systems of 1801 places the task of philosophical thinking in the restoration of the sundered harmony of spirit: the understanding sets the finite over against the infinite, matter over against spirit, the body over against the soul, understanding over against faith, necessity over against freedom; it fixes

[p. 110]

the diremption: “To sublate such congealed oppositions is the sole interest of reason.” We notice how the separations, the limitations, the fixed oppositions in Kant’s system, hence the whole labor of this mighty understanding, of creating order and clarity through demarcation, are felt by Hegel as a misfortune and an unbearable, painful state of consciousness. Impetuously his mystical-aesthetic spirit presses toward the consciousness of the unity of the universe. But this unity is for him not before the oppositions, but bears the oppositions in itself. So he continues in the said treatise: “This interest of reason, to sublate congealed oppositions, does not have the sense as if it set itself against opposition and limitation as such; for the necessary diremption is one fact of life, which forms itself eternally opposing: and the totality is possible, in the highest vitality, only through restoration out of the highest separation.” This proposition too is prepared in our fragment. In conclusion the Phenomenology says: “Absolute spirit gives itself the shape of self-consciousness.” And in this fragment there already appears the concept of reflection as the activity of separating, of opposing, which he then applied in the essay on the Difference between the Fichtean and Schellingian Systems and in the treatise on Faith and Knowledge, or the Philosophy of Reflection of Subjectivity, and laid at the basis of the whole account of the philosophical age that preceded him. Thus one sees arise in this work of Hegel the concepts that were then grasped more generally in his first writings and raised into a stricter philosophical form.

#### 7. The Religiosity of Jesus and the Metaphysical Content of Its Basic Ideas.

The connection of Hegel’s manuscripts that led from the beginning of Jewish history to the presentation of the discourses and the Sermon on the Mount, and to which we could then attach the three following pieces only as to content, now receives, whatever gaps there may be in between, its continuation in the connection of manuscripts that we now enter. This order results from several grounds. As for Hegel in the preceding period ethical life was always the basis of religiosity, so too the Basic Fragment

[p. 111]

says: “With the change of the objective law, the other sides too of the relationship of the Jews had to change,” namely their relationship to God. Leaves that interpret biblical passages and form a preliminary work for the presentation let the chapters Morality, Religion and History follow one another, and indeed they treat under Morality the relation of law and love, under Religion the metaphysical unity in Jesus, under History what in our account too makes up the last part and is designated as the Fate of Jesus. To the presentation, attached to John, of the metaphysical part of the teaching of Jesus in an older redaction a sketch is appended which gives the main points in the fate of Jesus. And in the executed presentation an uninterrupted connection runs from the prologue of John to the close of the whole in the dogma of the community. From this the position of the connection of manuscripts that are now to be discussed emerges with certainty. At the same time it is given with this that the manuscripts on fate and love, provided Hegel had assigned them any fixed place at all in his plan, belong before it, so that the place of these sheets in the connection of the whole would also be determined from here.

The notes themselves, which extend from the prologue of John to the dogma of the community, belong to two different redactions. Pieces of the first elaboration Hegel used for the second. It begins with Hegel’s metaphysical conception and the exposition of the mode of expression that John had at his disposal for it; then follows the presentation of the teaching itself, then the history of the relation of Jesus to the world, which makes up his fate; thereupon from this relation the position of the community toward the world is derived, and from it finally the origin of the dogma of the community. This connection is present in the manuscripts without a gap, even though by means of the system of cross-references and of the joining of the sentences to one another it can be established only with some difficulty. The later redaction is found partly in the margin of the former, partly in two sequences, of which one comprises three leaves and the other a larger quantity.

Hegel starts from the concept of pure life. To think this, one must abstract from all deeds of man, from what he was or will be. “Consciousness of pure life would be consciousness of what man is—in it there is no difference, no developed

[p. 112]

manifoldness. This simple is not a negative simple, a unity of abstraction, but a living being. Pure life is being. Multiplicity is nothing absolute—this pure one is the source of all individual lives, of the drives and of all deeds; but as soon as it comes into consciousness, as soon as he believes in it, then it is indeed still alive in man, but posited partly outside man; because what is conscious limits itself to that extent, it and the infinite cannot be wholly in One.” Accordingly there arises the consciousness of God, in which the subject grasps pure life in itself and separates it from all manifoldness in it, from all drives, all deeds, all determinacy; thus this pure life separates itself from the determinate individual existence of the individual; it steps, although still alive in man, yet partly over against him; it is now the divine: for “wherein there is no soul, no spirit, therein is nothing divine.” The divine is spirit, separated from the limited.

Thus arises the intuition of God as spirit. Where the individual always feels himself determinate, always feeling or suffering as this or that: there what abides is only an empty unity of the totality of objects; it is then thought as ruling over the objects. Only where the pure feeling of life appears, which possesses in itself its justification and its authority, can God be grasped as spirit. This pure life, however, appears in a determinate man, “who cannot give to eyes bound and desecrated by realities the intuition of purity.” “Thus man cannot appeal to the whole that he now is, as to an absolute; he must appeal to the higher, to the Father, who lives unchanged in all changes.” “In the determinacy in which he appears he can appeal only to his origin, to the source from which every shape of limited life flows to him.” Thus the intuition of God arises not from our finding ourselves determinate, but from the grasping, in the pure I, of the relations of equality and unity. And only in enthusiasm can this intuition be accomplished.

Hegel now finds this intuition of God as spirit in the Gospel of John. Its expression in words was bound to the Jewish language; it was poor in spiritual relations; it rested on a culture which broke up the living religious connection into relations

[p. 113]

of different beings to one another: so the expressions for this connection must be translated back into the religiosity of Jesus that is meant by them.

A deeper difficulty lies, however, in the fact that the language of reflection is altogether incapable of expressing this divine life. This is the other side of Hegel’s mysticism at that time. As the relations of life that belong to love always take on, in the alien form of the concept, the false character of a commandment, so every expression about the divine becomes absurd in the form of reflection. One must grasp the divine with one’s own deep spirit, and the grasp will be so different in different individuals “as different as the relations of life and the opposition of the living to the dead have come to consciousness.” Hegel sets the “objective language” of sensibility and of the understanding over against life itself; “what is contradiction in the realm of the dead is not so in the realm of life.” A tree forms a unity with its three branches; each branch, like the others children of the tree, leaves and blossoms, is itself a tree; in the living the part is the same One as the whole, and conversely, the living are beings as separated, and their unity is just as much a being. Only of objects, of the dead, does it hold that the whole is another than the parts. But if the understanding saves itself by stopping at the absolute difference of beings, then absolute separation is raised to the highest of spirit—and with it the dead and the understanding that lives in these separations.

And now Hegel seeks to penetrate into the mystery that is given in the designation of Jesus as the Logos. Again he makes use of Fichte’s concepts. God is the One, in which there is no division, no opposition, and which yet at the same time has in itself the possibility of separation, of infinite division. “The manifoldness, the infinity of the actual is the infinite division as actual”; but “the individual, limited, opposed, dead is at the same time a branch of the infinite tree of life; every part, outside which the whole is, is at the same time a whole, a life.” And because this life is reflected, broken up into subject and predicate, it is life at the same time and “grasped life,” light, truth. Here too, as in the fragment on love, the coming system emerges clearly from the mysticism. Light is life as something reflected. John the Baptist was not the light; he felt the

[p. 114]

One behind the oppositions, he believed in it, “but his consciousness was not equal to life.” Nevertheless the light was in the world and in every man. For “all relations and determinations of the world are the work of the developing man.” In the man, however, in whom the light appears, the whole of nature comes to consciousness. It is probably Schelling’s doctrine that resounds here.

In a similar way Hegel made clear to himself the designation “Son of God.” “The relation of a son to the father is not a concept, such as unity, agreement of disposition, equality of principles, but living relation of living beings, equal life, only modifications of the same life.” God’s Son is the same being as the Father and only for the act of reflection a particular. “The Son of God is also Son of Man, the divine, in a particular shape, appears as man; the connection of the infinite and the finite is indeed a holy mystery, because this connection is life itself; reflection, which separates life, can distinguish it into infinite and finite, and only the limitation, the finite considered by itself, gives the concept of man, as opposed to the divine; outside reflection, in truth, this separation does not take place.”

The basic conception of the Hegelian philosophy of religion is found here, according to which the designation of Jesus as the Logos or the Son of God is the expression of the metaphysical relation of humanity to the Absolute. Christ is the man who knows his unity with the Absolute. On the standpoint of the understanding, however, the doctrine of the Son of God and Son of Man becomes that of two natures, which are thought as one in Jesus; the intuition of Jesus thereby falls prey to the critique of the understanding; if one starts from the demand for unity between two absolutely different substances, the right of the understanding is abolished; if one starts from the separation of the two substances as what is certain to the understanding, one raises the understanding and the separation accomplished in it to the highest form of knowledge. In these propositions the method is already indicated which the history of dogma of Hegel’s school has made use of.

The Jews stood on the standpoint of the understanding. “They, the poor, who bore in themselves only the consciousness of their wretchedness and their deep servitude,” had to accuse Jesus of blasphemy.

[p. 115]

“The infinite spirit has no room in the dungeon of a Jewish soul; the all of life none in a withering leaf; the mountain and the eye that sees it are subject and object, but between man and God, between spirit and spirit, this gulf of objectivity is not.” The essence of Jesus as a relation of the Son to the Father can in truth be grasped only with faith. Religious faith is wholly different from that in the reality of an external object.

The principle of religious faith is: “God is a spirit, and those who worship him must worship him in spirit and truth.” “How could that know a spirit which were not itself a spirit? The relation of a spirit to a spirit is feeling of harmony, their union; how could the heterogeneous unite? Faith in the divine is possible only because in the believer himself there is something divine, which in that in which it believes finds itself again, its own nature, even if it does not have the consciousness that this found thing were its own nature.” This universal relation of man to the divine was proclaimed by Jesus. “All thought of a difference of essence between Jesus and those in whom faith in him has become life, in whom the divine itself is, must be removed.” All passages of the Gospels in which Jesus seems himself to express his difference of essence Hegel explains from the “separation of his personality from the Jewish character”: from this he wants to separate himself. Toward his friends, however, he ascribes to himself no personality different in essence from theirs; with them he wants only to be one, they are to be one in him. The truest mirror of his beautiful faith in human nature are his discourses at the sight of the uncorrupted children. Whoever recognizes their pure being feels Jesus’ own being; “the deepest, holiest grief of a beautiful soul, its most incomprehensible riddle” is the necessity of the destruction of nature, of the defilement of the holy in the child. The distance from God is as incomprehensible to the noble heart as being one with God is to the understanding. And the lovely thought of the angels of the children, who continually live in the beholding of the Father, Hegel interprets as the unconscious, the undeveloped oneness, whose being and life is in God, where subject and object are united in intuition and not yet separated. “But what is lost, what has fallen into diremption, will be regained through the return to oneness, to becoming like children.”

[p. 116]

In a similar way he understands another saying of Christ: “Where two of you are agreed on something, to ask for it, the Father will let it come to pass for you.” Here it cannot be a matter of objects, for “in proper objects spirits cannot be at one”; it is the “reflected oneness” for which we are to ask, “as object something beautiful, subjectively the union.” “The beautiful, a oneness of you two or three, is also in the harmony of the whole, is a sound, a concord with it, and is granted by it; it is, because it is in it, because it is something divine.”

The faith in the Son of God, in which a specific dignity of Jesus seems to be contained, is brought by Hegel through a historical interpretation into accord with his mystical metaphysics. “The intermediate state between darkness, remoteness from the divine, lying captive under reality—and between an own wholly divine life, a confidence in oneself, is faith in the divine.” In this intermediate state the disciples found themselves during Jesus’ stay on earth; “they did not rest on themselves; Jesus was their teacher and master, an individual center, on which they depended.” “Only after the removal of his individuality could their dependence on it cease,” “this objectivity too, this partition between them and God” fell, and “their own spirit or the divine spirit could subsist in themselves.” “The perfection of faith, the return to the Godhead from which man is born, closes the circle of his development. Everything lives in the Godhead, all living things are its children; but the child bears the oneness, the connection, the accord with the whole harmony, undestroyed but undeveloped, in itself.” In action separation comes about; but when the oneness is regained, now developed and self-created, then the holy spirit of God is present in man. This is once more a thought that was further developed in Hegel’s philosophy of religion. According to it the idea of the unity of the divine with the human spirit appeared for the disciples in a “This,” in the immediate singularity of this One Person: in it the absolute transfiguration of finitude is brought to intuition. It is manifest to the disciples in the religious form of representation. With Christ’s death begins internalization, spiritual apprehension of his sensible appearance. Reconciliation has turned to the

[p. 117]

inner; here it is certainty; the spirit of God has become actuality in the community.

The symbol for this immersion of the soul in the unity of all life, so that the whole world with its determinacy vanishes, is for Hegel baptism. In the submersion in a fullness of water the longing to overflow into the infinite seems stilled. More mystical still than this interpretation, which passes over the natural relation of baptism to purity, is then its connection with the “initiation into the relation of the Father, Son and holy spirit.” “Go ye into all nations, and let your disciple-making be that you initiate them into the relation of the Father, Son and holy spirit, that it (they), like the water the one immersed in water, may flow around and feel around him in all points of his being.”

From this life in the unity of all existence the community consciousness springs. It feels not only how the many-sided consciousness of humanity, the many shapes of life, sound together in One spirit, in One life, but sees also, above the equality of the divine co-beings, a higher oneness rise up, which makes of the mere assembly a community that is united not merely by a concept as believing, but by life and love. “This living harmony of men, their communion in God, Jesus calls the Kingdom of God.” This idea of a Kingdom of God completes and comprehends the whole of religion as Jesus founded it. In this Kingdom of God love is the bond that unites the believers, this feeling of the oneness of life, in which all oppositions as such are sublated; this friendship of souls, expressed as essence, as spirit for reflection, is the divine spirit, God, who governs the community. “Is there a more beautiful idea than a people of men who are related to one another by love? A more elevating one than to belong to a whole, which as a whole is one, which is the spirit of God, whose sons are the individuals?”

And yet it is “still to be considered whether this idea satisfies nature completely, or what need drove the disciples of Jesus to something further.”

So Hegel takes into view the limitation that is contained in this idea of the community bound by love. There is in it an “overleaping of nature,” against which Nemesis turns. Love presupposes separation, many-sidedness of life, and the more exclusively

[p. 118]

it loves, the more indifferent it becomes to other forms of life. It demands equality of spirit, of interest, and of the relations of life; but a love among many allows only a certain degree of strength and intimacy. After the death of Jesus the disciples remained in common life, in devotion, in the striving after extension of their communion. Beyond this lies an immense field of objectivity, which permits manifold activity, but “in the task of love the community disdains every union that is not the most intimate, every spirit that would not be the highest”: this religiosity of devotion and love withdraws from “every other connection in an objective one, for a purpose, a development of another side of life.” It must do so, for the members of the community would put themselves in danger of colliding with one another with their individuality. “This danger is averted only by an inactive, undeveloped love, in that it, the highest life, remains unliving. Thus the unnatural extension of the scope of love entangles it in a contradiction, in a false endeavor, which had to become the father of the most terrible passive or active fanaticism. This restriction of love to itself, its flight from all forms, even if its spirit blew in them or they sprang from it, this removal from all fate is precisely its greatest fate.” And at this place is also the “point where Jesus is connected with fate, and indeed in the most sublime way, but suffered from it.”

#### 8. The Fate of Jesus and the Religion of His Community.

In direct sequel there now follows the presentation of the fate of Jesus. As far as I can see, Hegel was the first who looked so deeply behind the sources into the development of the relation of Jesus to the world surrounding him. Here too one again perceives the scenting out of development in Hegel’s historical spirit.

To a manuscript that contained an earlier draft of the preceding chapter there is attached a short plan of what was to follow, in which one sees how the metaphysical views of the nexus of life won in the fragment on fate are the foundation for Hegel’s understanding of the life of Jesus. “Fate of Jesus. Renunciation of the relations of life a) citizen’s, civil, b) political, c) living together with other

[p. 119]

people—family, relatives, nourishment.—The relation of Jesus to the world partly flight, partly reaction, combating the same. As long as Jesus had not changed the world, to that extent he had to flee it.” Here this sketch breaks off. Our manuscript treats beyond this also the fate of the community; with the presentation of its religiosity the work closes.

“With the courage and the faith of a man inspired by God, who is called an enthusiast by the clever people, Jesus appeared among the Jewish people.” Thus Hegel begins the presentation of the fate of Jesus. “He appeared anew in his own spirit, the world lay before him as it was to become, and the first relation in which he placed himself to it was to call it to becoming otherwise; he began by calling to all: change yourselves, the Kingdom of God is near.” The Jews on their part wanted something other than what they had hitherto. Had there lain in them, in their unrest and dissatisfaction with reality, the need for something purer, then faith would have met the call of Jesus, the Kingdom of God could have developed among them—“the bonds would have fallen away from the old fate.” But as the state of the Jewish spirit was, only “a few pure souls with the drive to be cultivated attached themselves to Jesus.”

The next stage in the relation of Jesus to the world is the sending out of these disciples. “With great good-naturedness, with the faith of a pure enthusiast, he took their longing for a satisfied heart, their drive for perfection, their renunciation of some former relations, which for the most part were not brilliant, for freedom and healed or satisfied fate. For soon after his acquaintance with them he held them capable and his people ripe to follow a widespread announcement of the Kingdom of God, he sent his pupils in pairs about the land, to let his call resound multiplied.” The hope Jesus had placed in their mission was not fulfilled; “the divine spirit did not speak in their preaching”; the Jewish masses remained indifferent.

Thus there arose in Jesus a growing bitterness against his age and his people. In the Pharisees the spirit of his nation had found its strongest and most passionate expression; Jesus’ intercourse with them shows now that he has given up the possibility of refuting them, instructing them, or even cultivating them, and the words of Jesus (Matt. 11, 25),

[p. 120]

that God has hidden his truth from the clever and wise and revealed it to babes, are for Hegel the expression of Jesus’ changed position toward the Jewish world: he now renounced altogether the attempt to change the fate of his nation, he separated himself from it, he confined himself to the cultivation of individuals. “Because all, even the most beautiful forms of life were stained, Jesus could involve himself with none; in his Kingdom of God there could be no relation but that which would proceed from beauty and freedom themselves. The relations of life among his people were under the slavery of the laws and of the selfish spirit.” In the place of the hope of a reform of the nation and its orders there entered the expectation of the coming struggle of the holy with the unholy. Now he declared that he had come to bring the sword, not peace, and because his Kingdom of God on earth could not yet find a place, he had to transfer it into heaven.

Here the later redaction sets in. Jesus had to behave passively toward the fate of his nation. He let the rule of the Romans stand. “He stood with the state in the sole relationship of staying within its jurisdiction, and the consequence of this power over him he submitted to with contradiction of his spirit, suffering with consciousness.” Thus the limit in Jesus’ conduct, which proceeded from his fate in relation to this Jewish world, made itself felt as especially disadvantageous in his relation to the state. The Kingdom of God, according to Jesus, is not of this world. This is meant not only in the sense that for it this world does not exist, but in such a way that it is opposed to it. Consciously Jesus suffered from the state, hence between this and the community there exists an opposition: “Thus with this relation to the state a great side of living union, for the members of the Kingdom of God an important bond, is already cut off,” “a multitude of active relations, living relations lost.” The citizens of the Kingdom of God become private persons, who exclude themselves from the state, but cannot sublate it, and so fall under the domination of an alien power, which they despise but must bear.

Jesus could carry the Kingdom of God only in his heart, enter into relation with men only in order to cultivate them, “in order first to create men whose world would be his.” The relations of life around him were

[p. 121]

desecrated, “so he could find freedom only in the void.” He had to thrust the fate of his life from himself, in order to preserve his pure life within himself, yet undeveloped and unenjoyed. He could not fulfill nature: only as a gleaming shadow did its form stand before him, yet its animation and deed and reality he had to renounce. He had to flee all living relations, because they all lay under the law of death. He was not permitted to let himself be entwined by the threads of Jewish legality, and so he had to separate from his own, was not permitted to love a woman, to beget children, to be a fellow citizen, and to enjoy living together with others. “The fate of Jesus was to suffer from the fate of his nation, either to make it his own and bear its necessity and share its enjoyments and unite his spirit with theirs, but to sacrifice his beauty, his connection with the divine, or to thrust the fate of his people from himself.” “Jesus chose the latter fate, the separation from his nation and the world, and demanded the same of his friends.” But the more deeply he felt this separation, the less could he bear it calmly, and even his activity for the spread of the Kingdom of God was a courageous reaction of his nature against the world. So the existence of Jesus was partly activation of the divine in the struggle for the Kingdom of God, partly flight from the world into heaven and restoration, in ideality, of the life that was coming away empty-handed.

In this presentation of the consciousness of Christ Hegel leaves the Enlightenment far behind at a new and decisive point. Indeed, how much in present-day presentations appears backward in comparison with it! With the naïveté of historical profundity, which surrenders itself to no systematic or practical need, Hegel here draws the consciousness of Jesus as a historical magnitude, as this individually determinate thing that was there only once in this way. Far off recedes the colorless generality of the embodiment of humanity, the phantom of the unhistorical man in the middle of history. The Enlightenment is overcome on this point too, the historical consciousness has triumphed.

Hegel passes from this profound exposition of the religious consciousness in Jesus to the piety in the community founded by him. What follows on this is the triumph of his method at this stage, and it is, in the grandeur of a first cast, of marvelous genius.

[p. 122]

The total consciousness of the community is his object. He seeks out the necessary relations that connect the individual features of this consciousness with one another. In the nexus of life of the community, of the unorganized love alien to the world, there is contained an inner necessity, which leads to the objectification of this state in a transcendent Christian world. An inner dialectic—so he later called it—is accomplished, which advances from one feature of the Christian consciousness to the other and does not rest until the totality of the nexus of consciousness is reached that was possible under the given conditions.

The fate of Jesus was not wholly that of his community. For in this a plurality of like-minded persons was bound together. So the negative activity of struggle receded in it, and the Christian interest was transferred into the community itself. “The essence of their covenant was separation from men and love among one another; both are necessarily connected. This love was not meant to be, and could not be, a union of individualities, but union in God alone, in faith; in faith only that can unite which opposes a reality to itself, separates itself from it; with that this opposition was fixed, and an essential part of the principle of the covenant; and love had always to retain the form of love, of faith in God, without becoming alive and presenting itself in shapes of life.” And as now every shape of life was felt in its finitude and limitation, the relation to the world had to become a timidity before its contacts; there arose “a fear of every form of life,” precisely because it has shape and therefore limit. So in the community too love could not bring about a shaping of the whole of life. The beauty of existence as the mean between the extremes, namely the Jewish thirst for the possession of paltry realities and the withdrawal of the community from the same, remained denied to it. All the stronger was its longing to heighten the positive side in the union. From this longing there then grew the objectification of the ideal of the community: the miraculous, risen Son of God, the deified Jesus.

In this thought and its execution Hegel grasps the generation of the myth of Christ in the community. He derives this process of the

[p. 123]

elevation of the ideal into objectuality from the depths of the primitive-Christian community consciousness. He anticipates Strauss, but in a depth of religious understanding of the heart that none among his disciples has attained.

The negative side in the fate of the community was its opposition to the world: the relations to it became a crime for it. From its “flight into unfulfilled life” there sprang its “fatelessness”—an expression that Hegel has in common with Hölderlin. As the community stood alien to the forms of life, it did not even feel this lack itself. But its own nexus of life offered ever less substitute, the further love extended to an ever increasing community; for there arose in this way not a living union of the individualities, but it remained with the enjoyment of the consciousness of their mutual love. In the nexus of life of the community itself there lay thus a moment that led its consciousness beyond “the feeling” and the felt union of its members contained in love. Love had to present itself in an objective form. There was in it a “need to unite the subjective and the objective, feeling and its demand for objects, the understanding through imagination, in something beautiful, a God,” and this union is the God of the community. “The community has the need of a God who is the God of the community, in whom precisely the exclusive love, its character, its relation to one another is presented; not as a symbol or allegory, not as a personification of a subjective,” in which one would be conscious of the separation of the objective from what is presented, “but which is at once in the heart, at once feeling and object; feeling as spirit, which blows through all and remains one being, even if each individual becomes conscious of his feelings as his individual ones.” So in the Christian community consciousness Jesus is raised to the God who now becomes, as it were, as the objectuality of its ideal of love—“the shaped love”—the object of its veneration.

Now the distinction that Hegel made above between love and religion becomes quite determinate. Love is a divine spirit, but not yet religion. Only where the subjective nexus of feeling of love in the community was united with the objective objectuality given in the understanding did Christian religiosity arise. And this is

[p. 124]

now the decisive point, that Hegel designates imagination as the organ in which this unity is accomplished and the Godhead of Christian religiosity arises. The need to unite feeling and its demand for objects through imagination in something beautiful, that is God, “this need, the highest of the human spirit, is the drive toward religion.” Faith in God, however, could not satisfy this drive of the Christian community; for in the God of the world all beings are united, not merely the members of the community as such: “their harmony is not the harmony of the whole”; and yet precisely their communal feeling was to express itself in the object of their veneration. This God of the community is Jesus raised to divinity. The later redaction breaks off here, and we must take up the draft again.

Already during Jesus’ lifetime the community, under the power of the religious need which wanted to see its oneness presented, raised him above the human; “in order that the divine appear, the invisible spirit must be united with the visible.” As now in the individual Jesus this unity was there for them, it was by his death “thrown back into the separation of the visible and the invisible, of spirit and of the actual.” So his death had to be sublated by faith in a resurrection. Even if without this faith the image of pure humanity had gone forth for the disciples from Jesus’ grave, yet “the veneration of this spirit, the enjoyment of the beholding of this image, would have been accompanied by the memory of the life of this image, this sublime spirit would always have had its opposite in its vanished existence; and the presence of the same before the imagination would have been bound up with a longing which would have designated only the need of religion, but the community would still have had no God.”

“For beauty, for divinity the image lacked life; the divine in the community of love, this life, lacked image and shape. But in the one risen and then raised to heaven the image found life again and love the presentation of its oneness; in this remarriage of spirit and body the opposition of the living and the dead has vanished and has united in a God; the longing of love has found itself as a living being, whose veneration is now the religion of the community.”

[p. 125]

I do not doubt that Hegel conceived this religious process as the force that has brought forth the faith in the resurrection. He declined to examine the factual points of support that conditioned this faith. Indeed he wanted the whole problem of the historical reality of the resurrection to be kept entirely separate from the question of the connection of the religious processes in which the necessity of the faith in the resurrection was grounded. “The consideration of the resurrection of Jesus as an event is the standpoint of the historian, who has nothing to do with religion. Faith or unfaith in it as mere reality without the interest of religion is a matter of the understanding, whose activity, fixation of objectivity, is precisely the death of religion and to appeal to which means to abstract from religion.”

This religion of Jesus as the God of the community, of the risen one, was nevertheless unable to annihilate the consciousness of his earthly life and of his death on the cross, of his burial; so such human aspects had to be distinguished from the shape that is peculiar to the God. As the individual, external accessory being of his life, as a reality, drew the deified one downward, so he had to hover midway between the heavenly infinite, the unbounded, and the earth, this assembly of nothing but limitations. “It is not to be got out of the soul, the twofoldness of natures. As Hercules through the pyre, so the deified one too has swung himself up to the hero only through a grave; but there the altars are consecrated and the prayers brought to the shaped valor alone, to the hero who has become a god, no longer fighting nor serving, here not to the hero alone; not the risen one alone is the salvation of sinners and the rapture of their faith, the teaching and wandering one and the one hanging on the cross is worshipped too. This monstrous combination is that over which for so many centuries millions of God-seeking souls have struggled and tormented themselves.”

A few pages of the later redaction add to this view of the apotheosis of the risen one a few more features. Since the love of the community extended to a whole assembly of people, it could not attain an inner shaping and articulation. I should like to use the expression: it could not become an organizing and organized nexus of life.

[p. 126]

And as the expansion of the community constantly increased, the holy spirit of love became defiled in it, both in that it fought against the world and in that it took it into itself. This love did not come to presentation in the development of life, in the beauty of its relations, in the formation of the natural relationships. It did not become shaped life. So the community needed a criterion on which the mutual faith in this love could be grounded—a reality in which it could be able to recognize itself—a bond that connected perfectly; it had to set its fulfilled ideal over against itself as something alien, objectual, positive. The more dependence, the more the need to secure faith in a common master and teacher was felt by it, the more had the reality of his life and teaching to be bound up with his lordship and divinity. An inner contradiction, which now made itself felt in the deified Jesus, in his resurrection and in the miracles. “Individuality is to be object of adoration; and the shroud of reality stripped off in the grave has risen again out of the grave and has attached itself to the one who arose as God.”

Here these leaves of a later redaction end and the older one now continues, treating the relation of the miracles to the exaltation of Christ beyond the human. Jesus’ life is surrounded by miracle stories, not to demonstrate his divinity, but as proof that higher powers are bound to the individual Jesus. And still more sharply than in the faith in the resurrection the dualism of the human individual and the divine powers is stamped in the faith in miracles. “The miracles, which do not merely hover about him, but proceed from his inner power, seem attributes worthy of a God, to characterize a God.” The man who performs them and the divine power seem here inseparably bound, and the divine most intimately united with the objective. “But the closer the linking is, which nevertheless becomes no union, the harder falls the unnaturalness of the linked opposites. In the miracle as an action there is given to the understanding a connection of cause and effect and the domain of its concepts is recognized; but at the same time its domain is thereby destroyed; that the cause is not to be as determinate as the

[p. 127]

effect is, but is to be an infinite; since the connection of cause and effect in the understanding is the equality of determinacy, their opposition only this, that in the one this determinacy is activity, in the other suffering; here in the action itself an infinite is at the same time, with infinite activity, to have a most limited effect. Not the sublation of the domain of the understanding, but that it is at once posited and sublated, is the unnatural.” “Miracles are the presentation of the most ungodly, because they are the most unnatural and contain the harshest opposition of spirit and body combined in their whole monstrous crudity. Divine doing is restoration and presentation of the highest oneness.” “Through the abasement of the divine to a cause man is not raised up to it; a miracle is a true creation out of nothing and no thought fits so little with the divine as this. Instead of oneness being in the true divine and rest being found, the divine of miracles is the complete rending of nature.”

What now follows is inserted by a sign into the older version and begins on the reverse of the same leaf on which the conclusion of the original text stands; it is, however, very probable that this whole last part belongs to the new work. “The aroused expectation of raising to divinity, through wonderful capacities of this actual thing, the reality associated with the transfigured Jesus exalted to God, is so far from being fulfilled that it rather heightens all the more the harshness of this adjoining of an actual thing.” In the Oriental spirit there existed no such harshness of the opposition of spirit and body; the Oriental view is an indeterminate hovering between reality and spirit; the two were separated, yet not irrevocably: but as the European spirit now fixed what was so separated into absolute objectivities, realities absolutely opposed to spirit, the opposition sharpened, which lies in the faith in the resurrection alongside the striving for unity. The faith in the resurrection showed to “the deepest drive toward religion satisfaction, but did not grant it and made it into an infinite, inextinguishable and unstilled longing; for the longing is confronted, in its highest enthusiasm, in the raptures of the most finely organized souls breathing the highest love, always by the individual, an objective, personal thing, after

[p. 128]

union with which all the depths of their beautiful feelings languished, but which, because it is an individual, is eternally impossible, since it always stands over against them, eternally in their consciousness, and never lets religion become full life.”

“In all forms of the Christian religion that have developed in the progressive fate of the time, this basic character of opposition rests in the divine, which is to be present only in consciousness, never in life—from the enraptured unions of the zealot, who renounces all manifoldness of life, even the purest, in which the spirit enjoys itself, and is conscious only of God, and so could do away with the opposition of personality only in death, up to the actuality of the most manifold consciousness, the union with the fate of the world—and the opposition of God to it, either in this felt opposition or in all actions and expressions of life, which purchase their legitimacy through the feeling of the servitude and nullity of their opposition—as in the Catholic Church—or the opposition of God in mere thoughts, more or less devout, as in the Protestant Church, either the opposition of a hating God to life, as a disgrace and a crime, among some sects of it—or of a kindly God to life and its joys, as purely received, benefactions and gifts from him, as pure actuality, into which then the form of spirit hovering above it too, in the idea of a divine man, of the prophets, etc., is drawn down to a historical, objective view—between these extremes of the manifold or diminished consciousness of friendship, of hatred or of indifference toward the world, between these extremes, which lie within the opposition of God and world, of the divine and life, the Christian church has run through the circle forward and backward, but it is against its essential character to find rest in an impersonal living beauty; and it is its fate that church and state, divine service and life, piety and virtue, spiritual and worldly action can never melt together into one.”

This was the first attempt to grasp the transcendent Christian world of faith as a product of the community. It has been surpassed by none of those who

[p. 129]

have followed in the depth of historical vision. In the imagination he grasps the creative force, in the community the subject, from which this world of faith has risen, and this force works under the conditions of the religious need. As everywhere, so here too Hegel lacks philological criticism and historical method; he also has no understanding of the intermixture of Jewish, classical and other ancient motifs in the processes of formation out of which this world of faith sprang; but his surpassing greatness lies in his conception of the event as something spontaneous, generated in the religious condition of the community and grown up in its imagination.

#### Resumption of the Plan for an Essay on the Positive in Christianity.

Among Hegel’s manuscripts one bears the date of 24 September 1800. It contains the beginning of a work whose intention is clearly distinguished from the plan whose records we have just run through. It itself names as its subject the solution of the problem whether Christianity is a positive religion. “What this treatise makes its purpose is, in the immediate origin of the Christian faith, in the way in which it sprang from the mouth and life of Jesus, to re-understand whether circumstances occur therein which could have given an immediate occasion to positivity.” It delimits this task expressly and exactly in such a way that problems that had been dealt with in the preceding connection are excluded from it. At the same time, however, their importance and the necessity of their solution are pointed out, and the author lets it be noticed that he reserves their later treatment for himself.

Thus this work takes up again the plan that Hegel had carried out in the preceding stage of his inner development in the manuscript on the relation of the religion of reason to positive religion. It also represents nothing more than the reworking of the beginning of the old manuscript; essentially, a new introduction has taken the place of the earlier one, which, apart from the piece that Rosenkranz has already printed, though incompletely, is composed of passages of the old exposition, its supplements added in the margin

[p. 130]

and particular insertions; yet the reworking extends also to the beginning of the exposition itself. As far as it reaches, it forms a complete connection, but then breaks off in the middle of a leaf.

The older introduction stood on the standpoint of Kant and rested accordingly on the presupposition that the purpose of every religion, the Christian too, lay in morality; the later introduction, broadly conceived, has its center in the development of a new concept of the positive in religion and stands in avowed opposition to Kant. Its point of departure is the distinction, conclusively carried through by Kant, of natural religion from statutory, positive religion. But it then goes back to the opposition of the natural and the positive, embracing all historical and social phenomena, as it had been grounded in the natural system of the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries. The overcoming of this opposition, in which the false attitude of the Enlightenment toward what has come to be historically was grounded, was already at that time, quite generally, what mattered for Hegel in the fields of law, state and religiosity, and from this time on he sought to overcome the negative bearing of theory toward the historical order of society. His political notes will soon show this more closely; the whole circle of his works in this epoch proves how he had risen to the principle of the further development of existing conditions according to their historical foundations. He traces the opposition of the natural system of universally valid truths and of the historical as mere positivity back to the false extraction of a universal concept of human nature from the human and historical facts of the case. Abstraction separates the traits that constitute universal human nature from the whole manifoldness of the customs, habits and opinions of peoples or of the individual; in that it fixes these traits for themselves, those customs, habits and opinions, because they are not comprehended from the essence of man, become for it mere unintelligible factuality, accidents, prejudices and errors. Thus this standpoint is unable to understand the inner relation in which human nature stands to its infinite modifications. It does not see that religion, since it is not the product of the understanding and of reason, at all times contains components within itself that are not eternal and imperishable and yet are holy and worthy of veneration;

[p. 131]

for they follow quite naturally and necessarily from a historical modification of the human essence.

And it is therefore the main question for a genuinely historical consideration of religiosity “to show the appropriateness of religion to nature, how nature was modified in different centuries.” “The accidental is only one side of what counts as holy. If a religion has attached something eternal to something perishable, and reason fixes only the perishable and now cries out about superstition, then it is its own fault to have proceeded superficially and to have overlooked the eternal.”

The concept of positivity must accordingly be shifted to another place in the historical course. Religion becomes positive at the moment when the accidental as such, detached from its connection with the eternal, demands veneration. Does the original Christian religiosity, then, in this sense contain anything positive—“accidents which are to subsist as accidents as the very object of religion, which as something perishable are to have a high significance, as something limited holiness, and to be worthy of veneration”? This is the purely historical question as the treatise now poses it. That commandments or teachings of Christianity that were originally natural can subsequently become positive, when the free spirit rebels against them and they are maintained only by compulsion and external authority, has nothing to do with the theme of the treatise; “the investigation limits itself to whether such accidents already occur in the immediate founding of the Christian religion, in the teachings, in the actions, the fates of Jesus himself, whether in the form of his discourses, in his relation to other men, his friends or enemies, such accidents appear which, by themselves or through the circumstances, received an importance not originally lying in them, in other words, whether in the immediate origin of the Christian religion there lay occasions for its becoming positive.”

Beyond this theme Hegel catches sight of another, whose treatment seems important to him—a new working-up of the history of Christian dogma, as it had lain within the compass of his great work. He assigns it, however, to the future and gives here only the point of view under which such a work would have a meaning, namely if it were made truly historical.

[p. 132]

The pragmatic historiography of the Enlightenment was not equal to this problem. “One has church history show how upon simple truths that lay at the foundation, through passion and ignorance, such a heap of errors was gradually piled up, that in this gradual determination of the individual dogmas, continued through centuries, knowledge, moderation and reason did not always guide the holy Fathers, that already in the adoption of the Christian religion not merely pure love of truth, but in part very composite motives, very unholy considerations, impure passions and needs of the spirit often stemming from superstition were at work, that in general external circumstances alien to religion, self-interested aims, force and cunning molded the faith of the nations according to their purposes. But this manner of explanation presupposes a deep contempt for man, a glaring superstition about his understanding.” As religiosity was torn apart into a content of reason and something alien to it, one sought the ground of explanation for this alien element in superstition, deceit, stupidity, but especially in the sensuous nature of man; one made all this into a positive. And just as intolerable as this pragmatic history of the positive was the empty presentation, set over against it, of human nature and of the attributes of God in general concepts. After the tedious chatter about all this, it would now be a need of the time to justify historically the dogmatics so thoroughly rejected. That is a task that has yet to be solved. The justification of the Christian dogma in the old dogmatics is untenable; one must understand this religiosity from the need of human nature and derive from it the course of its development; then it shows itself in “its naturalness and necessity.” “Such an attempt presupposed the belief that the conviction of many centuries, that which the millions who lived and died upon it in these centuries held to be duty and holy truth—, that this was not sheer nonsense and even immorality, at least according to opinions. If, by the favored method of general concepts, the whole edifice of dogmatics has been declared a remnant of dark centuries untenable in enlightened times, one is yet so human as to put the question afterwards, how it can then be explained that such an edifice, which is so contrary to human reason and

[p. 133]

through and through error, could have been erected.” These words express the mood in which the various beginnings of the historical school were at that time united.

With a second task, too, Hegel does not wish to concern himself here, one which, as we know, he had already accomplished for himself; he does not wish to enter into the metaphysical questions to which the problem of the positive in religion must lead. For in the last analysis the origin of the positive character of religiosity can be understood only if it is traced back to an attitude of spirit in the religious process and the error in this is corrected from the standpoint of true metaphysics. Such an attitude is present “when human nature is absolutely separated from the divine, when no mediation of the two—except only in One individual—is admitted, but all human consciousness of the good and divine is degraded to the dullness and annihilation of a faith in something thoroughly alien and overpowering.” “One sees,” Hegel continues, “the investigation concerning this would, if it were to be conducted thoroughly through concepts, pass in the end into a metaphysical consideration of the relation of the finite to the infinite. But this is not the intention of this treatise. It lays down as its foundation the necessity that in human nature itself the need (lies) to acknowledge a higher being than human doing is in our consciousness, to make the intuition of its perfection the animating spirit of life, to devote to this intuition also, immediately, without connection with other purposes, time, institutions and feelings.” Of the questions that attach themselves here—how far do the many individual needs that connect with this general religious need likewise belong to nature? how far is nature able to become master of the contradictions arising in religious life and to resolve them by itself? or does the resolution of them lie outside nature, and can man attain it only through the passivity of faith?—of such questions, which relate to the metaphysical presuppositions, the origin and true sense of religion, Hegel says that their answer may “perhaps find a place elsewhere.”

Finally, the beginning of the work itself. In the reworking Hegel evidently has the manuscripts of the main work of this epoch lying before him; he combines them with the older writing, draws them together,

[p. 134]

supplements them from the latter. It would be pointless to report further on this, because no new thought is added in this extract on the Jewish conditions and on the development of Jesus. Only that Hegel emphatically brings out the admirable religious purity of Jesus: he was free of the spirit of his people; whatever of superstition occurs in him, such as the belief in the dominion of demons over men, does not belong to religion, his soul was independent of accidents, he lived in the one thing necessary, to be holy, to love God and the neighbor. That this religiosity of Jesus could assume a positive character Hegel finds grounded in the relation of authority that arose from the position of the disciples toward Jesus. Here the reworking ends. Hegel may have attempted it in order at least to bring his acquired fundamental historical view to presentation on the most complete manuscript he possessed, since the plan of a change in his situation was certainly already occupying him, since his father’s death, before the letter to Schelling; but he may then have flagged at the difficulty of shaping materials so disparate and so remote from his present standpoint.

#### Two Political Writings.

Everywhere Hegel’s theological labors led beyond religious inquiry to a new religious ideal, a new universally human community of life. The same relation between his historical thinking and his practical direction, between the critique of what exists and the demand for something future, now appears in this period also in the works through which he thought to intervene in political life.

If his ideal lay not in the beyond of an image of Christ and a community of the blessed, but in the intuition of God in the world itself and in the realization of the world’s best in it, then this realization had to be sought first of all in the further development of the orders of the state. From early years this direction toward political progress, strengthened by the French Revolution, was alive in Hegel, as in Fichte, Schelling and Hölderlin. In the poem Eleusis he had already praised the Eleusinian mysteries because they honored the secret of the divine essence and, for them, the Godhead was not present in dogmas

[p. 135]

but in life and in deed. These are precisely the two sides of his ideal of the future: the bringing about of the new worldview and, on its foundation, the new great, public, communal life.

In a note that was probably conceived as the introduction to the pamphlet on the constitution of Germany that he intended, he has expressed fully the ideal that then hovered before him.

Society is in a crisis. Its character is the consciousness of the contradiction of the nation with its existing life. It was the proposition of Rousseau and the consciousness of this whole age of revolution: “the existing life has lost its power and all its dignity”; “all phenomena of this time show that satisfaction is no longer to be found in the old life.” But how Hegel grasps this inner conflict of the time—therein lies the depth proper to him, which stems from the Greek ideal. What was the character of the age that has run its course? “It was a limitation to an orderly dominion over one’s property, a contemplating and enjoying of one’s wholly subservient little world, and then also a self-annihilation and elevation in the thought of heaven that reconciled this limitation.” But now the distress of the time has attacked property, luxury has abolished the order in limitation; the bad conscience of making property and things into the absolute, and with it at the same time the suffering of men, have increased. “A better life has breathed upon this time”; its urge toward new conditions feeds on the doing of great characters among individual men, on the movements of whole peoples, on the presentation of nature and fate by poets,” and pure metaphysics, which proceeds from the connection of the whole, determines for all these limitations of life their necessity and their limits. Thus the time has come in which the dissatisfaction of those who spread out in life meets with the need of those “who have worked nature forth into the idea in themselves.” They want to pass from the idea into life. Hegel expresses, with the hard force proper to him, the condition into which our German culture of that time had brought the greatest minds of the new generation, as they followed upon Goethe and Schiller; we think thereby of Fichte and his nearest disciples, Friedrich Schlegel, Hölderlin, Schleiermacher, Niebuhr. “The condition of man,

[p. 136]

whom the time has driven into an inner world, can be, if he wishes to maintain himself in this one, only a perpetual death, or, if nature drives him to life, only an endeavor to sublate the negative of the existing world, in order to find and enjoy himself in it, in order to be able to live.” It does not suffice for the man who has grasped the idea of nature to present the inner world: “he must also find what is presented as something living.” This inner world must bring forth an external legal order in which it becomes actuality, “power-wielding universality.” That is the point at which his essay on the future constitution of the German Empire sets in.

Only with a mixture of deep veneration and tragic feeling can one read Hegel’s sentences. What a sum of suffering the then impotence and unfreedom of our nation brought to this profound political man; how akin this suffering appears to that which Machiavelli endured; what experiences of the reality around him he must have undergone, until these ideals shriveled in his soul, and how his dream of a realization of the whole inwardness of our intellectual culture in the life of a free national community nevertheless reaches beyond his times into what we are still seeking today! It was a deep historical necessity that the liberation of religiosity through criticism proceeded from him and that the noblest element in social democracy linked itself to him. Despite all the entanglements that fate imposed on him in his metaphysics and his ideal of life, he was a man of the future.

And if one now looks into these political studies themselves, there shows itself in them the same method of determining the ideal of the future that is applied in the theological fragments. At this point Hegel precisely differs from the Enlightenment and from Rousseau. The deepening into the historical and the factual is recognized by him as the only possible way to determine valuable ideals of the future. Thus he separates himself from the reformatory striving of Rousseau and Fichte by his solid historical grounding, and he distinguishes himself from the historical school from the outset by having taken the practical energy of the Enlightenment into himself. Precisely in this Frankfurt period he immerses himself in detail; he follows the English parliamentary debates on the poor rate; he criticizes the provisions of the recently published

[p. 137]

Landrecht on the sharpening of prison sentences; on Steuart’s Political Economy he writes a detailed commentary; he goes into the finest details of the Württemberg constitution and of German imperial law: all detail, however, he permeates with his new great view of the unity of life.

The Hegel of this period knows no external limitation of the mighty striving kindled in the Revolution toward the transformation of the existing order of things; equally, neither at this nor at any other time does he know any reformatory action other than that founded on philosophical and historical consciousness. But he had at that time wholly rejected the retreat into theoretical consciousness; he thought entirely as the System of Ethical Life expresses it: “intellectual intuition is a real one through ethical life and in it alone.”

#### I. The Essay on the Constitution of Württemberg.

Hegel’s notes “on the most recent internal conditions of Wirtemberg, especially on the defects of the magistrate constitution” arose in 1798 in Frankfurt and were communicated in the summer of 1798 to some friends in Stuttgart.

It was the time after the accession of Duke Friedrich. The political life of the country had for a long time been consumed in the struggles between the aspiring princedom and the burgher-clerical oligarchy, as it had survived, as the good old right, from the polity of the old estates. The gifted new duke was filled with the concept of unlimited princely power and thought to bring it to bear. Into this critical situation Hegel’s essay wished to intervene. With a sharp pen he depicts the rule of the estates, the indolent committee of the Diet, guided by the interest of the ruling families, in whose hand the power lay, the busy, self-seeking, intriguing advocates and legal consultants who exploited this power in their personal interest. Yet he by no means took the side of the ducal power. Rather, here too considerations of a more general kind assert themselves, which characterize his whole political thinking of that time.

To be sure, what first determines him are the ideals of the Revolution and their effects in the various European states. “The quiet contentment

[p. 138]

with what is actual, the hopelessness, the patient resignation to a fate too great and all-powerful, has passed over into hope, into expectation, into courage for something else. The image of better, juster times has come vividly into the souls of men, and a longing, a sighing for a purer, freer condition has moved all hearts and set them at variance with actuality.” But these ideas of the Revolution are modified in Hegel by the objective sense, which includes at once the gift of the historian and the talent of the official. He starts from the same proposition of Aristotelian politics that he also exploited in his investigations in the history of religion. “Institutions, constitutions, laws that no longer agree with the customs, the needs, the opinion of men, from which the spirit has fled,” can be maintained in their existence by no artificial means. If they are not changed, upheavals of a violent kind necessarily arise. The strongest moment of his political thinking, however, lay in the model of the living Greek civic disposition. With the best political minds of the time he saw the nearest task for the Germans in overcoming individualism, self-interest and separate life, and in promoting the communal spirit by right measures. The same goal was at that time also determined by Schlözer’s public-spirited, magnificent labors.

It corresponded entirely to this circumspect political thinking that at this time Hegel immersed himself down to the smallest detail in the existing orders, in order to find the moments from which further development could proceed. In all his political utterances of that time the fundamental mood asserts itself through which he, alongside Niebuhr, was to become the reformer of our historical and political thinking. With a correct historical glance he recognizes that the expectations aroused by the Revolution in all Europe keep growing and will finally find their satisfaction. But his political sense teaches him also that in the struggle of interests played out here, individual political measures cannot help. In the spirit of the Platonic and Aristotelian doctrine of the state he sees in justice the only standard, in the courage to practice it the only power, and in the civic disposition, which is able to sacrifice one’s own limited advantage or that of one’s estate to the universal, the only help for the political confusion of his homeland. Against Rousseau he appeals to the parliamentary speeches of Fox, and the reservations

[p. 139]

that were at that time urged against the sudden transition of a population kept in tutelage to the representative system on the French model are also shared by him.

So he now demands for Württemberg, too, a circumspect, gradual realization of the political ideals. He sees clearly that popular elections are incompatible with a condition in which the power of the officials is not limited and the communal spirit not yet awakened; in such a condition the collision of the popular representation with the class of officials would bring about the overthrow of the constitution. Thus it seems to him possible only, for the time being, “to lay the right of election in the hands of a body, independent of the court, of enlightened and upright men.” What Hegel wanted at that time in Württemberg was in accord with what Humboldt and Stein later aimed at in Prussia; only that in Württemberg such an electoral system, built on the social articulation, was difficult given the lack of all foundations.

The essay was a program of the future. With its twofold tendency against the one as against the other of the two parties that were contending for power in Württemberg, it could serve neither; so the friends advised against printing, and it remained manuscript.

#### II. The Essay on the Constitution of Germany.

In Frankfurt Hegel now also, without doubt, conceived the idea of an essay whose subject was the most important political question that a German could at that time put to himself: the future of the German Empire. For this essay stands visibly under the impression of the mighty events that he experienced here. The sad outcome of the first war of the old powers against the French Republic, the Congress of Rastatt, which affected Hölderlin too so deeply, the second war, which after highly promising beginnings ended with Marengo and Hohenlinden, prophesied to him, as to everyone who wished to see, the irresistible collapse of the Empire. Probably the first collections and notes for the essay arose already in Frankfurt; for there was no room, in the pressure of work of the first Jena year, for the deep political-historical studies that are laid down in these manuscripts. The assumption receives its confirmation through a later struck-out

[p. 140]

heading, which Hegel wrote over the beginning of the exposition of his reworking of positive religion. He intended to precede the introduction on the concept of positive religion with a comparison of the process in which living religiosity degenerates under new conditions of spiritual life and becomes positive, with the degeneration of the constitution of the state, in which, only in another domain, the same process takes place, and he refers there to a sketch on this. Thus he had already at that time in Frankfurt brought the two main fields of his historical occupation together under his new historical conception. The executed drafts themselves, to be sure, demonstrably fall in the first period of the stay in Jena. For they already presuppose the Peace of Lunéville, which was signed on 9 February 1801, and have as yet no inkling of the radical changes in the German constitution that the Recess of the Imperial Deputation (Reichsdeputationshauptschluß) of 25 February 1803 brought about; these were essentially settled already in the summer of 1802, indeed were already then in part carried out. Since, further, the northern crisis of the year 1801 and the first Prussian occupation of Hanover are once recalled, the composition of the essay can finally be placed fairly securely in the half-year from the autumn of 1801 to the spring of 1802. In the multiplicity of the other labors and occupations that claimed the author in this first period in Jena, there remained then little leisure for this political work. Moreover, events advanced with irresistible speed. The resolutions of the Imperial Deputation of 1803 struck the ecclesiastical, imperial-city and imperial-knightly territories from the map of Germany, independences on which the author had hitherto entirely counted. They showed besides that the imperial policy of Austria was no less self-interested than that of Prussia; thereby another essential starting point proved false. Thus the essay would in good part have needed a reworking. It did not come to that; it remained lying, like the other one on Württemberg.

1.
