How can Germany again become a state? That is the theme the essay sets itself. For Germany is no longer a state. This assertion has as its presupposition the determination of what the state is, and so the essay takes its place in the development of political

[p. 141]

theory which, from Machiavelli, Bodin, Grotius and Hobbes onward, accompanies the political history of the modern peoples and in Germany, since the days of the Union and the League, can be followed chiefly in the manifold attempts at an interpretation of the transmitted constitution of the Empire. A clarification of the question had meanwhile nevertheless occurred here. On the ground of natural-law deduction, which conceived the essence of the state in sovereignty and grasped this as the legal unlimitedness of state power inward and outward, all effort to construct the German Empire as a state, of whatever form, had proved vain. Pufendorf, who stood on this ground, had been right: the Empire was altogether no normal state. But against the natural-law theory another principle had already been set in Leibniz, which took the real relations as its standard and founded a historical science of the state. Leibniz claimed the character of the state for every political organization that in fact has the power to bring its will to bear inward, but above all outward, toward other “states.” In this power alone he saw the mark of sovereignty. All legal limitations of state power, as they may appear in its incorporation into a higher political association or in its position at home toward provinces, estates, corporations and privileges of every kind, were for him indifferent from this point of view. It was a principle that could not measure itself with natural law in theoretical transparency and applicability, and that also in propagandistic force for the inner development of the state of that time stood far behind it. But it was a genuinely scientific principle. For it alone is equal to the manifoldness of political forms of life that reality has produced or will produce, and since the beginning of the nineteenth century it has worked, in the best minds of our people, now also practically in the direction toward an ideal of the state in which political energy and free cultural development are united and promote one another. Leibniz presented his theory in a writing that was to serve an ephemeral purpose of a small territorial prince: in the eighteenth century the Caesarinus Furstenerius will hardly have been read much any more. The same sense of reality in the conception of the state then spoke from the classic works in which the great king recounted his foreign policy,

[p. 142]

and in Schlözer it found its first representative among the guild scholars. But as the judgment, so too the effect of Frederick for political theory proceeded in the first place from his internal government, in which he appeared as the consummate representative of the natural-law doctrine, and Schlözer’s publicistic activity in general did not like to dwell on scientific investigations, but as a rule took hold of individual conditions and events directly from the practical side. Only this essay of Hegel’s accomplishes the theoretical restoration and further development of Leibniz’s thoughts. That the author was conscious of this relation cannot be demonstrated and probably cannot even be conjectured. The energy of this thinker, boring into the depths, together with the bright eye of his stock for everything that is practice, politics, suffices to explain the peculiar kinship of ideas. And one lived again, as in the seventeenth century, in a hard time, in which the significance of political realities forced itself upon one inexorably. Existence was again founded on power.

“A multitude of men can call itself a state only if it is united for the common defense of the whole of its property. It goes, strictly, without saying here, but it is necessary to note, that this union has not merely the intention of defending itself, but that it, let the power and the success be what it will, defends itself by actual warding off.” These sentences, which repeat themselves in ever new turns, designate for Hegel the essence of the state. It is thus not enough that the common defense is prescribed by constitution and laws. In the state-union the means thereto must rather also lie. A common “military and state power” must be present that is in a position to fulfill this purpose. In the end, whether a state is a state or not is decided only by the great test: war.

And this mark of common defense and of the power thereto exhausts the essence of the state. Hegel excludes one after another all other alleged marks of the state as indifferent for the concept and thereby gains the possibility of recognizing as a state every political association that fulfills that single essential presupposition, however its legal relation to other states or its own organization may be constituted. These closer relations and institutions

[p. 143]

of the states “belong not in the sphere of the necessary, but, for the concept, in the sphere of the more or less better, for actuality, however, in the sphere of chance and caprice.” That is to say, they differ according to the historical conditions under which a state forms and develops, according to the character of the people united in it, according to the capacities of its leading estates or persons, according to its particular inner and outer tasks and fates. The purpose of the state can indeed be realized in the most manifold manner. State power can be placed in the hand of one man or of a larger or smaller number of persons, and birth or election can entitle to its exercise. Serfs, burghers, noblemen, princes who themselves again have subjects, can come together as subjects of the same state, and rights as well as duties toward the whole can be very differently distributed among the individual estates. Other differences can attach to the geographical articulation of the state; old France was a state, although every province and almost every city showed its own laws and customs. How the administration and its individual branches are arranged, whether everything from top to bottom is placed in the hand of the actual officials of the state or room is left for the self-administration of urban or local associations, does not touch the essence of the state at all. And if we compare the ancient republics with the modern states, a difference leaps at once to the eye: Athens or Rome could not have subsisted without the inner bond of a people in language, religion, custom and culture; the same holds even today for every small state. The modern large states, by contrast, embrace, like the Roman empire of old, men of different descent, language, religion and culture. The gravity of the whole and the spirit and art of the organization of the state bring about this connection, “so that inequality of culture and of customs [is] a necessary product as well as a necessary condition that the modern states will subsist.” In particular, the separation of state power and state interests from ecclesiastical and religious tendencies is characteristic of the modern state.

“According to the theories of the state, to be sure, which in our times have been set up partly by would-be philosophers and teachers of the rights of mankind, partly realized in monstrous political experiments, is—only

[p. 144]

the most important of all, language, culture, customs and religion, excepted—everything else subjected to the immediate activity of the highest state power.” Certainly the state power must reserve to itself the supreme supervision over the inner relations of the people and their organizations arisen by historical caprice, with the right to change them as soon as they endanger the nearest purpose of the state-union. But it is a great advantage of the old states of Europe, especially of its old monarchies, that they, in possession of a surely and steadily working state power, leave a wide field to the citizens’ own activity in administration, administration of justice, economy, education, social and ecclesiastical welfare. This self-activity within the state is best able to replace the ancient ideal of the immediate participation of every free man in the direction of the state, which in view of the size of modern states can no longer be realized, and it fulfills the same high political and moral functions. For those other philosophers and rulers, on the contrary, the state is “a machine with a single spring, which communicates movement to all the other infinite wheelwork; from the highest state power all institutions that the essence of a society brings with it are to proceed, be regulated, commanded, supervised, directed.” In the whole territory of the state “every morsel, from the soil that produces it to the mouth, is to be led in a line that is examined, calculated, corrected and commanded by state and law and government.” The people is here treated “with reason and according to necessity, not with trust and freedom.” So trust and freedom cannot develop among the subjects either. What a “leathern, spiritless life” will be produced in such a modern state, “in which everything is regulated from above down,” the French Republic will teach, if indeed “this tone of the pedantry of ruling can remain.” “But what life and what barrenness prevails in another, equally regulated state, in the Prussian, that strikes everyone who enters the first village of it or sees its complete lack of scientific and artistic genius or does not regard its strength by the ephemeral energy to which a single genius knew how to force it up for a time.”

In this one-sided, unhistorical, but factually correct judgment on the Frederician state there makes itself felt, after all, not only the opposition

[p. 145]

of the Swabian, who remembers his popular liberties. There rises the consciousness of the historical school, which defends the independences grounded in historical life against enlightened absolutism and wishes to employ them as a political force. They are the thoughts and demands of Stein, Niebuhr, Schleiermacher at the renewal of the Prussian state. And the whole sharpness, too, of these men’s opposition to revolutionary France and its consummator already announces itself here in Hegel.

2.

By the standard of this modern concept of the state Hegel now measures the German Empire. He examines its military and financial constitution and shows that in fact they do not suffice for their task, the common defense. Germany is thus no longer a state. It has also had to experience this bitter truth again and again since the Thirty Years’ War, last and most clearly in the struggle with modern France.

This fact is eliminated by no constitutional-law deduction, however skillful. The German teachers of public law accordingly no longer dare, as a rule, to claim the Empire as a “state.” They would at once have to draw many consequences which they may not admit if they do not wish to put themselves in open contradiction with imperial law. But since the Empire is now also not to count as a non-state, they take refuge in expressions like “imperial association,” “imperial head.” As “legal titles” one can recognize these expressions; as concepts of public law that designate a living political relation they are not. As “imperial head” the emperor is “thrown into One category with the former Doge of Venice and the Turkish Sultan. For these two are likewise heads of a state, but the former the most restricted head of an aristocracy, the latter the most unrestricted of a despotism.”

The contradiction that the German Empire, with its constitution elaborated to the last details and externalities, seems to be a state and yet is no state, can be resolved only by conceiving it as a “state of thought.” The essence of this “state of thought” lies in this, that all members are linked by a system of political rights which, as they were “acquired” by individuals, are conceived as their

[p. 146]

“property.” Over against them there remains no room for a general public law, or it counts at any rate only secondarily. Necessarily, in such cases, the constitution develops further solely in the direction of finding ever new safeguards for this “political property.” The German Empire is the perfect state of thought. With a wonderful skill in showing political institutions in their functions, Hegel sets out how here the general activity of the state makes the impression of the highest liveliness, but in fact is regularly, from the first step to the last, paralyzed by the precisely circumscribed rights of the estates. An example of particular tragicomedy in this respect is formed by the work of the imperial courts. The German teacher of public law may acutely prove that this chronic unfruitfulness of the imperial activity is “lawful.” But as a constitution of the state the German imperial constitution, “this Urbarium [land register] of the most various rights of the state acquired in the manner of private law,” cannot be recognized. For it is “a pure thing of thought,” it does not work. Sharply the new historical school, with its eminent political understanding, separates itself in these sentences from that old one—to which it otherwise owes so much—of Moser and Pütter, who remained caught in the credulous veneration of the legal system of the old Empire.

3.

Beyond the practical purpose of the moment this critique of the German constitution maintains its value in that Hegel combines it with a historical justification of this constitution. He shows how it arose, how it too once lived, how it developed, how for a time it adapted itself with indestructible force to new conditions, how it finally succumbed to overpowering circumstances, and how it nevertheless fulfilled important functions at all times, political ones, to be sure, more in the service of the foreign nations that took it over and practically developed it further: to us it preserved in return imperishable ethical goods, the sense for right and fidelity, the understanding of personality, the pride of independence, the capacity for one’s own action—qualities which all the despotism of the bureaucratic state has not been able to break, and finally our Protestantism. With this fine feeling for the sense of historical development Hegel stands as an equal beside Möser.

[p. 147]

He surpasses him whenever the measures widen and it is a matter of the understanding of great political events and persons.

In seeking to explain the political misery of Germany historically, Hegel finds the deepest ground in our national character; this determines the individual to isolate himself, fearless and willful he stands on himself. And if at first “an inner connection of hearts” held the whole together, then the development of the cities, the religious split and the rise of the princely sovereignties constantly increased the independence of the individual political forces.

In a fine comparative consideration Hegel pursues at this point the effect that the feudal system had in the states founded by the Germans. He sees in it—and here too he recalls Möser—the first expression of this form of freedom of the Germanic peoples, in a time in which they flooded Europe and the rest of the world. Here he finds the concept of representation laid down. He calls it the silliest fancy when representation has been held to be the invention of the most recent time. In England it has come to unfolding; its degeneration has destroyed the constitution of France; from Germany it has come; “but it is a higher law that that people from which a new universal impulse is given to the world in the end itself perishes before all others, and its principle, but not it itself, subsists.” The political future of Europe Hegel already then saw in the development of the representative constitutions, and indeed then, as always, on their national historical foundations. “This system of representation is the system of all the more recent European states. It has not been in Germania’s forests, but it has proceeded from them. It made an epoch in world history. The connection of the culture of the world has led the human race, after oriental despotism and the rule of a republic over the world, from the degeneration of the latter into the middle between both, and the Germans are the people from which this third universal shape of the world-spirit was born.”

In England, France, Spain it succeeded for the monarchy in bringing the elements fermenting in their interior to union, and from there dates the period of the power and wealth of these states. Hegel

[p. 148]

emphasizes the significance of Richelieu. Here already there appears a proposition that became significant for Hegel himself and then for Ranke. Political genius consists in the individual’s identifying himself with a political principle; in this union he must necessarily carry off the victory: by binding his person to the necessary principle of the unity of the state, Richelieu came to victory over the nobles and the religious parties.

In contrast to these states, Italy and Germany did not attain unity. In speaking of Italy Hegel recalls Machiavelli, and with a profound glance comprehends the harsh power politics of this great political thinker from the goal of the rescue of Italy. Hegel proceeds, like Machiavelli, from the idea of the national state, and here he meets, as Machiavelli did, with the classical ideals which he otherwise pursued. “Freedom is possible only in the lawful union of a people into a state”; all the misery that Germany endured from the Seven Years’ War on, “the experience of the French frenzy for freedom,” has nonetheless not been able to help this truth to victory. It is once more one of the fundamental thoughts of the historical school that finds expression here. The highest duty of the state is self-preservation, and what would be a crime in private life can here become a duty; “gangrenous limbs cannot be healed with lavender water.” The decisive points of view in the expositions of Gervinus, Mohl and Ranke on Machiavelli are here already indicated in Hegel.

The fate of Germany is kindred to that of Italy. Like the latter, Germany too has for centuries been the scene of internal wars and of the wars of foreign powers. Robbed, insulted, despised, it sees its affairs decided by foreign countries. And here too this fate is the effect of the dissolution of the national bond into the plenitude of political independences, whose egoism excludes every common action. But there lies in this nonetheless an essential difference in the development of the two nations. In Italy the process of dissolution advanced early to the last parts; for it was accomplished at a time when the smallest political bodies could assert themselves even against the greatest, as Milan against the Emperor, Venice against the League of Cambrai. The sovereignty of the German states was decided in essence later, when such a possibility

[p. 149]

no longer existed. Thus here the dissolution was accompanied by the formation of new nuclei around which the parts gathered again. It is to these that the restoration of Germany too will attach itself.

4.

How will this restoration of Germany be accomplished? How will Germany become a state again?

When, since the days of Leibniz, German patriots who despaired of the revival of the traditional constitution of the Empire itself put this question to themselves, they thought of a new union of the German states, founded by the magnanimous resolve of all the participants, in which the nearest end of the state, protection within and without, would be secured through an effective concentration of the military and financial forces, but the independence and equality of the members would for the rest remain recognized. The German Confederation of 1815 these men would have regarded roughly as the realization of their hopes. The idealists among them then dreamed further, no doubt, that this confederation of states, or federal state, would one day become the bearer of a mighty national culture.

Hegel declares that the future of Germany does not lie in this direction. He calls to mind how violently Prussia lately dealt with the Estates of the Empire that had entrusted themselves to its protection, or France with its “allied” republics. From these experiences there results for him the harsh lesson: the possibility of a union of small and large states on the footing of any sort of equality of rights is, in the long run, absolutely excluded. Small political bodies beside large ones have today only the choice between ruin and subordination. And so the question narrows itself for him clearly and definitely to this: will Austria or Prussia accomplish the restoration of Germany?

Hegel weighs the prospects of the two German great powers and decides in the end, though very cautiously, for Austria. Therein lie the temporal limits of his political thinking. But who, once the question had been so posed, would at that time have cared to give a different answer? Prussia had, for Hegel, placed itself completely outside the common German interests. Austria’s old con-

[p. 150]

nection with Germany, on the other hand, has been strengthened by the most recent wars. Even if the small German states certainly have not much less to fear from Austria than from Prussia for their freedom, Prussia is nonetheless the more dangerous opponent, because it has the greater need of power still. And everything that formerly drove the German states away from Austria and toward Prussia, the apprehension of universal monarchy, the fears for Protestantism, has now become an empty specter, which only the Prussian publicists may still make use of. The strongest aversion to Prussia must nonetheless be aroused by the mechanism of its administration, which kills all life. But the German state of the future requires, like the military hegemony of a single prince, so also the participation of the people by means of a national representation. In Prussia the historical corporations of Estates, on the analogy of which this national representation will one day form itself, have been annihilated. In the Austrian states they have maintained themselves and have shown only recently that they have understanding for the needs of the modern state. Austria offers the better prospects for the insertion of the element of freedom into the structure of the new Empire.

However it may come about, whether Austria or Prussia may bring unity to the nation: only a great war, a conqueror, will accomplish this work. This Theseus would then have to give, magnanimously, to the people he has created a share in the commonwealth through a representative constitution. The whole relation between this prophetic insight, which his essay would like to communicate, and the realization itself the great political-historical thinker sums up in the words: concept and insight must be justified through force; only then does man submit to them. If one takes these sentences together with Hegel’s exposition on Machiavelli, the right to the assumption becomes plain that he himself would gladly have become, in this essay, Germany’s Machiavelli, only more fortunate and more successful than the great Florentine.

In the consciousness of the significance of the great man for political life, beside the doctrine of Machiavelli the impression of the personality of Napoleon makes itself felt. In this genius there dawned upon Hegel at that time the relation that obtains between the spirit of an age and the world-historical individual. Thus he says in a Jena lecture: “These sober natures do nothing but pronounce the word, and the peoples

[p. 151]

will cling to them.” They must be exalted above the terrors of the chaotic world and free from the fetters of ethical actuality. With this his philosophy of history agrees: the historical power of these individuals consists in their foreseeing the next stage of world development; they put their prodigious energy into this end, they shatter whatever hinders them on their way—they are not subject to the moral tribunal of the moralists, but the region in which they nest lies above this tribunal. And when he now demands for Germany the political genius who unites it by force and martial success, and then gives it a representative constitution, he probably still hoped at that time for a similar renunciation from the Consul Bonaparte.

Hegel’s essay remained unfinished. Among the treatments of the question of the future of the German Empire it would have taken the same eminent position that the writings of Gentz of that time hold in the appraisal of the relation of the political forces of Europe. If these are superior to Hegel through their topicality, that was nevertheless also conditioned by their tasks. Hegel could at that time only indicate the direction in which the resolution of the question of our future had to be accomplished. And the reproach of a contemplative quietism that has been made against him wholly misjudges the mood, plan and character of the political essay on which he was then working. The factors that hindered its completion have been set forth. So these fragments now stand there as a monument of a boundless longing in the German political minds of those days, but at the same time as a sign of the immense superiority which the successful, felicitous erection of a purely spiritual realm of culture in the Weimar and Jena of this period and in the whole German world, filled with these spiritual ideals, possessed over against the nearest and so urgent political need.

#### The New Worldview and the Beginnings of the System.

There now arises the task of discerning, behind the fragments that have passed by us and whose subjects and ultimate goal were of a theological-historical nature, the worldview that unfolded in Hegel. The task is difficult, for individual passages that belong to quite different

[p. 152]

places in the manuscripts must be connected with one another, and since these individual utterances occur in the interpretation of religious texts or concepts, they lack the scientific rigor, connection and grounding which a systematic-philosophical presentation would have given them. Yet even as they now lie before us they permit us to perceive some essential features of Hegel’s system in the making. I limit my task to this, and renounce the attempt to infer, by means of a relative chronology of the individual fragments, the stages of Hegel’s new worldview. It lies in the nature of the case that for the three parts which the system contains, mystical pantheism, the history of religion and the ideal, the theological fragments supply most of the material, and the political writings could be used almost only for the ideal.

#### I. Mystical Pantheism.

I take as my point of departure those of the fragments which express Hegel’s mystical pantheism of that time most clearly and whose date can at the same time be established externally, and so seek to determine the connection of metaphysical thoughts which still came to Hegel in the Frankfurt period and in the study of the theological problems; into this connection I then fit the earlier, isolated passages that are found in the theological fragments of this period.

If the religiosity which, according to Hegel, was at that time the highest stage of insight into the connection of things finds its final consummation in the grasp of the unity in which all separations and limits of the finite, the particular, are sublated—of the whole, the infinite: then precisely here, in the unity which excludes everything opposed and in which nonetheless all oppositions are preserved, Hegel’s basic metaphysical view of this period is also to be sought. To this correspond all the metaphysical statements in the whole range of these theological fragments.

Hegel determines the character of all reality through the concept of life. Life is for him the relation of the parts to the whole, according to which, isolated from the whole, they can neither exist nor

[p. 153]

be thought. The character of life consists in this, that a manifold is there, but is, and can be understood, only in this connection, as a sum of parts in a whole. From this basic concept of life as the whole that comprehends the manifold in its unity it follows that the concepts of whole, part, unity, separation, opposition, union dominate Hegel’s thinking of that time. If one raises these concepts of Hegel’s to logical consciousness, which at that time had not yet happened in any passage I can recall, it may be said that he thinks reality under the categories of whole and part: world-totality is the concept in which the connection of reality becomes graspable for him: a concept which then attained conscious logical presentation only in Hegel’s next period. In an original metaphysical mind a certain way of perceiving is the first thing; it gives color and tone to everything that proceeds from him, and in the course of his development there arise logical consciousness, grounding and systematic elaboration of what is contained in his way of seeing reality.

These concepts, by which Hegel constructs the real, now prove to be the categories contained in the connection of consciousness, as transcendental philosophy had determined them. However differently Kant, Fichte and Schelling may order the categories of unity, plurality, combination, separation, whole and part, they all nonetheless conceive them as the forms of thinking through which reality is comprehensible, and from Kant on the separation of the categories of the understanding, as a lower stage, from those of the totality of reality, as a higher, is already laid down. These categories, however, now receive a depth of their own through Hegel’s lived experience and his historical re-understanding. His metaphysical experience made the history of religion intelligible to him, and when he then brought certain sides of Christianity to historical knowledge from this standpoint, the very ones whose significance at the same time Schelling, Schleiermacher and the later Fichte also divined: so there came to him at once from this kindred element in Christianity a considerable furtherance of his metaphysics; the Gospel of John above all exercised the strongest effect upon him too. By the deepening of the categories thus arising into the accompanying states of the heart Hegel is distinguished from Schelling or Schleiermacher and enters into a relation to the mystics; he once copied out passages from Eckhart and Tauler which

[p. 154]

he encountered; with the Neoplatonists he found points of contact, and in the same direction his own constitution of soul in this time worked. It grew ever lonelier around him, the more his thinking removed itself from the philosophy of those days as from its theology, and with that his feeling for the relations of life grew ever more sensitive. In such a situation of the spirit Hegel gave to the connection of the categories, which transcendental philosophy had created, a new significance through the transposition of the heart into the concepts of thinking that is peculiar to mysticism. The longing for complete union, which speaks from the soft features of the images of Christ, from the hymns of the church, from the writings of the mystics, was renewed in his soul; separation he felt as pain, in every opposition he felt a suffering proper to it; “in misfortune the separation is present, there we feel ourselves as objects and must flee to what determines, in happiness this separation has vanished”; in the logical-metaphysical relation of transcendence he grasped separation and opposition in the heart, which tears apart the connection of the inner world. Orders of concepts thus point back for him to a constitution of the soul; it is as if with the concepts a tone were heard along in the soul. Through his whole thinking runs the consciousness of the kinship of the categories with the constitutions of the soul. With this another deep feature of his worldview is connected. The history of religion shows him an increase of the religious value that lies in union, but union is always there only at what is separated, and separation is pain: so the consciousness of the limit and of the opposition in pain proves to him the condition to which the overcoming of the limit is bound. This significance of pain is a new feature which Hegel adds within the history of philosophy since Leibniz, and in this too his kinship with the mystics appears. So he grasps world and history in the totality of all the forces of the soul. It is perhaps Hegel’s most peculiar basic trait among the modern thinkers—a charm goes forth from this and seizes us even today in his language.

At this point there confronts us a new moment in the remarkable relation of Hegel and Hölderlin. Hölderlin, when he wrote the first volume of his Hyperion, which appeared for the Easter fair of 1797, was beyond question unacquainted with Hegel’s ideas as they are contained in his papers.

[p. 155]

For Hegel had come to Frankfurt only in January 1797, and by letter these thoughts surely did not reach Hölderlin. This first volume of the Hyperion, however, contains pantheistic speculations that stand close to those of Hegel’s manuscripts. Hölderlin proceeds, as an artist, from the value and the beauty of life. In nature, in heroes, artists and thinkers the soulful depth of the world is revealed; this is unity of the manifold, love and beauty. But the divine, in the world, comes under the laws of succession, and so there arise dissatisfaction, separation, unblessedness; the One falls apart into plurality, and so strife becomes the form of life. The many are, as individuals, each separated from the other, and with that the richness and force of life, the advance to the higher, are bound to the energy of suffering. The objective, logically graspable relations of unity, separation, opposition, plurality and finally of union have in themselves at the same time the side of the life of feeling, in suffering and blessedness. “Reconciliation is in the midst of strife, and everything separated finds itself again.” In all these traits Hölderlin and Hegel are most closely akin, Hölderlin is in them independent of Hegel, and the only question can be whether the chronology of Hegel’s fragments makes possible the assumption of a development of these traits in Hegel’s conception of the world with Hölderlin’s co-operation, or whether the two heavy-blooded and profound Swabians developed the same ideas on the basis of kindred thinkers. From the standpoint of objective idealism they both grasp the two-sidedness of life. It would be interesting to compare Novalis with them in this.

1.

These are the traits proper to all the fragments of this epoch of Hegel. They confront us most clearly in two manuscripts whose sheet-marking shows that we are dealing with the fragments of a larger work of Hegel’s. The one of them formed the conclusion and bears the date of its completion, 14 September 1800; that was some months before Hegel’s departure from Frankfurt. And about the same time falls the revision of the manuscript on the positivity of religion, which likewise must be especially considered in what follows. I begin with Hegel’s discussion of the metaphysical principle, as it is contained in the first of the fragments just named.

[p. 156]

Hegel proceeds from the intuition of life. This is “of infinite manifoldness, infinite opposition and infinite relation; as plurality an infinite plurality of organizations, individuals, as unity a single organized separated and united whole—nature.”

He then determines this life further as spirit. “The infinite life can be called a spirit, in opposition (to) abstract plurality, for spirit is the living oneness of the manifold in opposition to the same as its shape, not in opposition to the same as a dead, mere plurality separated from it; for then it would be the mere unity that is called law and is something merely thought, lifeless. Spirit is animating law in union with the manifold, which then is something animated.” In these sentences the logical relation according to which the unity of the manifold is set apart from it in a universal and posited as something thought, as law, that is, the relation of the subordination of the particular under the universal of the law thought in addition, is distinguished from the relation of whole to part. And when this latter relation is applied, there arises the concept of a living unity that dwells in the manifold and shapes it. Every part, as in connection with the animating unity, is then organ, and the whole is “the infinite all of life.” The “animating law” that holds together in this all the individual lives or organs is spirit. This important passage shows that Hegel determines the essence of spirit from the beginning from the logical category of the whole, of totality; it has for its presupposition that the comprehending of a manifold in a unity is precisely the essence of spirit, as it is lived through in the nexus of the individual spirit and then, detached from lived experience as a form of logical relations, makes up everywhere the mark of spirit. With this the fundamental thought of Hegel’s philosophy is pronounced. From it there follows consistently, in Hegel’s further development, the logical character of the universe as the manifestation of spirit; logic must be for Hegel the presentation of spirit according to its formal relations; in nature spirit presents itself as in its otherness, in the subjective spirit as in its being-with-itself, and in community as in its objectivation: everywhere the same system of relations, the same texture, as it were, which as such is spirit

[p. 157]

wherever it is—as lived experience in a soul, but just as well as the system of relations in an animal organism or in law.

Hegel further delimits the concepts life and nature from one another, and here his further view of the process in the all-life confronts us. “Nature is a positing of life, for into life reflection has brought its concepts of relation and separation, of the individual, subsisting for itself, and of the universal, the connected, the former thus a limited, the latter an unlimited, and has made it nature by positing.” Here reflection designates a real process in nature itself; positing, opposition, reflection in which the individual distinguishes itself from the universal, are processes of the all-life in which this becomes nature. In this sense Hegel continues: “Life as infinity of the living, or as an infinity of shapes, in this way as nature is an infinitely finite, an unlimitedly limited.” Nature is not itself the all-life, but a “life, though treated in the most worthy manner, fixed” by reflection. Thus the infinite life has in the living its expressions and presentations. It is an organized whole. This organized whole embraces a boundless plurality of living things, and every living thing is again “an infinite plurality, because it is living.”

If one now separates such a living thing, a part of the infinite life, from the other parts and grasps it insofar as it is relation, “having its being only as union,” if one abstracts from the separation of plurality in it: so there arises the concept of individuality. This concept, however, includes in itself, just as much as the opposition to the infinite manifoldness, also the connection with it. Man as individual life is at once separated from all life outside him and one with it—“he is only insofar as the all of life is divided, he the one part, everything else the other part, he is only insofar as he is no part and nothing is separated from him.” So man is a presentation of the all-life.

This infinite life cannot be brought in thinking to perfect cognition. For the relation between the thinking life and nature, as what is given to contemplation, resulted as the foundation of all knowing; but this relation contains an opposition insurmountable for thinking—namely precisely that between thinking

[p. 158]

and the infinite life. Consequently cognition is unable to grasp the infinite life, since it is unable to overcome the opposition of subject and object.

The same is evident from the contradiction into which thinking falls when it sets itself the task of conceiving the world-totality. Its concept must exclude nothing, it must express its essence without any abstraction. This demands that life “be regarded not as union, relation alone, but at the same time as opposition.” The task that thus arises is not soluble; in that thinking posits something, it excludes something else. If I think the relation, if I grasp the manifold only as organ of the all-life, then the opposition, which is nevertheless contained in life, is excluded in it. And if I now say that it is a union of opposition and relation, then non-union would nonetheless be excluded by that. So I arrive at the self-contradictory formula: “life is the union of union and non-union.” The inadequacy of reflection to the infinite whole also shows itself in this, that it fixes its parts “as resting, subsisting, as fixed points, as individuals.”

If thus the thinking of the world-totality never succeeds, then the infinite life can be possessed only in that the finite life raises itself to the infinite life. This is the process of religion. The limit, which cannot be overcome in thinking, since this always excludes in the positing, “thus has an opposition, partly of the non-thinking, partly of the thinking and the thought,” is sublated only within life itself in this elevation of the limited life to the infinite. Were the finite only understanding and not itself life, then the elevation to the infinite would be excluded. “Philosophy has to point out in everything finite the finitude and to demand through reason the completion of the same,” “it must for that very reason end with religion.”

Hegel wished moreover to show expressly, as Schleiermacher has done in another way, that the philosophy of Fichte has found no way to grasp the infinite life. Fichte too remains in the opposition of the subjective and the objective. The procedure that “adds to the limited the limiting, recognizes this again as something posited, itself as something limited, and anew for the same the limiting

[p. 159]

seeks out, and makes the demand to continue this into the infinite,” never attains precisely the totality that lies beyond the oppositions. Precisely in the exposition of this the fragment breaks off.

2.

I now attempt to show how the new metaphysics runs through the other theological fragments of this period as well; for only so does insight into their organic growth arise. Here we are dependent on utterances that are scattered through the theological-historical presentation; many of them have already been touched upon in the connection of the manuscripts.

As the basic concept of his philosophy of that time there proves to be here too that of life. Every being he designates as a modification of life. “Life is not different from life, because life is in the one Godhead.” For God is pure life, that which subsists in transience and is One in plurality and yet the ground of transience and plurality. As we saw, Hegel, in the introduction to the discussion of the Johannine prologue, sought the origin of this concept, in the sense of Fichte and of the earlier Schelling, in the I. The universal of conscious actions we call character; but if one sublates every determinateness whatever, such as constitutes the individual, then what remains is pure life. With this it is said, in Fichte’s sense, that through the I we enter into the realm of the timelessly One; the subject, which always feels itself determined, suffering, in the relations to the many and the changing, does not get beyond the intuition of that which governs this play of forces; but when spirit grasps in itself the changelessly One, the access to Godhead is opened to it here. Beside Hegel, Schleiermacher in the Monologues and Schopenhauer sought on the same path, from the I, the entrance into the metaphysical world. Yet I find for it in Hegel only this one passage; otherwise in the manuscripts life, the unity contained in it, the manifold posited in it, are introduced without such reference back to the pure I. Further properties of life are now touched upon in them. To life itself belongs fate as the reaction of the living against its violation: “Fate is incorruptible and unbounded like life.” Life separates itself in itself,

[p. 160]

and so its individual modifications arise. “Being must be considered in two respects, once as the One, in which there is no division, opposition, and at the same time with the possibility of separation, of the infinite division of the One.” Thus the unity has, by means of division, the manifoldness of the actual in itself. “The manifoldness, the infinity of the actual, is the infinite division as actual.” From this there results the nature of every single being within this manifoldness. “Every part, outside of which is the whole, is at the same time a whole, a life, and this life again, also as a reflected one, also in respect of the division of the relation of subject and predicate, is life.” Again and again in other forms he expresses it that single being may not be thought as a single substance, but is inseparate from the life of the whole. “The individual, limited, as something opposed, dead, is at the same time a branch of the infinite tree of life.” “Only of objects, of dead things, does it hold that the whole is something other than the parts, in the living, on the contrary, the part of it is just as much, and the same One, as the whole.” Often he treats the relation of the universal and the particular in its bearing on that of whole and part, which he lays at the foundation of the understanding of the world. So in his critique of the doctrine of virtue he shows how the former relation, as abstract and merely thought, tears reality apart, instead of grasping its unity; it lifts a rule out of it and leaves life behind in it.

The feeling of the living is love. In it the connection of life itself comes to expression. To let the mysteriousness of its essence be seen, he cites the saying of Julie: “The more I give, the more I have.” Like Schleiermacher he once treated the problem of shame, and he finds in it the indignation of love at individuality: “Love is indignant at what is still separated, at a property.” “In love the separated is still there, but no longer as separated, rather as one, and the living feels the living. In love the whole is not contained as in the sum of many particular, separated things. In it life itself finds itself, as a doubling of itself and oneness of the same. Life has run through the circle from undeveloped oneness, by way of culture, to a completed oneness.” A sentence that is found in very similar form in Hölderlin’s first Hyperion fragment. As love is the consciousness of life and at the same time

[p. 161]

the reality of ethical life, and indeed as the sublation of the opposition that exists at the stage of morality, so the true ethical life, which comes to presentation in the utterances of love, lies beyond the commandments of morals, the relations of guilt and punishment, the categories of dominion and obedience. In the sphere of this true ethical life, which realizes itself in community, there exist only the relations posited in life itself, of the violation of it, of the reaction of fate and of reconciliation with this through love. Here too Hegel again meets Hölderlin, who likewise in his Empedocles brought to expression the relations of life lying beyond all morality: of heroism, of the unavoidable violation of foreign life by it, of the reaction of fate, of its reconciliation through love. In this connection of concepts there lies then the deepest ground for Hegel’s concept, appearing soon after, of the realization of ethical life in a connection of life animated by it. Life, the consciousness of it in love, and the objectification in life of the ethical life posited in love: in this connection consists the first schema of Hegel’s realm of ethical life. “In every truly free people each is a part, but at the same time the whole;” “each individual bears the whole of the state in himself.”

And at various places the determination of the infinite life as spirit confronts us—with that the principle of this system in the making works its way into the light. He finds this basic concept of his system again in the Gospel of John. In the presentation of the teaching of Jesus in John he brings out how this Gospel grasps the divine and Jesus’ union with it most deeply—consequently the essence of spirit. “But,” he continues, “the Jewish culture, so poor in spiritual relations, compelled him to make use, for what is most spiritual, of objective connections, of a language of actuality, which for that reason often sounds harsher than if feelings were to be expressed in the style of mutual exchange. The Kingdom of Heaven, to go into the Kingdom of Heaven, I am the door, I am the right food, he who eats of the flesh, etc.: into such connections of meager actuality the spiritual is forced, and nowhere more than here is it necessary to grasp with one’s own deep spirit, nowhere is it less possible than here to learn, to take something in passively, since this objective language of the spiritual,

[p. 162]

but understood in its form of concepts of actuality, unhinges the spirit.” Thus the consciousness Jesus had of God as the spirit behind its imperfect expression must be sought. And as Hegel grounds faith in Jesus upon the equality of the spiritual in Jesus, God and the believing man, so here too he brings out the moment of unity contained in spirit, which makes union through religion possible. “Because the divine is pure life, then necessarily, when one speaks of it, what is said of it must contain nothing opposed within it;” “for the effect of the divine is only a union of spirits; only spirit grasps and includes spirit in itself—expressions such as commanding, teaching, learning, seeing, recognizing, making, willing, coming into the Kingdom of Heaven, going, express only relations of the objective, if it is a taking-in of an objective in a spirit. Of the divine one can therefore speak only in enthusiasm.” “Whoever separates himself from the divine, blasphemes nature itself, the spirit in it, his spirit has destroyed the holy in itself.”

The all-life can never be completely expressed by the understanding. The reflection of the understanding meets here its limit. “Reflection, which separates life, can distinguish it into infinite and finite, and only the limitation, the finite considered for itself, gives the concept of man as opposed to the divine; outside reflection, in truth, it does not take place.” “Everything expressed about the divine in the form of reflection is nonsensical, and passive, spiritless reception of it not only leaves the deeper spirit empty, but for that reason also unhinges the understanding, which takes it in and to which it is contradiction.” Thus the beginning of the Johannine prologue expresses in the language of reflection only relations between God, Logos, life, and it must not be understood as a sequence of judgments whose predicates would be universal concepts. One must translate these words back from the language of reflection into that of life, in order to grasp what is meant in them. In the problem of the Trinity Hegel seeks to make clear that in the divine life itself everything is connection, as in a tree roots, trunk and leaves: this connection is not grasped when reflection conceives the parts as particular objects and these objects as substances, each with its property as an individual in numbers: so there arises only a concept which

[p. 163]

unites the particular into a unity. And in the doctrine of the two natures of Jesus he shows how the demand upon the understanding “to grasp absolutely different substances and at the same time an absolute unity of them” is unfulfillable for logical thinking; but if this difference of the substances is retained and their unity denied, then the understanding is indeed saved: “but if they stop at this absolute difference of the beings, then they raise the understanding, the absolute separation, the killing, to the highest of spirit.” So Hegel shows the limit of the understanding in religious intuitions too, which are for him only symbols for the connection of the divine life itself.

The concept of development Hegel uses in these fragments first of all to describe the way of the individual from undeveloped unity through culture to the union of what is separated; “the completion of faith, the return to the Godhead from which man was born, closes the circle of his development.” He then designates development as a property of life. “Because love is a union of life, it presupposed separation, a development, a cultivated many-sidedness of the same.” The Kingdom of God he characterizes by this, that in it “all relations have proceeded in living fashion from the development of life.” He speaks of the “modifications, stages of development,” of the “consequences and developments of the original fate” of the Jews, and at the beginning of Jewish history also of the “development of the human race.” Yet in such chance utterances, which follow the usage of some writers of the Enlightenment, especially Herder, there is no trace to be noticed of a conceptual formulation and systematic exploitation of the concept of development.

If we now summarize the concepts by which Hegel determines the world-totality, it shows itself here too, as later in his conception of history, that his peculiarity is the comprehending and holding together of what has been historically won in his consciousness. His objective profundity assimilates everywhere whatever is akin to his spiritual constitution, without any need to assert his subjectivity in polemic. No citations enable us to establish how Shaftesbury, Hemsterhuis, Herder acted upon him; but the kinship with their ideas makes itself felt everywhere to the knowledgeable, and especially Hegel’s

[p. 164]

mystical pantheism stands just as much under Herder’s influence as his philosophical view of history. The doctrine, represented by these writers, of the organic animating force in the universe then enters under the clear concepts of the whole determined by purpose in Kant’s Critique of Judgment and of the spiritual connection in Fichte and in Schelling. Precisely under the same influences stood, besides Hegel and apart from Schelling, above all Hölderlin and Schleiermacher. According to Hölderlin the activity of thinking, in its progress uniting and distinguishing, only prepares the grasp of the infinite life, but this itself is accomplished in the enthusiasm of the artist. According to Schleiermacher the knowledge of the universe is grounded on religion, and that is now Hegel’s doctrine too.

3.

That Hegel at this point of his course passed through the standpoint too which Schleiermacher took up in his Speeches on Religion becomes clear when one puts together his notes of September 1800. Since the subject rises to the fulfillment of the totality of reality only in religion, he had to undertake to determine the essence of religion, and here he arrived, under the influence of Schleiermacher’s Speeches, at a new deepening of his concept of religion.

He is in agreement with Schleiermacher on the following points. A universal natural religion does not exist, according to Hegel either. It could be derived only from a universal and necessary concept of human nature and of its relation to the Godhead. In relation to the universally valid content of natural religion resulting from this concept, the “manifoldness of customs, habits and opinions of the peoples or of individuals would then become accidents, and among them also every single religion.” But it can be strictly proved that human nature never existed in pure form, but lives itself out in infinite modifications. And indeed Hegel derives this proof, in a consistent carrying through of his standpoint of that time, from the fact that the concept never reaches the living nature of man. Precisely that which, from this concept of human nature, would be accident and superfluity proves itself, from the intuition

[p. 165]

of this living nature itself, to be the living, the necessary, indeed perhaps the only natural and beautiful. Religious representations therefore cannot be derived through understanding and reason, they are something transcendent for reason. Religion “makes no claim to be intelligent or rational”: hence it does not belong under the jurisdiction of understanding and reason either. Hegel proves this from the history of religion. Man raises himself above finite life only by freeing himself from entanglement with objects, from the power which their possession exercises over him himself; the sacrifice which expresses this is for every religion of reason something wholly irrational. And even if a religion subjects man to the unknown in trembling, renunciation and silence, human nature may well be wretched which needs such a religion, but precisely such a religion gives this nature what it can bear and what satisfies it. This does not exclude for Hegel positive moments already at the origin of a religion, as he wished to show for Christianity; but every religion is natural insofar as it springs from the living nature of man, and becomes positive only insofar as, as an inheritance of past times, it needs violent institutions in order to produce the religious feelings and actions, and insofar as the religious representations contradict the insights and ideals attained. In the same sense Schleiermacher in his Speeches denies the existence of a natural religion and sees in it only an empty generality, an abstraction, which brings to full expression the religious life of no individual and in which there shows itself only the aversion of the age to the incomprehensible. Schleiermacher and Hegel are further, at that time, closely akin to each other also in the positive determination of the concept of religion. They agree also in this, that the totality, the infinite life, as inapprehensible for the understanding, inaccessible to philosophy, is grasped in religion. For both the process in which this takes place is not resolvable into psychological concepts, but is a life-process of a mystical kind, in which the relation of the finite to the infinite changes. It is for them the entrance into a higher life.

But right here there already shows itself, even then, the difference between the two, to which Hegel then gave public expression for the first time in his essay on Faith and Knowledge. “Divine feeling,” remarks

[p. 166]

Hegel against Schleiermacher, “the infinite felt by the finite, is completed only through this, that reflection is added, dwells upon it.” Religion is for Hegel the living source of the determinations which thinking is able to give concerning the infinite life and its organic connection and which we have run through. The formula in which the notes of 1800 part company with Schleiermacher: “Religion is the elevation of man not from the finite to the infinite, but from finite life to infinite life”—this formula sets the content-fullness in the concept of the infinite life, according to its universally valid philosophical determinateness, over against the subjective and individual manifoldness in the conception of the infinite which Schleiermacher had asserted. And it is characteristic that even the practical consequence from this difference, as it came to such sharp expression from Hegel’s essay on, is already to be pointed out in this epoch. Religion presents itself for Hegel as the inner force uniting the political commonwealth, Schleiermacher demands, beyond the state, its unfolding in individual formations.

Thinking, to be sure, according to Hegel’s notes of 1800, strikes upon a limit; it is never able to bring to unity the opposition between subject and object, finite and infinite, which clings to reflection itself. With this agrees a passage from the presentation of the Son of God and Son of Man: “The opposition of the intuiting and the intuited, that they are subject and object, falls away in the intuition itself; their difference is only a possibility of separation; a man who were wholly sunk in the intuition of the sun would be only a feeling of light, a light-feeling as a being.” Here there is already in sight in Hegel a philosophy which takes its standpoint in the indifference of the subjective and the objective; if Hegel no longer wants to acknowledge the incomprehensibility of the infinite life, he is led to the standpoint to which he went over, with Schelling’s co-operation, soon after these notes.

4.

Schleiermacher had determined the essence of religion in the Speeches on Religion and then given a kind of phenomenology of the religious consciousness: a piece of such a phenomenology is found also

[p. 167]

in the concluding fragment of Hegel’s larger work of 1800. In content, two undated notes can be attached here, which fall within the range of the theological writings and in any case belong among the later theological fragments.

In the adoration of God there is accomplished a projection of the infinite life, which is immanent in the adorer himself, into a beyond. “When man posits the infinite life as spirit of the whole at the same time outside himself, because he himself is a limited thing, posits himself at the same time outside himself, the limited, and raises himself to the living, unites himself most intimately with it, then he adores God.” This objectification of the infinite, as the first of the fragments of 1800 presents it, receives its continuation, according to the concluding fragment, in the cult; in that it transposes the divine into time and space, antinomies arise. That which always takes place is transposed in the cult into a Now, and the being infinite in the infinity of space is at the same time in determinate space. If every part of the cult is an “increased religious union, which completes the previous ones,” then one of the most important forms of it arises from this, that the religious man “keeps back a particular property for himself.” “With the firm having of things man would not have fulfilled the condition of religion, namely to be free from absolute objectivity, to have raised himself above finite life; he would be incapable of union with the infinite life, because he would still be retaining something for himself, still engaged in a ruling over, or ensnared under a dependence; and therefore he gives, from the property whose necessity is his fate, as sacrifice only some, for his fate is necessary and cannot be sublated; he annihilates a part also before the Godhead; from the annihilation of the rest he takes, through communality with friends, the particularity, as far as was possible, and by its being a purposeless superfluity; and through this purposelessness of annihilating alone, through this annihilating for the sake of annihilating, he makes good his otherwise particular relation of purposive annihilating.” When everywhere “the divine service sublates the contemplative or thinking consideration of the objective God, or rather fuses it with subjectivity in living joy,” it has as means thereto song, movement, dance, sounding speech, ordering of the offering.

[p. 168]

The plurality of expressions and of the persons expressing themselves demands a priest as orderer.

The expressions of religiosity represent an increasing completion of the union in religion, and in it the opposition of the objective and the subjective is overcome. Unhappy peoples never wholly overcome this opposition. It “is necessary that, the stronger the separation, the purer the I, and the farther at the same time the object is above and remote from man; that, the greater and more secluded the inner, the greater and more secluded the outer, and, if the latter is posited as the independent, the more subjugated man must appear.” The religion that proceeds from the object leads to the fear of God, who is sought above the heaven of all heavens; the religion that proceeds from the subject “posits itself as a pure I above the ruins of this body and the shining suns, above the thousand times thousand world-bodies and the so many times new solar systems, as many as all of you are, you shining suns.” It is the religion of Fichte of which he here speaks, and at this passage he designates at once its greatness and its limit. “This religion can be sublime and fearfully sublime, but not beautiful, human; and thus the blessedness in which the I has everything, everything opposed, beneath its feet is a phenomenon of the age, at bottom equivalent to depending on an absolutely alien being that cannot become man, or, if it had become so, thus in time, would remain even in this union an absolutely particular, only an absolute One—the worthiest, noblest, if the union with the age were ignoble and base.” Thus he explains the religiosity of Fichte from the ethical greatness of his nature, which makes him unable to unite with his age and its men. If peoples among whom separation prevails, and the suffering from it, must remain behind at these standpoints of religion, the more fortunate nations advance beyond them. Hegel thinks of the Greeks in contrast to Judaism, and then, in contrast to the fragmentation of his own age, of that higher future for which Schleiermacher too hoped: already the coming consciousness of infinite life stands before his eyes as the metaphysical foundation of a higher form of human existence. At this point he approaches the treatise on Faith and Knowledge that appeared some time afterward.

[p. 169]

I now single out, from the other manuscripts that prove, by their problem of positive religion, to belong to this period, and indeed, by the manner of the resolution, to its last phase, that which belongs to this phenomenology of religious consciousness. Here too Hegel proceeds from the philosophy of Fichte, from its opposition of the theoretical and the practical I, and here too he is directed toward the overcoming of an opposition in religious consciousness. “The theoretical syntheses become wholly object, wholly opposed to the subject—practical activity annihilates the object and is wholly subjective—only in love alone is one one with the object, it neither rules nor is ruled—this love, made a being by the imagination, is the Godhead; the separated man then has reverence, respect before it, the love united in itself; to the other his bad conscience gives the consciousness of division, fear before it.” “Where insuperable separations stand in the way of the striving for union, religious representations of a peculiar kind form from here, first of all that of immortality as a supplement to the imperfection of the existing state. Where the separation between the drive and actuality is so great that real pain arises, union is impossible, and if man has strength enough to be able nonetheless to bear the separation, he still sets himself against fate without succumbing to it; if he does not have this strength, he posits this union in a future state, and hopes for it from an alien uniting object, since that one puts nothing into his object that is not in him.” Hegel explains further the representations of hostile divine beings, of punishment and of fate. These too rest on the separation of the drive from actuality and on the suffering arising from it. “Man indeed posits as the ground of this suffering an independent activity and animates it, but since union with pain is impossible, in that it is a suffering, union with that cause of the suffering is also impossible, and he posits it over against himself as a hostile being; had he never enjoyed a favor from it, he would ascribe to it a hostile nature that does not change; had he already had joy from it, had he already loved it, he must think the hostile disposition only as transitory, and if he is conscious of any guilt, he recognizes in his

[p. 170]

pain the punishing hand of the Godhead with which he formerly lived in friendship. But if he is conscious of his purity and has strength enough to be able to bear the complete separation, then he sets himself mightily over against fate, an unknown power in which there is nothing human, without submitting or otherwise concluding with it a union that, with a mightier being, could only be a servitude.” And the assumption of miracles, revelations and apparitions Hegel derives from the fact that the mode of action of the infinite object exceeds the faculty of cognition. In this connection he has now once more attempted to determine the concept of the positivity of religion. Where man unites the incompatible, there is positivity. In the union of subject and predicate an assertion about a being is made, the certainty of which is grounded in the spiritual connection itself; here union, being, faith are in inner necessary connection. “All union is supposed, in positive religion, to be something given; what is given, one does not yet have before one receives it.” Thus in positive religion the subject is determined by a power that calls forth faith, and it restricts activity within more or less narrow limits.

6.

We look back upon the whole connection of Hegel’s works, insofar as the mysterious process of the becoming of his worldview can be read off from it, and seek in it the moments that lead over into Hegel’s further development.

First, the connection of Hegel’s ideas that we have traversed is akin to every mystical pantheism: infinite life as spirit, which has its realization in the totality of the world, inapprehensible to the understanding, attainable only in the religiously contemplative elevation of the soul. If one thinks this worldview as a system, such a system has the structure and construction of that of Plotinus, of medieval mysticism down to Nicolaus Cusanus, of Spinoza, of Schopenhauer; but it is new in this, that the totality of the world is constructed from the connection that the relations of the forms of apprehension of spirit constitute. This connection is necessary and universal, and the nature of spirit itself expresses itself in it. Hegel accepts the negative result of Kant’s theory of knowledge, insofar as it contains the refutation

[p. 171]

of a transcendent world; in this his new ideal of an elevated humanity determines him: freedom and harmony of life demand that the estrangement of spirit from itself in every kind of transcendence be converted into the intramundane relations of kinship, equality, participation of the finite in the infinite, and that the connection of the world be sought in the world itself. This connection immanent in the world is thought by Hegel as a system of relations in which the oppositions of subject and object, whole and part, separation and connection, finite and infinite are connected with one another. And if the all-life in which these relations subsist is spirit, Hegel will have to go on to recognize the character of spirit everywhere where these relations subsist. This system of relations will then form the structure of the world, and it will be the object of our knowledge. In it there is no opposition between subject and object. In several passages of Hegel the thought has already met us that the totality of the world is grasped only from a standpoint beyond the opposition of subject and object. In this the direction toward the philosophy of identity is already contained, which Schelling then took up in 1801, and if Hegel attached himself to him, this advance lay wholly in the line of his own development.

Hegel teaches further, already in this period, that the relations contained in the totality of the world, the same that make up the connection of the transcendental I, must also form the nexus of the phenomena in history. Therein lay the moment that had to lead him to grasp the totality of the world under the aspect of development. I find in the fragments that can be assigned to this time with some certainty no trace that Hegel had at this time already placed a development in nature. All constructions that derive his system from the immanent drift of the thought of development are completely erroneous. Even within the domain of the historical world, in which for him everything in fact fits together into a developmental history of spirit, no systematic use is made of this concept anywhere. Everywhere, however, it is the process that Hegel seeks in history, and so this leads him to the problem of making higher forms of life comprehensible from the lower. To this are added the other problems that Hegel found in the grasping of the totality of the world. Here is tied

[p. 172]

the knot, the resolution of which led to Hegel’s logic. For if the logic of the understanding cannot solve these problems, then from the striving to grasp the world the demand for a higher logic will arise. Hegel’s doctrine of the unknowability of infinite life, as it reached its sharpest development precisely at the close of this period, must lead him toward this higher logic. This logic rests, viewed historically, in the first place on his study of Kant. Its structure is conditioned by Kant’s demarcation of the three domains of intuition of objects, of understanding and of reason according to the form of the relations of apprehending that subsist in them. Its goal is conditioned by the attempt given by Kant in the Critique of Judgment to resolve, by means of the idea of immanent end, the task posed at the close of the Critique of Pure Reason, of making the totality of the world thinkable. The use of the materials that were contained in Kant’s system is then determined by the opposition of this higher kind of knowing to the view of the understanding as it was formed in our epoch. Hegel at first separated the basic science of philosophy into logic and metaphysics. In the former he proceeded from finite knowing or reflection, showed how finite knowing is sublated by reason, and so found the entrance into metaphysics. For a long time this separation of logic from metaphysics was maintained in his system; logic remained for him the exposition of the forms and categories of knowing by the understanding, while in metaphysics the idea of the infinity returning into itself stood at the center. And even the Nuremberg dictations show what significance the constant struggle against thinking by the understanding and its fixed demarcations has had in the history of this philosophy. Thus the fundamental significance appears that the ideas of our period had for Hegel’s system, however meager the indications of the theological fragments about his mystical pantheism of that time may be.

If one gathers together, under the most comprehensive historical point of view, that which connects the systematic ideas of this epoch with the future system, it is Hegel’s struggle against the dominion of the Enlightenment, his consciousness of the right of the higher content of life, of the mightier historical view, of the stronger ideals, as the new spiritual forces that have now developed and that cannot be subjected to this view of the understanding; it is

[p. 173]

mysticism—a form ever recurring of warding off the understanding, but one which in him is joined with the development of a system of concepts through which the sovereign spirit had in the end to undertake the resolution of the task of knowledge of the world.

So Hegel had become a metaphysician under the laborious work of this epoch. He had given up the genuine method of philosophy as it had been founded in Kant’s analysis of science. At the stage of metaphysics which he had reached from critical idealism he had to perceive that the center of philosophy lies in a higher logic; yet with the genuine analytic method he had at the same time lost the means of thought for the resolution of this problem. But as this metaphysics rested on the lived connection of spirit, new views into historical life opened to him from there. The process in its lawful changeability opened itself up in this metaphysics. And here too the inner unity of historical situation, lived experience and metaphysics shows itself in Hegel’s development. In the historical world around him there offered itself a spectacle which he followed with ever equal tension, the restless transformations of the French state, and under the influence of this the changes in the other states, among which he investigated penetratingly those taking place in Germany—the greatest example of the historical process in its restless agitation that any historical thinker has lived through. He burrowed from his metaphysics into the ultimate depths of history. Historical categories of a peculiar kind began to dawn on him. He experimented with resolving his task by a real dialectical method. The most important and surest gain that he worked out in this period lay in a deepening into the inwardness of the historical world, which went far beyond all earlier historiography. That Hegel penetrated into it precisely from religiosity was decisive for the greatest thing he has achieved for European scholarship. He stands beside Niebuhr, whose political genius and historical criticism created the first history of a political body, as the founder of the history of the inwardness of the human spirit. To this he brought qualities of the rarest kind, above all that holding together of what is lived in the heart, in which he was so similar to his friend Hölderlin; from this arose in him the consciousness

[p. 174]

of how not only this or that acquisition of an age is inherited into the one that follows, but the whole spiritual constitution, as lived, preserved, sublated, constitutes the condition of the next state. And from the withheld depth of a spirit that never scattered itself upon the world there came to him an energy of experiencing the spiritual movements around him, which made it possible for him to make the past live again down to its ultimate inwardness—with everything, with separations, with suffering, with longing, with blessedness. What was thus acquired by him at that time in concrete understanding of historical reality forms the foundation of his Phenomenology of Spirit: often down to the very words this mightiest writing of Hegel has been determined by it.

#### II. The History of Religion according to Its Place in the System, Its Coherence and Its Method.

The position which the history of religion occupies in this period of Hegel’s labor of thought now emerges. If one looks to the connection of the system of that time, then the doctrine of the consciousness that in religion rises to the idea of the unity of the world and to its realization in the sphere of the human world forms the second part of this mystical pantheism. If one looks at the work accomplished in this epoch, its center is formed by the problem of Christianity, grasped in its universal-historical connection. And if one then tries to grasp the significance of the thought-work of that time for Hegel’s definitive philosophical system, the history of religion shows itself as the domain in which the thought of development first formed itself in Hegel at that time.

From mystical pantheism one proceeds, in this second part of the system, to the manifestation of the divine in consciousness.
