For the divine life that holds sway in the whole of the world comes in man to consciousness of itself: a proposition that follows necessarily from the fact that the connection of spirit realizes itself in that of the world. If infinite life is present in finite life, if every part of it is at the same time itself a whole, life, then the part that constitutes man is reflected life, in that in him life is apprehended; he is the truth, and since for this mystical pantheism the divine life attains consciousness in its totality only

[p. 175]

in religion, truth appears in religious consciousness. Thus for him the process in which religion generates the consciousness of the divine life becomes the center of history, the continuation of the world-process itself, and its exposition, insofar as he regards it from the standpoint of his system, must take in it the place of the second part. This position of religiosity is given by the fact that the unity of the world is lived in it and raised to knowledge. Hence the Phenomenology of Spirit still regards absolute knowing as separated from the highest stage of religion only in form. “The content of representing is absolute spirit, and what is still at issue is only the sublation of this mere form;” for religion remains always bound to the form of representing in objectivity.

What, then, is the closer connection of the history of religiosity with universal history, and of both, backward, with the philosophy of nature in his system of that time? No manuscript on the philosophy of nature can be placed in this period with any credibility; indeed the transition from the intuition of infinite life through the philosophy of nature to history cannot be documented by any passage known to me, and the historical studies extend indeed over the whole domain of history, but of a connection that embraced all sides of the historical interest nothing has been transmitted. Yet one may draw from the absence of such manuscripts no conclusion as to what Hegel put on paper at that time, and still less as to what occupied his thoughts.

1.

Hegel has now undertaken to show an inner connection in the history of religion.

Consciousness finds itself at first in naive unity with nature. In this doctrine Hegel is at one with Rousseau and Schiller, and with these as with Hölderlin’s fragment of 1794 he then agrees in the striving to overcome Rousseau’s cultural pessimism by the assumption of the progress from the disintegration of unity with nature to its restoration. But he goes beyond these predecessors in that he emphasizes how only through the oppositions

[p. 176]

in consciousness could history attain a higher stage of the unity of spirit with itself. This was the beginning of his theory of historical development. Among the various utterances of Hegel on this, one is especially clear, which attaches to the presentation of the unity of infinite and finite life accomplished in Christian consciousness. “This unity is perfected life for the reason that in it reflection too has been given its due; over against undeveloped unity stood the possibility of reflection, of separation; in this [perfected unity] unity and separation are united, a living being that had been opposed to itself, but did not make this opposition absolute. The living now feels in love the living. In love, then, all tasks are resolved: the self-destroying one-sidedness of reflection and the infinite opposition of the unconscious, undeveloped one in unity.” “The child”—he says at another passage—“begins with the belief in gods out of itself, with fear, until it has itself acted more and more, has separated, but in the unions returns to the original, but now developed, self-produced, felt unity.” And indeed the pain of opposition always and at once calls forth the striving for unity. Thus in Jewish development the unions in the idea of God that spring from this striving have constantly accompanied the increasing oppositions in actuality. For the unions themselves have a double form. As Schleiermacher distinguishes the unions of reason and nature into organizing and symbolizing ones, so according to Hegel they are accomplished either in actuality or in the idea; spirit, where the accomplishment in actuality does not suffice for it, seeks a supplement in religious or metaphysical consciousness.

Into this schema of religio-historical development there must now be fitted its connection, as the Basic Fragment contains it and the other pieces of the theological work carry it through. Three members join together in the fragment; each of these members consists in a connection of concepts in which Hegel presents a part of the history of religion, and again and again there lies at the basis of the presentation the progress from unity to separation and opposition, and from it to the restoration of unity through the union of the opposed. The presupposition of this conceptual

[p. 177]

construction of the history of religion is here too the naive unity of original consciousness with itself as a state of innocence; Hegel has already at the beginning of his presentation expressly pointed back to this stage. Jewish, Greek, Roman religiosity form on this basis: separation and opposition have reached the highest tension in the historical moment in which the religion of love emerges from Judaism and is taken up by Greeks and Romans; but as this appears in the midst of the world of oppositions, a barrier arises from this in the consciousness of Jesus himself, and as the consequences of this assert themselves first in the religion of Jesus himself, then more strongly in the community, history points to the necessity of a higher union of what is separated in modern consciousness. This is the connection of the history of religion that Hegel sets forth in the fragment.

2.

The first member in this connection is formed by the development of the Jewish, Greek and Roman religion. Hegel’s presentation restricts itself to the Jewish and the Greek history of religion, but gives it the broadest basis in the culture of these nations. The Greeks, Polybius, Machiavelli and Vico had grasped the cycle of national developments; Herder had presented the total culture of the individual nation in its growth, its flowering and its decline; Hegel stands strongly under his influence, but is already in the older sketch of Greek development superior to him in genetic power. In his work itself he now seeks, from his general ideas, to penetrate still deeper into the development of national cultures.

He thinks on here from a proposition of the Aristotelian Politics which must be counted among the most beautiful discoveries of Aristotle. He does not mention Aristotle in this connection, yet it cannot be doubted that he is dependent on him, mediately or immediately: thus there attach themselves to the Greek comparative study of state and history the beginnings of the modern philosophy of history, in Hegel as in Vico. The passage is at the beginning of the Basic Fragment. “At the time when Jesus appeared among the Jewish nation, it found itself in the state

[p. 178]

that is the condition of a revolution occurring sooner or later and always bears the same general characters. When the spirit has departed from a constitution, from the laws, and the former through its alteration no longer agrees with these, there arises a seeking, a striving for something else, which is soon found by each in something different, whereby then a manifoldness of formations, of ways of life, of claims, of needs comes forth, which, if they gradually diverge so far that they can no longer exist side by side, finally effect an outbreak and give existence to a new general form, a new bond of men.” The starting point of this method of presenting relations of the changes in a historical whole lies in the correlation of the spirit of a community to its constitution and its laws. Hegel applies this method here to make the emergence of Christian religiosity from Judaism comprehensible; he then made use of it, as we have seen, likewise in the essay on the German constitution for the conception of our political history, and also where, within the Greek and Roman world, the moment is to be pointed out that brought about the acceptance of Christianity, he moves in relations of concepts that go back to this foundation.

A historical record that at least may not be assigned to a later epoch deals with the oriental spirit in general. Here he proceeds from the rigid unchangeability of the oriental characters, from their incapacity to take into themselves what is opposed to them and to reconcile it with themselves. What disturbs them on their way is hostile to them. Ruling and serving, law and necessity are thus the basic relations of their social existence; for what cannot be united from within in life and love must be bound from without by law. The soullessness in the apprehension of the actual results in flight into imagination and the adornment of naked factuality by alien images. From this incapacity to grasp, in actuality itself, what dwells in it, unites and transfigures, it arises that precisely nature is driven out of the natural, and it appears only as something common, subjugated.

Thus certain basic traits of the Jewish essence are already laid down in the spirit of the Orient, above all the diremption from nature and the service

[p. 179]

under the law. From the total life of the Israelites, from its first accessible beginnings on, Hegel then derives the peculiar shaping of these traits in their religiosity. His Jewish history founded on this procedure has been presented in detail; it need only be recalled once more how the decisive point of view of his history of religion, grounded in the history of culture, was here first put to use. The consistent energy and the abstract character of the religiosity of the Jews spring from the poverty of their life. Nature is unholy to them, bereft of gods, and so the relation to it is conceived under the representation of an external dominion of God; from friendly community with other nations the people has withdrawn, and so it needs an almighty God of its own; it has no independent will to free and beautiful orders; it has maltreated life in itself; nothing holy remained to it, and so it submits itself wholly to the power of the ruling God, alien to life and nature. Its independence was not a state of happiness and humanity but of total passivity and ugliness, and after its destruction nothing remained to it but service under its God and dependence on the law.

Under the same point of view of his history of religion Hegel comprehends the character of the religiosity of the Greeks from their strong and beautiful total life, as has likewise been set forth. So he then understands further the dissolution of Greek religiosity from the political changes, and he finds in this dissolution the ground of explanation for the fact that the nation had to seek a restoration, a new union, in Christian consciousness. Greek religiosity did not make room for the Christian as error yields to truth; it had to vanish with the conditions under which it was effective. Devotion to the state gave way to the individual’s care for his own life; when the freedom of the classical peoples perished, their gods could afford them no replacement or consolation for such a loss: so the Greeks and Romans had to carry out the realization of their need, which was directed toward an Absolute, in Christianity. “The wretched, unhappy condition of the oppressed Romans made them receptive to the expectation of an early destruction of the world and the appearance of the Messiah.” And as the religiosity of the Jews, after the inner dissolution of their state and their culture into parties and

[p. 180]

sects had to fall apart, so too after the downfall of Greek political life and of the Roman republican orders the spiritual life contained in them disintegrated. “When men had been deprived of the dominion of their ideas over the objects, the genius of mankind divided.” A historical record, which admittedly may also fall into the Jena period, develops this in a manner that recalls the Phenomenology and its three forms of self-consciousness, Stoicism, Skepticism and unhappy consciousness. Some maintain themselves independently over against the objects as over against something alien, indifferent, which is too contemptible to lay a hand on; the others go under in the change of the objects; and still others set over against their powerless, dispirited self an invisible world.

If we now compare the two series of development, what is common to their course lies in an inner dialectic, which is the same in them; they advance from a system of national life, in which constitution, culture and religion form a connection, through the dissolution of this system to a higher unity.

The new in this conception of history attached itself first of all to what the great historians of the Enlightenment, Voltaire, Hume and Gibbon, had already achieved. They had given cross-sections through the history of the culture of a nation. They had recognized the correlation by which the parts of such a cultural whole correspond to one another like the members of a living creature. Voltaire had thus sketched the connection of French culture in the age of Louis the Fourteenth; Hume had then woven into his political history of England such cross-sections, in which he presents the total constitution of the English spirit at a given time; in the greatest historical work of art of the eighteenth century, Gibbon’s history of the Roman Empire, the presentation of the culture in the Roman Empire under the Antonines has been one of the most brilliant parts. Hegel, however, possessed, in the connection of the general and necessary achievements of spirit as transcendental philosophy had set it up, the basis for the representation of the manifold facts of culture according to their relations of kinship in a connection of concepts. The kinships that subsist between the individual sides of culture, and on which the consciousness of their inner belonging together rests, he sought

[p. 181]

thus to raise to a connection presentable in concepts. This connection was for him the unity in which the manifold of the historical facts of culture articulates itself into the whole, and in which he strove to express the essence of the culture of an age. So there arose for him also a new procedure for making the progress from such a total constitution to a following one really intelligible.

How can the alteration of such a whole, which, viewed empirically, springs from the cooperation of a manifoldness of factors, forces, relations, be raised to knowledge? I mentioned how Gibbon had already treated the question “by what means the Christian faith attained so remarkable a victory over the other ruling nations of the earth.” His method is characteristic of the way in which pragmatic historiography resolved the problem of such an alteration in the total spiritual state. He gives five causes for the growth of Christianity. Without doubt each of these causes must be regarded as a moment that has worked on the spread of Christianity. Taken together, they represent, in the ethical-religious total constitution of the Roman Empire, a sum of energy that has been active in the direction of the effect of Christianity, hence toward its expansion. But the pragmatic historian has no means of convincing us that in the struggle with the forces that still lay in Roman religiosity, bound up with all the institutions of the Roman Empire, and in Greek religiosity, connected with all the knowledge and all the beauty of Greek existence, Christianity had to win so rapid and complete a victory. We recall how Hegel had early, in his reading of the historians, mastered the concept of pragmatic history; he grasped it in its whole depth as the method that has for its object the connection of the phenomena in a given state and the grounds of the alterations of it: here lay the starting point for the tasks which he set himself. Not from without only, as a philosopher, but in the inner further labor out of pragmatic history he arrived, after long wrestling with his subject, at the method peculiar to him. The relation of concepts in which he presents a total state discloses to him the necessity of the alteration that leads from this total state to a following one. He shares with pragmatic historiography

[p. 182]

the intention of deriving, by means of a deepening into history itself, at every single point the necessity of progress. Together with it he stands in the sharpest opposition to that shallow philosophy of history as it had made itself broad in Germany, which wished to set up a general principle for the explanation of history, such as, say, the predisposition of man, the drive toward perfection. And if Herder rose beyond this pragmatic history to a full understanding of the intrinsic worth of the individual nations and penetrated into their feeling of life, Hegel is in this everywhere conditioned by him. But he leaves behind the quality of the understanding in pragmatic history, as well as the blurred and enthusiastic quality in Herder, in that he raises the intuition of the total spirit of a nation, of the inner unity in the expressions of life of an age, to conceptual determinacy by the return to the inner connection of spirit in general. Thus arose his historical understanding of Jewish and Greek culture and religiosity, as it has been set forth.

4.

The inner growth, the flowering and the decline of nations are intelligible to us from our own life. The difficulties of the universal-historical conception of history lie first in the thought of a general development that links the achievements of the nations, sets the intrinsic values of their existence in relation to one another and so advances through the peoples as its stages. Whenever the historiography of the eighteenth century posed itself this problem, it made an idea of its enlightened reason the standard by which it determined the values of development. Herder first sought to grasp the immanent values that realize themselves in the life of nations in their free manifoldness. But he failed where, with the interlocking of the nations under the Roman Empire and the appearance of Christianity, this problem of universal development comes forth. He saw the task clearly indeed, and he brought out the increase of inwardness, of fullness, of the freedom arising in historical consciousness, in the course of history. But this intuitive spirit lacked the energy of thought to gather and carry through such points of view. Christianity is for him the realization of his ideal of humanity, lifted out of every

[p. 183]

historical determinacy—without contour. His alleged natural laws of history, which are supposed to bring about the progress of the human race, are trivial generalities. He takes flight into the external teleology of belief in providence. If one wished to extract from the historical course itself more securely recognizable development than the progress of the sciences that proves itself in it and, brought about by it, growing dominion over nature, increasing solidarity of the nations, then it was necessary to penetrate more deeply into the inwardness of the historical events: the fixed values of religiosity and ethical life had to be dissolved into the relativity of history; the relations of these values, according to which they form a progressing series, had to be sought out; in the limits of human states the ground had to be discovered—as it were the power of negativity which forces forward to a higher state. Therein consisted Hegel’s great achievement, and so one now grasps of what decisive significance it was for him that he approached the problem of universal history from the standpoint of the historian of religion. For the advance of spirit in its ultimate depth could best be studied in the course of religiosity; the infinity of historical values and at the same time their relativity had to be shown in Jesus, or there was no developmental history of mankind; the enigmatic relation in which states of the heart and concepts stand to one another could be recognized here more easily than anywhere else.

The concepts that Hegel had formed were well suited to the resolution of this task. The highest all-embracing category of his system was the relation of a whole to its parts, of a connection to its members, and precisely as such a whole history presents itself, as such a connection it wants to be comprehended. The connection of spirit, as it runs its course not only in the single individual but is common to all, was for him identical with that of the world and consequently also with that of history. And however doubtful this identity might be in regard to the relation between the transcendental subject and nature: certainly in universal history the connection of the pure I was present. This led further to a system of those actions in history in which spirit realizes its essence. This system seemed to contain the unity

[p. 184]

and the development that history sought. Thus equipped, Hegel in this epoch approached the question what necessities led from the religions of the ancient world, in particular from Judaism, to Jesus, and then from him to the history of our European religiosity. The problem of alteration, in which from one total state another is brought about, now determined itself for him more closely thus: how does the advance of the total spirit from a given stage to a higher one take place?

The Basic Fragment sets in with the progress from Judaism to Jesus. It leaves behind all the historical grounds of explanation that had lain in the conditions of the society of that time and of which Hegel himself had made use in various places; it has as its presupposition only the result that had emerged from the older history of religion for the age of Jesus—the oppositions, separations, contradictions that tore the society of that time asunder, and only the conceptual relations thus arising, which lead from here to the union of everything separated, form the connection through which the religious consciousness of Jesus is comprehended.

We have here the same procedure that will meet us later in the Phenomenology, mature and in universal application. Hegel grasped conceptually the progress that leads from a total constitution of the soul, such as that of Judaism, through various moments to another, such as lies in the religion of Christ. The application of such a procedure to this object had prepared itself when, under the influence of Kant, he comprehended this progress as that from an external ecclesiastical faith to a religion of reason. This conception lay behind him. He polemicized here against it. As he had formerly transferred the lived experience of his struggle of liberation from theology through Kant’s science of reason to the great historical process that occupied him, so the same now happened to him; the new lived experience that was accomplished in him lay in the sublation of the abstract separation of the universal rule of morals from the impulses of life, of a transcendent Godhead from the human soul, of the great domains of actuality from one another, as it was accomplished in the new pantheism, and through this experience he now made the origin and essence of Christianity intelligible to himself. Both modes of presenting the origin of Christianity have with one another

[p. 185]

in common that the progress from Judaism to Christianity is represented by an inner relation of concepts.

Concepts of human states of the heart, such as those just indicated, are accordingly so set in relation to one another that, as the moments of a historical process, they make this process intelligible as a necessary one, and with that the explanation of the process falls from the individual subjects into a total consciousness, which advances from a given stage to a higher one. This advance of the total consciousness is Hegel’s theme, the possibility that it raises itself from a lower stage to a higher one, in which there is more than in the lower, becomes his problem, and the search for moments presentable in concepts, of which one demands the other—that is his method. The task of history, to grasp inwardness, receives through this a new deepening: the immense concentration holding together what happened in historical consciousness, the condensation of the factual into concepts, the mastery over the circle of these historical categories—this was the singular gift which Hegel contributed when, beside Niebuhr and Schleiermacher, he took part in raising the human sciences and history to a higher stage of knowledge. How this gift developed, how his individual conceptions arose, how his method formed itself—this the developmental history of Hegel must set forth.

The relation of the concepts of states of the heart through which Hegel in our fragment seeks to show the transition from Judaism to the religion of Christ in its necessity is accomplished by means of the categories of separation and connection. It is expressed in the proposition: “Disposition cancels the positivity, objectivity of the commandments; love the limits of disposition, religion the limits of love.” The sphere in which these moments are linked with one another in the historical process is that of religiosity. It is distinguished from that of theoretical conduct; in this too the relations of connection and separation subsist, and already here there appears in Hegel the proposition, important for his conception of the standpoint of reflection: “Through the unity of the understanding the separated are left as separated, the substances remain separated; the union is objective.” And the moving force of the progress that leads from the separations realized in the Jewish religion of law

[p. 186]

to ever more complete unity is not expressly designated, but everywhere it is presupposed that in the separations there lies something painful, and in the bringing about of unions a satisfaction.

The moments of the progress will accordingly be determined by the separations that are contained in Jewish religiosity. The first of these arises from the relation of man’s bondage to the law; the law is the objective power which stands over against man, to whom its commandments are directed, as something alien: thus the power of the commanding will is wholly transferred into this law, and man himself is condemned to will-lessness. Further, this otherworldly law leaves human beings themselves in their separation from one another; accordingly there results under the law insensibility, a lack of beautiful relations in the intercourse of men with one another. Finally, man’s religious belonging to God is canceled by the estrangement of the lawgiver from him, and hence godlessness arises under the law. Thus will-lessness, insensibility, godlessness, which develop under the bondage of the law, are grounded in separations, and they must be overcome by union between will and law, between man and men, between men and God. Jesus accomplishes this union.

Hegel sets over against the objective commandment disposition, in which the unity of the ethical personality with itself is restored. His method is now to show the limit of this union, which is contained in disposition; when it comes to consciousness, the dissatisfaction thus made manifest demands a higher union: this then forms the next moment, which is contained in Jesus’ constitution of life. The union that is accomplished in disposition and moral action remains always limited and relative, for every action is determined by the kind of separation that it cancels; the cancellation of this limit of disposition is the striving to spread out the acts as a manifold, and this is now for Hegel a first moment in love. Disposition is, further, only an external union, insofar as the persons made unitary by it still stand side by side as “a multitude of absolutely separated and unconnected ones,” and so the inner union of these with one another is the second and decisive moment in love. Thus disposition becomes love in Jesus.

[p. 187]

Immediately, however, when one raises the psychic constitution of love to consciousness, the limit inherent in it appears again with regard to the union here accomplished. In the psychic constitution of love independent beings, substances as it were, each for itself, strive toward union. The consciousness of the limit of this striving as dissatisfaction must lead to the sublation of this psychic constitution in the consciousness of the living unity of God and men and of all men with one another, and only in this has the striving for union reached its goal. This unity Hegel grasps as the process in the divine through which the real finitization of the infinite in Christ and in his disciples completes itself.

Thus in this process of union every limit of it is sublated, every pain thereby annihilated, the dominion of love realized. But Jesus, in whom this psychic constitution has been accomplished according to its three moments, appears in a world that has been characterized as full of opposition, enmity, disintegration of all beautiful connections. This is the foundation for the third part of the fragment, from which we here single out only the relation of concepts in which the character, life and fate of Jesus are more closely determined. Jesus is in conflict with the given world, he injures its life for the sake of the higher that is in him. Morality has nothing to do with these injuries; it remains behind when we enter the sublime precinct of historical life, where the higher value attacks what is opposed to it, and yet this injury of life calls fate up against the guiltlessly guilty. At this point there now confronts us a still more comprehensive combination of concepts—as methodological means for penetrating into the ultimate depths of the heart and of the course of life of Jesus. While union through love is accomplished from within, there arises from without, through the conflict of a decomposed world, torn by oppositions, with the pure heart, the necessity that it come to terms with such a world. From this springs the tragedy of the greatest historical personality. His fate enters into contradiction with the harmony of his soul. If Jesus takes up the struggle with the world, then life enters thereby into conflict with life: life, which for the religion of love is one, then contradicts itself. But if Jesus withdraws from the relations of life, renouncing the attempt

[p. 188]

to overcome the debased world in struggle, then with this elevation above the historical fate in which he was placed every possibility is canceled of shaping life and of uniting in love the surroundings about him. So in this case too there arises in Jesus a limit for the disposition of heart directed toward the union of everything separated. Unable to shape the disorganized world by action, he must succumb to this world. That is the historical necessity in the life of Jesus, as it presents itself to Hegel.

One last trait in the religiosity of Jesus, since our fragment ends here, must be supplemented from others; this trait too proves necessary in the connection of Jesus’ consciousness. From the pain that arises for Jesus out of all the oppositions in which he lives, there arises for him at the same time a deeper feeling of life. In the separations life has become conscious of itself. Therein lies the advance that Jesus signifies even in relation to Greekdom. In his Sermon on the Mount he proclaims to the assembled multitude a higher, other region of life, whose relation to the world could only be to be hated and persecuted by it. So there arises a new valuation of pain, of suffering, over against the Greek doctrine of eudaemonia. Into love itself this trait enters; it is “the holy considered after separation and union.” The result of the presentation of the religious consciousness of Jesus is a two-sided one, as the dialectic of Hegel that is preparing itself demands. This consciousness marks a higher stage in relation to every earlier one of the religious spirit; but in it too we encounter a limit: “such enmities,” so Hegel designates this limit, “as Jesus sought to sublate, can only be overcome by bravery, not reconciled by love.”

So in this presentation of the fate of Jesus the teaching of Jesus, his disposition of soul, his life and his fate are raised to clarity through relations of concepts that express states of the heart. Hegel has here solved most completely the problem of a methodical procedure for presenting the historical conceptually, as it hovered before him at this stage.

5.

In Jesus an advance of the spirit of humanity took place, and in him Hegel first showed how an individual becomes an organ of the development of spirit and how spirit represents itself in him. As

[p. 189]

we now advance to the consciousness of the Christian community, a new decisive concept of this conception of history is prepared: that of objective spirit.

The consciousness of Jesus conditions the development of religious life in the community founded by him, and indeed in the limit of that consciousness lies the chief moment that brings about the development of the community’s consciousness and determines the direction of its development. Hegel’s procedure in this part of the history of religion brings out more strongly than had happened in the preceding one two essential foundations of his historical conception. In the oldest Christian community he can show, still more clearly than in the Greek polities, a unitary consciousness that is the bearer of a total activity. And in that he thus regards this community as the subject that produces the whole Christian suprasensible order of intuitions and concepts, analogously as Fichte’s I produces the world, he finds here in history itself the confirmation of the doctrine of Kant and Fichte on the creative nature of the subject. So there arises here the first significant attempt to make history understandable from this basic concept of transcendental idealism. Hegel proceeds in this from the moment in the consciousness of Jesus that leads over to the life and dogma of the community. It was grounded in the relation of Jesus to the world set out above that his community “abstained from all forms of life in relation to one another, or determined them only through the universal spirit of love, i.e., did not live in these forms.” So the satisfaction of the community could lie only in its relation to the divine spirit, and it is only this relation, moreover, that is religion. A consciousness of God in the community had to arise; and since the God of the community had to bring its members, who are bound together through love, to a closer union, Jesus was raised to be this God of the community.

Immediately Hegel now derives a new moment in the connection of the religious consciousness of the community—the function that the divinity of Jesus has in relation to the Christian community. Since “love did not itself found the thoroughgoing union,” since the community accordingly required another power of union, the equality of faith in the deified teacher Jesus received this function. And with tenacious dialectic it is now further constructed from the already known properties of the community’s consciousness that the deified Jesus confronts the community as something alien,

[p. 190]

positive, objective: the community thus enters into the relation of dependence upon him.—To the external dependence of a religious community upon a transcendent founder Hegel here opposes a truer relation. “Nothing is given to life, to spirit; what it has received, that it has itself become, that has so passed over into it that it is now a modification of it, that it is its life.” In place of the category of external dependence there thus enters, as a means of understanding religious influences, that of equality and of connection—concepts that are grounded in the doctrine of the transcendental I and lead toward the doctrine of identity. So there grows the new, important historical insight of how, on the basis of this, receiving becomes a becoming in the self, a modification of it.—Finally our record brings out as a further moment, to which the logical consistency of thought leads the community, that such a union of divine power with a human being requires for its attestation the extension of belief in miracles.

How instructive is the comparison of this piece of Hegelian history of dogma and of the church with what was thought before, alongside and after him on the same subject. Hegel takes as his basis the concept of the Christian community of love spreading in a world of separations, and by means of it he carries the consciousness of Christ over into that of the third or fourth century after him; he draws in no influence of Greek or Roman notions; we have before us the first example of the immanent developmental history of dogma, such as it won so powerful an influence on historical thinking through the later Hegel, Baur and Strauss. One-sided as this procedure is, there nevertheless lies an enduring truth in such inner dialectic of history, if one grasps it in its historical relation to the play of forces within which it runs its course and by which it is constantly influenced.

From the conceptual connection of Hegel’s history of religion much that is new and surprising light falls on the phenomena of the Christian consciousness; but in this whole presentation Hegel’s greatest and most consequential conception is nevertheless the demonstration of how the Christian community produces the Christ myth, in which its higher consciousness attains intuition of itself. The pragmatism of

[p. 191]

individual psychology had possessed no means of thought for approaching the understanding of the Christian myth. Precisely in that Hegel and Schleiermacher grasped the community as the myth-forming subject, the great historical problem of the origin of the Christian world of faith became soluble.

6.

With the connection of this history of religion there is joined the process in which the faith of the Christian community becomes positive—the time is then ripe for the overcoming of this Christian faith of the community. We saw how Hegel at the close of our epoch reworked the first sheets of the old draft on the positive in Christianity; even if at first only the positive moments were to be spoken of that were already given with the origin of Christianity, he nevertheless at the same time, from his new metaphysical-historical standpoint, allowed a second possibility of positivity to be seen, one that affects every religious phenomenon, because it necessarily arises from the process of life itself. This concept of the positive shows us the highest stage of insight into the history of religion to which he raised himself in this period.

Only a course of culture lasting centuries explains to him how a procedure could arise that comprehends the essence of man in one concept and sets over against this the historical phenomena as a manifold not conceptually grasped; from this concept springs the false opposition between the One natural religion and the multiplicity of its positive forms. But there is not one universal human nature that could be presented in its necessary traits; “living nature is eternally something other than the concept of it, and thereby that which for the concept was mere modification, pure contingency, a superfluity, becomes the necessary, the living, perhaps the only natural and beautiful.” How, then, does a religion become positive? The consciousness of a higher power, transcendent for understanding and reason, must occur in every form of human culture; peculiar religious actions and feelings are in such a form of religion bound up with the other sides of an individually constituted life: this individual and determinate character of religion does not make it a positive one, for particularity, determinateness is a property of every life.

[p. 192]

And if reason declares this determinateness contingent, in contrast to eternal, unchangeable, necessary properties of human nature, the contingent does not thereby receive the character of the positive; “for man can attach holiness to the contingent, and mostly to something contingent.” “Even to tremble before something unknown, to renounce one’s will in one’s mode of action and to subject oneself wholly to given rules like a machine; without any understanding, through doing and renouncing, speaking and keeping silent, to lull oneself into a shorter or lifelong dullness of feeling—all this can be natural, and a religion that breathed that spirit would on that account still not be positive, precisely because it would be appropriate to the nature of its age. A nature that required such a religion would indeed be a wretched nature, but the religion would fulfill its final end. It would give this nature something higher, such as it alone can bear and in which it finds satisfaction. Only when another courage awakens, when it acquires a self-feeling and with it demands freedom for itself, and does not merely place it in its overpowering being, can the hitherto existing religion seem to it a positive one.” When the progress of thought has destroyed the religion at hand, when it persists only as an heirloom of past times, then, to be sure, the content of the religious teachings and commandments does not change, but the form under which religion attests the truth of its teachings and demands the carrying out of its commandments has become another. Religion now enforces its recognition as a power alien to spirit, as an external objectivity and positivity. Hegel knows—this introduction shows it—that such a conflict has arisen between Christian religiosity and the aspiring culture of the time. Here too the kinship of his ideas with Schleiermacher’s Speeches on Religion shows itself.

What now is the standard by which what has thus become positive in the Christian religiosity existing today can be measured? Understanding and reason cannot be judges of religion: “what makes no claim to be intelligible or rational does not belong at all to their jurisdiction.” At this point the turn becomes evident that has now been accomplished in Hegel. From his absorption in the historical becoming of human conditions it had dawned on him that the standard of judgment upon them lay in need and the historical

[p. 193]

situation, not however in their relation to abstract propositions of reason. “Understanding and reason can summon everything before their tribunal, and the presumption easily arises that everything should be intelligible, everything rational, and thus they do, to be sure, discover enough of the positive, and the outcry over slavery of the spirit, pressure on conscience, superstition has no end at all. The most unconstrained actions, the most innocent feelings, the most beautiful presentations of imagination experience this rough treatment. But the effect too is in keeping with this unsuitable doing. Intelligent people believe they speak truth when they speak intelligently to feeling, to the power of imagination, to religious needs, and cannot grasp how their truth is resisted, why they preach to deaf ears. The fault is that they offer stones to the child that asks for bread. When a house is to be built, then their ware has usefulness. But likewise, if bread laid claim to fitness for building houses, they would rightly object.” The standard for the judge of religion is an ideal of human nature. By this every single need and every satisfaction of such a need, as they appear in time, must in the end be measured. “An ideal of human nature is something quite other than general concepts about human destiny and about the relation of man to God. The ideal very well admits particularity, determinateness and even requires peculiar religious actions, feelings, customs, an excess, a multitude of superfluities, which before the lantern light of general concepts appears only as ice and stone.” This ideal, which contains the broad fullness of human liveliness, determines for all convictions of religion their truth-value, for all commandments their validity, for all creations of imagination their reality, and they all become dead forms if they do not change with the ideal, which in every century is a different one.

7.

Having arrived at the conclusion of Hegel’s presentation of the history of religion, we now attempt to clarify its method, in order to determine the significance of his work achieved in these theological fragments for the genesis of his new procedure, which was directed at the understanding of the historical world.

[p. 194]

It has been shown how Hegel proceeded from the achievements of pragmatic history and how there then resulted for him, from the doctrine of transcendental philosophy on the connection of spirit, aids for overcoming the limits of pragmatic historiography. If the historian’s task is to make a following state comprehensible out of an aggregate of states, forces, processes, as well as of the relations of these to one another, and if this is possible only in that constant relations are found in this process between ever-recurring partial contents of the concrete states, to which the changes are bound, then it is clear at the outset that the historian, if he wishes to advance beyond the re-experiencing of what is historically given to the comprehension of what has happened, can solve his task only through a relation of concepts in which a rule of change is contained. Hegel is the first who presented this problem quite generally. He first sought principles that make possible the establishment of universally valid, necessary relations of concepts in which the historical changes can be expressed and comprehended.

We have now seen him at work; his attempt to demonstrate, through relations of concepts, as necessary the great process in which the religion of Jesus formed itself and the community brought forth the dogma, has passed before us. And now we can determine more closely the procedure by which he solved his problem in this period. Both the individual concepts of which he made use and their connection are familiar to the connoisseur of the history of philosophy from transcendental philosophy. The reader will recall what has been said on the origin of the concepts of connection, development, dialectic of the spiritual processes in the age of Kant and Fichte. The problem of history is related to that of natural science, and the two circles of knowing resemble each other also in this, that for the explanation of changes, for the connection of the given facts, they must seek a foundation, the latter in mathematics, the former in the connection of spirit. Mechanics constructs the process of motion from a relation of concepts that have been won through the combination of experiment with mathematical presuppositions. Historical science, which possesses in change a problem analogous to motion in the spiritual domain, needs as foundation secured concepts of

[p. 195]

a universal and necessary connection pervading spiritual life, if it wishes to make the course of historical changes comprehensible. The historical method rests on the representation of the changes in a whole through relations of concepts; but precisely thereby it is at the same time distinguished from the methods of natural science, in that its foundation is the connection of the life of the soul, hence the essential element active in history, which comes to expression in the totality of the individual states. So Hegel’s problem becomes the connection of universal history, as presentable by means of the concepts contained in the necessary and universal connection of spirit. This connection is a whole whose unity works itself out through the differentiation of its parts. The relation of the whole to its parts is accordingly the basic category under which Hegel—and rightly—placed history. He is untiring in bringing out the subordinate character of the procedure which, starting from the particular, derives from it a universal separated from it—the law; the aim of history lies for him rather in the presentation of the whole factual fullness and depth of historical life through relations of concepts that present the essence of the whole of universal history by means of a representation of the connection of the states of the heart that constitutes history. The concepts through whose relations Hegel represents history are concepts of essence. Whether this problem be soluble or not—it is just this that Hegel grasped on the foundation of transcendental philosophy.

Each of those who before him philosophized about history sought the guiding thread of universal history in a single idea, for the sake of whose realization the whole of history was supposed to exist, and in it the standard was found by which the historical processes were measured according to their values. Beyond this Hegel sought to go, and therein lay the great combination of scientific circumstances under which Hegel thought: that the whole of history had been embraced in the eighteenth century, indeed had already been investigated by great historical thinkers by nations, by domains of historical life, by world epochs, and that now from the depths of transcendental philosophy—however imperfectly—a developmental connection of the spirit of

[p. 196]

history came to meet it: in this combination arose the advance that Hegel made.

The course of the history of the sciences, according to its general moments, constantly sets new tasks; their solution is accomplished in the depths of the individual person, and in order to understand such a solution one must follow how such a mind forms itself, how it gropes, tries, works in its material under ever new points of view. And where, as we intend to prove later of Hegel, the problem is indeed pushed forward but not really solved, there the attempts at a solution point beyond the unsatisfactory final result and so gain a significance reaching beyond mere preparation for a life’s work. The relation of concepts into which Hegel’s dialectical method finally contracted the developmental connection of spirit has not done justice to the fullness of ideas that he embraced in this epoch. How far he raised to methodical consciousness his procedure, its grounds and its connection with pragmatic history, with the century’s drive toward universal history and with transcendental philosophy, we do not know; it was indeed natural that he brought the form of his mind, the categories in which he grasped the world, to consciousness only gradually: totality, process, essence, development were now the categories in which he comprehended the world and history. For the further carrying out of his method there came to him from his nature a furtherance of its own. Gathered together in himself, re-living in himself, in the innermost depths of the heart, the great religious and philosophical changes of the time, advancing in secluded work to new positions of consciousness, he possessed in his lived experiences aids of a peculiar kind for the understanding of history. This becomes especially clear in his work of grasping the origin of the religion of Jesus, and we shall also be able to show it in the further course of his development. So for him the passionately experienced opposition of the method grasped in 1801 by Schelling and himself to the philosophy of reflection has become a new means for historical understanding—in short, his understanding of what has been grew constantly with his experiences. In the poet, lived experience works creatively for the understanding and presentation of the world, where it discloses a trait of it that had not been so perceived before. It is

[p. 197]

equally active, however, in the interpretation of the historical world, since every understanding is a re-experiencing and every re-experiencing has its first material in the lived experiences themselves.

So the attempt at a solution that Hegel gave in this period for the tasks we have just outlined was conditioned by his personality as by the philosophical movement; but at the same time the situation of our historiography also acted upon it. The historian investigates the connection of events. This leads him on until he has spanned world history. For all parts of it are linked with one another in doing and suffering. What is an indispensable moment of this connection and so conditions the present state, he lifts out as significant from the flux of events. In that he now singles out from the infinitely complicated present state what is important to him, be it political freedom, right, security of the order of peace, refined taste or enjoyment of art and of life, he is guided in this always by a determination of value, which brings one moment of life, mostly one-sidedly, to prevail. As now in the eighteenth century history in collection, criticism, presentation spanned the whole extent of history, this procedure was carried out completely and attained in the enlightened philosophy of history a principled foundation. Every earlier age was measured by its relation to values of the present abstractly and arbitrarily singled out. One won the idea of progress by seeking out in the past only what conditioned the values of the present and overlooking what constituted the intrinsic value of earlier times. This immense deception was uncovered by Rousseau, Hamann, Herder. Herder’s work asserted that the different times and nations realize values of their own. But how could one now compare these intrinsic values with one another? If one saw through the fallacy by which Herder arrived at his development of humanity in his history, there arose the task of grasping with him, from history itself, the intrinsic values as the generations have brought them forth and, going beyond him, of grasping by the same immanent method the relations of these values to one another in a development. The nations had to become representatives and bearers of a unitary development. The moving force in this development had to be grasped.

[p. 198]

So the situation of historiography too led Hegel to the task outlined. He brought with him an intuition about the nature of the religious process that had issued from constant intercourse with the sources under the influence of his religious-metaphysical lived experiences. It constitutes his methodological limit that it sufficed him to ground it in the connection of the life of the soul, instead of verifying and improving it by a comparative procedure extending over the whole history of religion. His historical greatness, however, showed itself in how he traced every feature of the sources back into the deepest religious inwardness. So there arose for him, from the given facts, his discovery of a development of religiosity whose immanent working force lies in religious experience itself: in that the latter lives through the religious values of an age, but at the same time also their limits, it is led beyond them; in that the values are preserved, in which precisely the essence of spirit consists, but the consciousness of their limit too enters into the new union, the fullness and articulation of spirit grow at each stage: at each of them the religious values are relative, and the last one lived through always points again into the future. So history is recognized as a development in which values work as causes. Its understanding rests on following the different forms in which the advance takes place. In them lies, as it were, the technique of historical development. Since the core of Hegel’s historical achievement in this period lies in their free presentation, we have set out his work in detail. Behind the connection that the eighteenth century had uncovered between the facts of knowledge, the progress of knowledge conditioned by them, its applicability to dominion over nature and the solidarity of humanity, Hegel went on to a still deeper and equally illuminating connection of religious inwardness.

On this foundation Hegel even then begins to develop an art of presenting development in concepts. The categories of the history of religion—separation, union, poverty, fullness, articulation—express the formal relations in the religious states and are in the end grounded in the relations of a whole to its parts. In them lie for Hegel the means of representing religious conditions in concepts; for they stand in relation to

[p. 199]

the religion-historical categories of law, dominion, obedience, disposition, love, religiosity. So there arises for him the possibility of showing the development of religion by an immanent procedure of historical knowledge, with the exclusion of external standards. And here his philosophy of history is prepared. In that the concepts indicated are set in logical relations, there arises insight into stages of the structure of spirit, and since the spiritual states themselves cannot be compared with one another according to their value, but their feeling-value stands in fixed relations to this structure, Hegel gains in it a standard of the development of value immanent in history itself. With this the circle of his methodological ideas closes. In this too this period prepares the Phenomenology and the philosophy of history, just as in regard to the great historical views themselves that we owe to Hegel.

#### III. The Ideal.

The worldview of mystical pantheism finds its conclusion in the conscious unity of the finite spirit with the infinite. This was also the articulation of the systems of Plotinus, of the medieval mystics, of Spinoza, and the closed unity thus arising gives them all, as it does also the related system of Schopenhauer, the character of works of art. But therein lies, from the beginning, a distinguishing basic trait of Hegel, that his ideal contains at once the grasp of the immanent divine connection of things and the realization of this idea in human community. Such a conclusion in an ideal is also demanded by his developmental history of religion. For there is for him, within the development itself, no form of religiosity in which it would already be fulfilled and completed; each is relative, and the religiosity of Jesus too is not exempt from this relativity; precisely because as finite it had a limit, the development had to lead beyond it, and because hitherto every stage lived through of this course has not really overcome the limit of the religiosity of Christ, the completed religiosity is an ideal of the future.

[p. 200]

1.

From this historical standpoint the ideal is determined by the cognition of the trend of development. From the record most important for Hegel’s history of religion it emerged that religious development is a process of increasing union of human beings among themselves and with the divine being. Spirit is to be united in itself, each human spirit with the other and all these with the divine. We saw further how in Jesus the consciousness of the unity of all life was attained, but how the shaping of existence demanded by this consciousness met inhibitions everywhere in the world of that time, how the urge toward realization of the ideal was held back (a process that Hegel, incidentally, could equally have observed in the course of the Reformation), and how in this way the Christian heaven had issued from dissatisfaction with the here and now. So from the dissolution of this opposition of here and beyond, of earthly reality and transcendence, there had to arise in the modern consciousness the ideal of the completion of religious union: organization of actual life through the religious consciousness. Already in the presentation of the teaching of Jesus the basic features of this ideal confronted us. Hegel distinguished it from Fichte’s. Fichte’s ideal of infinite striving arises “when sensibility and reason or freedom and nature or subject and object are so absolutely opposed that they are absolutes. Through the syntheses: no object no subject, or no I no not-I, their property as absolutes is not sublated.” “Law is a thought relation of objects to one another. A thought relation is fixed and abiding, without spirit, a yoke, a chaining together, a dominion and servitude—doing and suffering—determining and being determined.” To this stands opposed the ideal of the Kingdom of God. “Love, the blossom of life, Kingdom of God, the whole space with all necessary modifications, stages of development; the modifications are exclusions, not oppositions, i.e., there are no laws, i.e., what is thought is equal to what is actual, there is no universal. No relation has become objective, a rule. All relations are alive, have issued from the development of life.”

Closer to Hegel than Christ’s ideal of the Kingdom of God would stand the renewal of the intimate association that had once

[p. 201]

existed in the Greek city-states, under the conditions of life of the great modern states. His ideal is a pure league of human beings—the freest that is possible—a league of beauty full of divine life, in which love goes beyond “the form of love,” a league that becomes a living relation of individualities and “presents itself in shapes of life.” What he understands by such shaping of life becomes clear where he once sets Hercules, “bravery shaped,” in praise against the Christ ideal. He rejects the passivity of the Christians toward the world; they fixed the relations of developing life into objective realities and then, in the feeling of their love, disdained its wealth and its articulation; they did not find “the mean of the extremes, beauty”; “to the Greeks body and soul remain in one living shape.” And when he speaks of the stale idea of a universal love of humanity and of the free people in which each single one is the whole, in contrast to the modern states, he too thinks above all of Greece.

Most remarkably this longing for the Greek beauty of life is expressed in the discussion of the Lord’s Supper. The spirit of Christ, in which his disciples are one, has become in the Lord’s Supper something actual for outer feeling; but this objectification remains incomplete; in eating, matter, the soulless, vanishes: there remains only a living sensation: this is in keeping with Christian spiritualism; for Hegel there appears in it the whole incongruence of the divine with the things in which it is here to gain shape. Thing and sensation, spirit and outer factuality do not really unite; imagination can never combine them in something beautiful. There are always two things present, devotion and tasting. Here imagination has given no image in which intuition and feeling would unite, whereas in an Apollo, a Venus one must forget the fragile stone. One sees in their shape only the immortals, and in beholding them one is at the same time pervaded by the feeling of eternal youthful vigor and of love. In a truly religious action the whole soul is satisfied; the Lord’s Supper, however, leaves behind only a devout astonishment, “something divine was promised and it has dissolved in the mouth.” Hegel’s reflection on the task of raising to beauty the shape and cultic actions of the religious community expresses itself also in another record. “To found a beautiful religion; the ideal of it?

[p. 202]

Does one find it?” It is as if this had been written after a conversation with Hölderlin.

A further light falls on Hegel’s ideal of the future from a passage in his commentary on Kant’s Rechtslehre, written in 1798. He turns against Kant’s conception of the relation of state and church. As the two are separated in their task, so according to Kant the state too is indeed to have the religious societies located in its territory under its police supervision, but not to meddle in their internal affairs. Hegel finds that such a separation is impossible. If one restricts the task of the state, sets it, say, upon the principle of property, then the spirit of such a state is in constant contradiction with that of the church, which strives to keep man in the feeling of his wholeness. “Either the citizen is not in earnest about his relation to the state or not about that to the church, if he can remain tranquil in both.” And against Kant he asserts an ideal that had undoubtedly dawned on him in the Greeks, but that at the same time prepares his concepts of the objectification of spirit in the forms of the state. He defends the unity of church and state in the sense that religion is, as it were, to form the soul of the life of the state. “If the principle of the state is a complete whole, church and state cannot possibly be different. What for the latter is the thought, the ruling, that is for the former precisely the same whole, as a living one, presented by imagination.” The individual passages on the relation of church and state do show differences, yet Hegel’s opinion at that time is evidently that unity of religious faith does not indeed belong among the marks without which the state cannot be thought, but that for the perfection of the state a spiritual bond is requisite that establishes inner belonging.

If one takes these and related passages of Hegel together, they contain his conception of a future society that would be able to bring to expression and effect the deepest, and therefore a religious-metaphysical, content in organic shaping and beauty. He does not look backward, as his critics take it, into the Greek world, but, guided by its intuition, into a more beautiful future of our society. And the ideas of the present are in many ways similar to his. At this point too he is akin to Hölderlin, who has suffered the same misunderstanding.

[p. 203]

2.

To such views of Hegel’s is joined his new conception of the form in which practical philosophy possesses its knowledge. This knowledge of the ethical is not originally there as law, norm, commandment. We saw how Hegel derives the form of the commandment in the sayings of Jesus from the fact that the living consciousness of love, from which religious action flows, enters into the reflected form of a proposition directed to others. Love cannot be commanded, and its essence stands in contradiction to the form of a law that demands respect. “The honor that Kant bestows upon that saying of Jesus (‘Love God above all and your neighbor as yourself’) in regarding it as the ideal of holiness attainable by no creature is superfluously lavished; for such an ideal, in which duties would be represented as gladly done, is self-contradictory, because duties require an opposition and gladly doing requires no opposition.” The knowledge of the ethical attains adequate expression just as little in a doctrine of virtue. For the doctrine of virtue posits each virtue as a fixed magnitude. If one thinks a man determined by One virtue, then, since every virtue has a limit, beyond this limit he is no longer ethically determined at all; but if his moral action is regarded as grounded in two or more virtues, then each takes away room and strength from the others, and collisions of the virtues arise. “One can indeed say that the virtuous disposition by itself, considered in general, i.e., abstracted from the virtues posited here, does not come into collision, because the virtuous disposition is only one; but with that the presupposition is canceled. And with both virtues posited, the exercise of the one cancels the possibility of exercising the other, which is equally absolute, and the well-founded demand of the other is rejected.” “If the man of many virtues, among the multitude of his creditors whom he cannot all satisfy, wants to make an order of rank, he declares himself less guilty toward those whom he puts behind than toward others whom he calls higher.” So ethical life is expressed adequately neither in the form of a law nor in that of virtue, but must be grasped as the unity of life in the ethical community. Ethical life is for Hegel “something living,” “a being, a modification of life.”

[p. 204]

The turn that Hegel here gives to practical philosophy is related to that which Schleiermacher gave it at the same time in his doctrine of goods. Likewise Hegel’s treatise on natural law and Schleiermacher’s critique of the doctrine of morals then correspond to one another.

3.

Let us now try to indicate the line that leads in Hegel’s development, through our period, to his later System of Ethical Life. Everywhere the fragments point beyond the religious ideal into the universally human. If religiosity was then for Hegel the highest form of spiritual life, then, as soon as he recognized in philosophy a superior form of it, his Kingdom of God had to become secularized, as it were, into the realm of ethical life. The generation to which Hegel belonged stood at once under the influence of the idealism of Kant and Fichte and of the French Revolution. It was filled with the idea of an elevation of humanity and of coming higher orders of society. Fichte was the hero who proclaimed this new time, and his philosophy was devoted to bringing it about. The pupils of Fichte in Jena, in Berlin and in Tübingen were united in these ideas. Hegel, Schelling, Hölderlin held fast to the ideals of their Tübingen years and strengthened one another in them. And as the movement that the French Revolution had called forth bore a European character, as the writers of the ideological school in France, the defenders of the Revolution in England and those in Germany worked together in this movement, the new ideals maintained themselves, by virtue of their energy and extent, even against the reaction that spread from the execution of the king and the revolutionary wars on. Hegel too remained upright and brave. If one examines his theological fragments, they are wholly borne by the spirit of this movement. His absorption in history does not stand in contradiction with his work for a more perfect religiosity and a renewal of society; rather, precisely in that he, far more radically than the average German Enlightenment, set Christianity into the flux of history, in which this form of religiosity too must pass over into a higher one, in that he thus took development fully

[p. 205]

seriously, his work for the future received, and his faith in it, strengthened energy and a determinate direction. “Now that Protestantism has taken off the alien consecration, spirit can dare to hallow itself as spirit in its own shape and to produce the original reconciliation with itself in a new religion, into which the infinite pain and the whole gravity of its opposition are taken up but dissolve untroubled and pure, namely when there shall be a free people and reason shall have been reborn in its reality as an ethical spirit, which can have the boldness to take its pure shape on its own ground and from its own majesty.” These are sentences that Hegel wrote some time later, but which might similarly have stood in our fragments too.

The new ethical world that dawns in him has as its basic trait the embodiment of the highest ideas in the state. In connection with his most important political pamphlet he says that man strives to raise himself to the consciousness of the idea, but that he must at the same time feel himself as a living being in the state. In the theoretical grasp of the ideal connection of things spirit is solitary, it is unable to remain in its inner world, and so in the historical process the formation of the highest ideas is always at the same time bound up with their realization in society. Ethical life has its existence only where life, spirit and love have organized themselves into a whole. Since the essence of the single life is one with that of the whole, so that the relations to the latter correspond to the needs of one’s own existence, the development of the person from Christianity on is canceled and yet preserved when the political ethical life of the ancients comes again to recognition under the conditions of modern times.

A second trait of the new ideal allows a still deeper view into the originality of Hegel’s lived experiences. The concepts in which the new system of ethical life constitutes itself belong to a region that lies beyond morality. Guilt is every injury to life, fate every reaction of the whole against such damage. So guilt, suffering, fate are relations that occur in life in the struggle of forces, and fate strikes even the morally innocent injury of an existence. Hence the pain that stems from the destruction of life, which after all is one with us in the all-life, and the reconciliation through love which

[p. 206]

restores this unity, have nothing to do with remorse, fear of punishment and the plea for mercy. Every moral order that runs in the relations of law, violation of the law, punishment and reconciliation with it through love holds man fast in the inescapable circle of abstract relations, beyond which the wholeness of his essence remains.

The last trait of the ethical world dawning in Hegel is the consciousness that the way of spirit to the realization of such an ideal, like that of all development, passes through pain and labor, and that without them there is no state that could ever be attained. Separation, pain, labor, limit are moments of every human state, since they belong to the process of life itself. Herein is expressed the terrible earnestness with which Hegel confronts the fantasies of happiness of the Enlightenment. His new ideal rejects the caprice and covetousness of private life just as it does the moral concepts stemming from the transcendent view of the world. So both had to become subordinate moments in Hegel’s System of Ethical Life. Extraordinary difficulties lay in the task of shaping the new view of ethical life systematically—the greatest of them was the contradiction between the historical consciousness of the relativity of every historical reality and the metaphysical need for a last, conclusive word and for an absolute value.

[p. 207]

#### Notes.

The manuscripts of Hegel on which this work is based are in the Royal Library at Berlin. They were already used by Hegel’s two biographers, Rosenkranz (Hegels Leben, Berlin 1844) and Haym (Hegel und seine Zeit, Berlin 1857). Both of them moreover had before them a whole series of Hegel’s works that are no longer contained in the literary remains as we have them today in the Royal Library. The incomplete and often incorrect communications of Rosenkranz and the fragments taken out of context as Haym prints them have been used only where the corresponding manuscripts are missing, and accordingly only there are they referred to below. The manuscripts themselves have been bound into volumes and paginated by the Berlin Library, and they are cited below accordingly; for easier location we designate the two sides of each leaf by a and b.

XI 89a–91b, VIII 21a–92a (date on p. 82a: 2 November, on p. 90a 29 April 96).

Folk Religion pp. 30–39

VIII 93a–100a, 100b–115a (Difference between Greek religion of imagination and Christian positive religion, see Haym p. 474 ff.). Further 121a–163b.

1. History of Jewish Religiosity pp. 76–82

XI 22a–24a, 54a–58b, 61a–68b, VII 104a–107b.

2. The Basic Fragment pp. 82–91

VII 76a–87b, XI 92a–97b.

3. Discourses and the Sermon on the Mount pp. 91–95

VII 89a–94a, XI 34a–37b, VII 166a–169b.

4. Reconciliation with Fate through Love pp. 95–104

VII 148a–157b, XI 26a–30b. (To justify the placing of this fragment after the Sermon on the Mount, compare—apart from the opening words—p. 148b in the margin: “the opposition of duty and inclination, etc.,” and above all p. 149a in the margin: “in the preceding sublation of the law through virtues only the form of the law vanished, its content remained; but here, with the form, its content too would be sublated.”)

[p. 208]

5. The Virtues and Love pp. 104–106

XI 31a–33b, VII 95a–99a (Lord’s Supper).

6. The Ideal of Love pp. 106–110

VII 100a–103b.

8. The Fate of Jesus and the Religion of His Community pp. 118–129
