VIII 9a–20b (the date on p. 9a: 24 September 1800) the revision extends to 25b.

Rosenkranz has described (Prutz, Literarisches Taschenbuch II 159 ff.) a theosophical fragment dealing with the divine triangle; he places it in the Frankfurt period. It is not now to be found in the Berlin literary remains, and I was also unable to use Rosenkranz’s excerpts, because it is especially problematic to me, with regard to the terminology, whether it belongs to the period treated here.

Two Political Writings pp. 134–151

The Introduction on the Constitution of Germany XIII, 1a–3a.

The Essay on the Constitution of Württemberg pp. 137–139 XIII 64a–66b.

The quotation reported on p. 139, line 10, see Haym p. 66.

The Essay on the Constitution of Germany pp. 139–151.

There are several drafts or remains of such, and all sorts of notes and collections. The two drafts I, 1–47 and XIII, 11–51 are the most important. I call them Ms. I and Ms. II. Neither the one nor the other gives us the whole essay; but taken together they complement each other quite well. Hence an edition could be prepared from them which, however uncritically it proceeds, essentially fulfills its immediate purpose, to reproduce the course and content of the essay. “Kritik der Verfassung Deutschlands von G. F. W. Hegel. Aus dem handschriftlichen Nachlaß des Verfassers herausgegeben von Georg Mollat, Kassel 1893.”

Ms. I (I, 1–43) is the earlier and demonstrably already falls in the time of Jena. Proof: Ms. vol. I, leaf 37 (printed: Mollat 107 f.). It is stylistically fully worked through, even if an outer division into chapters is still lacking. Leaves 1–4 give us the beginning of the essay (printed Mollat pp. 1–9, as “Introduction”). Then several leaves are missing which, besides the beginning of the presentation of the constitution of Germany, namely the imperial military constitution, perhaps contained the whole exposition on the concept of the state (cf. Mollat pp. 10–14). Leaves 5–10 deal with the imperial military and imperial financial constitution. They correspond to Mollat pp. 25–38, and indeed the edition at first (pp. 25–27) takes precisely our Ms. I (leaves 5–6) as its basis. After leaf 10 again several leaves are missing; they must have contained the greatest part of the critique of imperial legislation. From leaf 11 on Ms. I presents a gapless whole, which leads us to the end of the essay. Through carelessness in binding, the leaves have unfortunately come into a wrong order; the correct one is: leaves 11–18, 21–24, 19–20, 25–46. This part of our Ms. (leaves 11–46) corresponds at first (leaves 11–12) to Mollat pp. 55–56 and then (leaves 12–46) forms the immediate source for Mollat pp. 56–131.

[p. 209]

Ms. II (XIII, 11–51) presents a second elaborated draft, which clearly arose with Ms. I at hand and agrees with it essentially, often verbatim. Leaves 11–15 bring the new form of the “Introduction.” Most of this is printed as “Appendix” in Mollat pp. 132–136, with an arbitrary transposition: the section Mollat p. 134 “Germany is no longer a state”—136 “would have led” precedes in the Ms. the section Mollat 132–134. This introduction is followed first by a chapter that Hegel himself headed “1. Concept of the State,” and then, without outer division into chapters, approximately the first third of the actual treatise. This whole part of Ms. II (leaves 15–48) is printed in Mollat pp. 10–56; only on pp. 22–27 does the edition, as already mentioned, make use of Ms. I. The last two leaves of our Ms. II bear the heading: “Chap. Legitimacy, that the exercise of the laws of the state does not come about.” They are printed essentially in Mollat pp. 136–137, as “Appendix.”

As Ms. III I designate I, 47–57. For these leaves seem, to judge by their outer character, to have arisen at the same time. For the rest, however, they form no coherent whole, but fall clearly into the groups leaves 47–48, 49, 50, 51–53, 54–55, 56–57. These parts also differ in the degree of their inner completion. Thus leaves 47–48 are already an elaborated draft, though preserved for us only in part, whereas the remaining parts still represent, more or less, the stage of brief, fragmentary notes. Hence one will indeed have to place this Ms. III before Ms. I and perhaps still in the Frankfurt period. It does not contradict this that leaves 47–48 in part clearly formed the immediate source for XIII 15–20, hence for Ms. II; one must then only assume that these expositions (on the concept of the state) were passed over for the time being in Ms. I precisely because they already lay at hand in a proper draft. In content the whole Ms. III brings nothing that is not also contained in Ms. I or II.

This holds also of the notes XIII 61 (Ms. IV), XIII 13 (Ms. V) and XIII 55–56 (Ms. VI) and of the draft remains XIII 5 (Ms. VII) and 7–11 (Ms. VIII). These Mss. moreover probably all likewise belong to a stage of the essay that lies before Ms. I. Of interest are Ms. IV (perhaps altogether the first attempt to become clear about the treatment of the subject) and Ms. VIII (the remainder of a third version of the “Introduction”).

What XIII otherwise contains of manuscripts demonstrably belonging to our essay consists of mere materials (copies and excerpts). They reach into the time of Jena. That Hegel took up his Machiavelli once more for this essay is shown by an excerpt, preserved for us on the back of leaf 56, from a French translation of the Principe.

The New Worldview and the Beginnings of the System pp. 151–206.
